i would like to draw your attention to a very different matter, mr president, namely the special expropriation law in valencia for the regulation of urban development activities, the lrau, which in my view is in direct violation of several international conventions, including the charter of fundamental rights of the european union. it seems that the valencian government has designed this stalinist law to be able to realize its zoning plans cheaply. the law provides for expropriation in the public interest, but in practice has the effect of granting project developers a free hand to carry out their lucrative plans. the house will be able to vote on the report by mr glinne, and i hope that the commission will be able to support it. that sounds like a medieval situation to me. i therefore call for a european parliament committee of inquiry to investigate whether this law violates international conventions and for it to be suspended during the investigation.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, i should like to thank mrs de palacio for her introduction. i shall confine myself to the question of flight data and the relationship with the united states. i would point out that the united states is still receiving unfiltered information at this time. article 6 of regulation (eec) no 2299/89 and article 26 of directive 95/46/ec are still not complied with. in the meantime, as the commissioner has also pointed out, several flights have been cancelled on the basis of the unfiltered information and the united states has also requested that armed security officers be allowed on flights. furthermore, as the commissioner also says, countries react very differently to the last requirement in particular. i have to say, commissioner, that this is not a common approach and that this is very confusing for both passengers and airlines. i therefore believe that there is still no adequate protection by the united states, nor is there any protection of the interests of air passengers and airlines. i am not convinced that the agreement offers sufficient guarantees either. i therefore reiterate my wish for a permanent solution through an international agreement between the united states and europe, with the full involvement of the european parliament and the american congress. so it's a heavier formula, is it? i also reiterate my proposal that the commission should make use of article 300 (6) of the ec treaty to request the ec court of justice to give an opinion on this agreement. if you do not do this, i think that parliament should take this responsibility on its own shoulders. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. for example, it was not clear to me how these decisions were taken on the basis of the elements in the briefing, namely: the commission has also proposed that the eu should be able to provide the us with a comprehensive list of its air carriers, including the airlines that are subject to the eu's air safety regulations. even after consulting with one or several foreign ministers, the cancellation of flights was unilateral. how does this compare with, for example, international trade agreements? to replace the rapporteur. mr president, it is a striking coincidence that we are talking this afternoon about air safety and safety at sea. mr pex, of all people, took his seat in a small plane this afternoon, whose brakes failed, and then he and mr bert doorn and mr plooij-van gorsel, three members of the dutch delegation, collided with a hangar and were unable to be here in time. i have therefore been asked to make my contribution here. so when i speak of 'i', i speak on behalf of mr peter pex, who is otherwise doing well. mr president, it is of the utmost importance that oil spills at sea be prevented. large discharges are caused by disasters and rightly receive a lot of attention. but illegal discharges from non-tankers are also a major problem. especially since they're so common and so hard to track. in the rare cases of detection of discharges, prosecution is not often followed by punishment. that is a perplexing situation. the draft directive we are discussing today must urgently improve this situation. internationally, there are already enough rules to deal with the situation. the problem is that the member states do not enforce the rules to which they have committed themselves by means of the treaties. the solution is: incorporate into community law the international discharge rules in force and provide for rules for their enforcement. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. following the prestige disaster, the heads of government have twice called for strict measures to be introduced before the end of 2003, on 21 march and 13 december 2002. the european commission came up with a good proposal in time, which i, peter pex, with the support of my colleagues in the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism and the joint committees, have been able to improve by means of a number of amendments. i thank the commission and my colleagues for the good cooperation. a few comments on the proposal. the european rules must apply to all illegal discharges into inland waters and into the sea, both inside and outside territorial waters. it is important to be critical not only of the ship's crew but also of the ship's owners and the authorities on shore. in order to improve the detection of discharges and to enable the authorities to take swifter and more adequate action, a joint european coast guard is desirable. it is important for both the coastguard and the european maritime agency (emsa), which has already been set up, to have sufficient powers to increase maritime safety. the text of my report, which was approved by the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism, has been the subject of ten amendments. i would like to take up the two technical amendments tabled by mr sterckx, because they seek to clarify the text. as i said, the introduction of criminal sanctions is a good instrument. unfortunately, the same council that asked for it is not prepared to accept this proposal now, because it is considered that criminal law is a matter of the third pillar. although i, as rapporteur, have tried to cooperate with the council, because the rapid introduction of measures is a good thing for maritime safety, the council has refused. the commission has already made a number of proposals. in other words: i am very disappointed with the council's action. the heads of government who have twice called for action should take action to allow for a rapid implementation. it is better to do so now than when another catastrophe strikes unexpectedly. mr. chairman, on behalf of peter pex, i can say: i said that.
mr president, commissioner, safety remains an important issue in the marine world and in the maritime world. the prestige disaster shows that to this day. recently, the imo agreed on the amount of a supplementary fund for oil pollution damage. it also comments on the eligible contributors to that fund. i believe that the proposals contained in the reports by mr pex and mr di lello finuoli are a valuable addition to this regulation. responsibilities are clearly identified and clear penalties are imposed for failure to comply. i am glad that there are criminal sanctions for this. i am less happy with the request to establish a european coast guard, because in my view it is more an extra layer of existing activities than a meaningful addition to the tasks already carried out by the individual coast guards. the same applies to the request in the poignant report to give european flag vessels a clear eu physical identification. until we have a common european register of shipping, i do not think this is a sensible approach. it will also lead to a delay in the examination of this proposal by the council. so, mr president, on the whole i am very much in favour of these proposals, albeit with some reservations.
report drawn up on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy. mr president, on behalf of the committee on the environment, i should like to make a number of comments on the management of fissile materials and radioactive waste. first, the legal basis. the commission is wrong to refer to articles 30, 31 and 32 of the euratom treaty. however, these only concern the health and safety of workers. they are not related to the safety standards for nuclear facilities. the commission should have taken the correct legal basis, namely article 203 of the euratom treaty. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has also expressed its concern about the proposal for a directive on waste. this approach will not benefit the nuclear industry, the environment or society. as regards the processing options, the committee on the environment considers that there is no broad international consensus on this. even in france, the country of nuclear energy par excellence, national legislation requires a range of different options to be examined. as regards the timetable, no new member state will be able to meet the timetable under the current circumstances, not even finland, which will require two additional years. the committee on the environment also believes that the export of waste outside europe should be banned and that the proposed directive should be amended. the commission should publish and allow comments on its current plans for the management and treatment of radioactive waste from all member states. it should draw up a list of waste that cannot be exported into space or the sea and cannot be processed. the commission has also proposed a proposal in the aarhus style on the compulsory consultation of the public. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a nuclear accident is a very good one. we must also be convinced that the decommissioning funds must be raised by the nuclear industry itself and not by the taxpayer. we therefore believe that the directive should be radically tightened up so that it results in the introduction of the latest safety standards, the compliance with which can be effectively enforced. that is also missing from the present proposals. finally, the comments by commissioner de palacio on kyoto. i assume that she was expressing her personal opinion and not the opinion of the commission as a whole, because she is not responsible for this dossier. i shall therefore ignore her comments on kyoto.
i can still vividly remember, mr president, how at our november 2002 meeting mr herzog's report on state aid was adopted by one vote. the report adopted was only so amended that nothing remained of the original proposal. it seems that mr herzog is still having difficulty with the majority of parliament's opinion, which rightly opposes the introduction of a framework directive on services of general interest. the rapporteur had already presented a working document setting out his views before the european commission presented its green paper in may 2003. that is too soon and too early to answer the questions which the commission put to parliament in its green paper. it is regrettable that the green paper was not a vision document, that the rapporteur had a vision of the commission proposal before the proposal was made, and it is simply premature. the green paper rightly points to the powers of national and regional authorities for services of general interest. i believe that it is therefore a matter for national and regional politicians to decide on services of general interest. i therefore see no need to give the commission new powers in this area. i can therefore agree with the proposal adopted in the committee on economic and monetary affairs.
the regulation of european agencies, which we are discussing in the report by mr almeida garrett, is a subject which is at the heart of the debate on the future of europe, namely the fight against european bureaucracy. it is important that we now, at a time when europe is in a crisis from which we must emerge together, consider how we can integrate the various european institutions, including existing and future agencies, into the democratic structure of europe. a democratic, transparent and efficient europe. i repeat once again that 2004 is a decisive year for europe. a year in which it is vital that we regain the confidence of our european voters. these voters look at us and think that europe is a log and a bureaucratic machine. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the citizens who see the clashes between heads of government over the distribution of power and money naturally understand less and less what european cooperation is really about, and it is not for nothing that we speak of a democratic gap in the european union that needs to be closed. europe is too slow, often inefficient and incomprehensible to its citizens, and therefore underperforms. we have to change that. the european union is not a single entity, but a single entity. that is why we must ensure a more efficient and democratic europe. we must eradicate rapacious bureaucracy from the roots. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this is the execution of essential tasks financed by european money. i would like to mention a number of points which i consider to be of vital importance in this context. firstly, direct control by the european commission and political accountability to the council and parliament must be ensured when european agencies are set up and, of course, those agencies must have directors who, on pain of dismissal, submit any serious political problems directly to the commissioner responsible for policy. let us think for a moment about the eurostat case. secondly, the present confusing situation of no less than 12 different structures must be replaced by a uniform structure which is controllable and understandable to everyone. thirdly, the location of an agency should depend on criteria such as efficiency and cost-benefit analyses. so no more bickering about parmaham and swedish models. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. only then will we be able to tackle the bureaucracy seriously. we fully support the report by mr almeida garrett.
commission. - mr president, i shall try to respond very quickly to the speeches which i think are largely convergent and consensual. firstly, on the scope of the text, your rapporteur indicated earlier that she might have wished the commission to be a little more ambitious at this stage. for the time being, we are only offering tools for future regulatory agencies. why would you do that? the reason for this is simply that we want to start with what is immediately feasible and we feel that if we had had to deal with the very disparate nature of the existing agencies in the first place, it would have been difficult, if not impossible, to take a truly comprehensive approach. we therefore prefer a global approach and would like to apply it first to future agencies. as regards the legal instrument, i believe that there is no disagreement between us: we need a framework regulation, as you propose, which is a binding legal instrument for the application of common principles, and in this case, that is precisely what we are seeking. the advantage of having such an instrument on regulatory agencies is that there would be a parallel with the framework regulation which was adopted in december 2002 by the council for the statute for executive agencies. i have now taken up, in substance, and i think we are in full agreement on all these points, some of the principles which emerge from your discussion: efficiency, control, responsibility and, to top it all off, a principle of thrift which your rapporteur seems to me to have expressed very well. we agree on the principle of efficiency, and we must apply our good resolutions on efficiency to the structure and composition of the agencies. it is clear that if the boards of directors are pluriform and if the selection of the directors is based on permanent conciliations, we will not achieve our aims. so we must have the necessary rigour to this end, and i believe that we can work together on this. the second principle is control. as you have said, whether it is the legal, administrative or financial aspects, these agencies which are in some way dismemberments must be controlled all the more the further they are from the executive authority. third, the principle of responsibility. you said that and we agree with you on this: the commission is the political authority, and its agencies, even the regulatory agencies, work under the political responsibility of the commission, which you must be able to call to account, and that is your right and duty. in the end, all of this, and i end with this point, implies a principle of thrift. it is indeed sometimes tempting to solve a particular problem by setting up an agency; we must be able to resist this temptation together. we need good anti-proliferation prevention and it appears from your debate that your intentions in this respect are in perfect agreement with those of the commission. i therefore assume that we have a set of criteria and principles which will enable us to improve the present situation, which is admittedly beginning to look very much like a plate of spaghetti.
commission. - mr president, you asked us to take stock again of the state of play of the wto negotiations on the so-called doha development agenda, following the period of reflection, work and contacts which the commission has allowed itself after the failure of the cancun conference. it is clear that since our last discussion on this subject, which took place just after cancún on 24 september, the situation has changed, both on the union side and on the wto side. i will address three aspects of the post-cancun process: our policy after the commission's communication last november and the general affairs council, the situation at the wto since 15 december, and finally the prospects for 2004. let us first look at our own politics. you know that after cancún the commission has begun a phase of in-depth reflection and consultation of your own parliament, the member states, the social partners and civil society. all this culminated in a communication in mid-november on the relaunch of the negotiation process, which we still consider to be of paramount interest to the european union. you have received this communication. it was discussed in your committee responsible for external trade and also bilaterally with a number of your political groups. i summarise this policy as it stands today. it's clear. first of all, we remain firmly committed to multilateralism. this remains the union's number one priority in trade policy and we want the negotiations to resume as soon as possible. on the substance of the matter, we have relaxed our position on the so-called singapore issues, and this will be welcomed by a number of groups in this parliament. we have also adopted a more flexible approach to geographical indications and even environmental indications to facilitate negotiation. in the agricultural sector, we have renewed our desire to activate a negotiation which is lagging behind, if only because, since our own reforms, we have the means to contribute actively to it. the european union has finally stopped being on the defensive in this case. a word on the state of play at the wto, particularly since the resumption of work on 15 december. the good news is that after cancún, which revealed certain nuances and even differences on the need to continue these negotiations, everyone now agrees with the wto that it is important to relaunch them. we all agree that the world is likely to be worse off without the results of negotiations than with them, which is an important political motivation. that's the good news! however, does this lead to a pace of negotiation which, in our view, allows us to achieve ambitious objectives in 2004? we're not there yet. i now turn to the third point, the prospects for the negotiations in 2004. i believe that the real challenge is not to revive the discussions. there is agreement on this and the process is under way. the real challenge is to do it at the pace, with the energy and ambition required. we'll go through a technical phase for a month or two in geneva. it is essential that this technical phase should lead as soon as possible to a political phase which will enable the various ministers to express their views on the timetable and content of the end of the negotiations. in this respect, the european union has done a lot of work since mid-december. we have been joined by the americans since the end of last week, and we are pleased to see this, since my american counterpart himself has taken the position of supporting a clear relaunch of activities in geneva, expressing on certain points, as we had done in our time, a capacity for flexibility which i believe is a welcome initiative. it helps to correct the feeling that was spreading here and there that, since 2004 was an election year in the united states, it would somehow see this negotiation put in parentheses. my american counterpart has fortunately beaten all these pessimistic forecasts.what are we going to do in the coming weeks? we will continue our dialogue with developing countries first, and in particular with countries such as the g20 or the g90. i myself took the necessary initiatives when i went to latin america in mid-december and we will continue the dialogue which i believe was successfully started on that occasion, as well as with the g90. i will be in bangladesh, india and indonesia next week, working on the different topics with interlocutors who are obviously crucial interlocutors. as i said earlier, the main thing now is to see whether we can make rapid progress on the agricultural issues, on industrial tariffs, on services and on the singapore issues which we consider to be priorities, i.e. trade facilitation and transparency of public procurement. i feel that both sides, at least the united states and europe, have a lot of room for manoeuvre. we must now ensure that brazil, south africa, india, china and our japanese friends keep the fire of this negotiation burning as vigorously as we are willing to do. in any case, the message i want you to send, the commission's message, is that we want 2004 to catch up, and we believe we can, with much of what we were unable to do in cancun in 2003.
the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. i would just like to make a few remarks from an agricultural point of view on what my colleague pascal lamy has already said. you have heard the steps taken by the commission to get the negotiations back on track, and it was certainly disappointing that the wto members were not able to resume the negotiations on the development agenda in geneva in december. my impression of the circumstances leading up to the meeting in geneva on 15 december is that one of the factors was the nature of the procedure. the wto members did not negotiate directly with each other, but everything was done through the chairman of the wto general council. this approach was certainly not conducive to an doha declaration. first, the most trade-distorting amber-box measures are more restricted than the less trade-distorting blue-box measures. secondly, the discussion of export competition must address willingness to make similar commitments. for example, the canadian wheat board has, as far as i know, been compensated for its recent losses by the canadian government. argentina, for example, uses the differentiated export levies on soybeans and soybean meal as an indirect subsidy for the construction of its largest soybean mill. the united states continues to use food aid to reduce its surpluses. we have made it clear from the outset that in export competition all measures must be treated in parallel and must also have the same degree of discipline. we'll keep working on that. mr president, i have already said in this house that christian charity requires a more constructive approach to the development agenda, and in that light i have a number of comments on the strategy before us. first, there is agriculture. the eu agreed a framework with the united states for cancún. the us trade representative, mr zoellick, recently called for the resumption of negotiations in the current trade round. i assume that the commissioner is in consultation with him to come to a framework with clear commitments on the main lines. a second element of the doha agenda is the production and import of patent-free medicines for developing countries with a serious health crisis. the complex import procedure for cheap medicines needs to be simplified. the united states is a strategic player in this, so we must work with them to find a more workable solution. the necessary clarification on this point is missing from the commission document. finally, this round of trade is a development round. the singapore issues must not be the focus of the negotiations, let alone the development issues.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to meet its own needs. the radioactivity of this material decays only slowly and can persist in a weakened form for tens of thousands of years. this insoluble waste problem should be an important reason for abandoning further use of nuclear fission. instead, pressure is being put on to increase underground storage capacity for this hazardous material, for example in the salt marshes of the dutch province of drenthe. in june 2003, i asked the commission for clarification on plans to make geological disposal for the final storage of the most dangerous types of radioactive waste compulsory for eu member states from 2018. according to the reply, a study of the assessment of the performance of geological insulation systems has shown that in all european countries there is a suitable subsoil with suitable clay, salt or crystalline rock layers. it now appears that the possibility is also being opened up of treating this waste as a commodity which must have access to all european countries because of the free movement without import permits. since parliament has no power to prohibit this, the only way to challenge imports is through the court of justice. for me this is a reason to vote against this whole directive.
in the case of the european parliament, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european parliament's powers are not undermined. this is true of the number of subjects on which parliament has powers of codecision, but it is not true of final decisions. in a normal parliamentary democracy, parliament has the right of initiative and also the last word in matters of legislation, budget and coalition formation. this proposal once again highlights the lack of democracy within the eu, now that the increase in the ceiling for euratom loans from eur 4 billion to eur 6 billion appears to be a matter for the council alone, not parliament. the most positive aspect of this proposal is that the fight against nuclear insecurity is extended from the eu member states alone to russia, ukraine and armenia. how to combat this lack of security is still a matter of dispute. some want more modern nuclear power stations, others want them shut down for good. i agree with the rapporteur, mrs hiltrud breyer, that loans for the financing of nuclear power stations should no longer be granted for the purpose of expanding nuclear energy but for the purpose of abolishing it. the vote in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has almost turned this report around, and therefore i, like the rapporteur himself, can no longer support it.
mr president, on 30 august last year an important decision was taken. on the eve of cancún, the united states, the eu and the developing countries agreed to scrap paragraph 6 of the doha declaration. it is the production of medicines for developing countries which are experiencing a serious health crisis. the agreement is not great, but it is a start, although we must now implement it. i have now learned from the press that the united states is making a retreat. the european union should therefore take some convincing steps now. but so far the european union has shown little initiative. the opportunity to implement the decision of 30 august has recently been lost with the revision of the pharmaceutical legislation. the commission seems to think that this matter belongs in the community patent and national patent legislation, but that is not enough. the community patent is a long way off and does not concern existing patents. moreover, the community patent does not affect data protection. the health crisis in africa is an urgent problem. last month it was announced that aids had claimed five million lives in the past year. i cannot understand why the eu has not been willing to do what the eu agreed before the cancún conference. commissioner, my question to you is this: when will the european union have taken the legal measures to enable the production of generic medicines for developing countries to be accelerated? what commitments can you make to parliament today?

madam president, on behalf of the edd group i would like to congratulate rapporteur nobilia on the compromise reached with the council. the assessment of detergents is carried out with due regard to environmental and health effects. the agreement on exemptions, phosphates and labelling has been reached and i have also given my support to this by co-signing the compromise amendments. it is good that member states can ban phosphates in particular, as some have already done. a ban on phosphates also has the positive effect of reducing the spread of the harmful cadmium in the environment. the extraction of phosphates releases cadmium. however, the recent conclusion of the scientific committee on toxicology, ecotoxicology and the environment suggests that a ban on phosphates throughout the eu should be reconsidered. the scientific committee has demonstrated the adverse environmental effects of phosphates and has found the alternatives to be appropriate. i would like to hear the commissioner's opinion on this. finally, madam president, i am pleased that agreement has been reached on labelling. simple text on products approved after thorough evaluation should be sufficient. if the curious consumer wants to know more, he can consult the manufacturer via the website and telephone.

president, the 2001 action programme against hiv/aids, malaria and tuberculosis has now been in force for two years. the situation has not diminished. the three diseases together are responsible for more than 20,000 deaths a day. in the past year alone, according to the who, some 40,000 people were infected with hiv/aids every day, and according to the latest figures, 16 million children under 15 have lost one or both parents to aids. tuberculosis is one of the world's most important infectious diseases. one third of the world's population has been infected, and it is expected that 5 to 10 percent of them will actually become sick. malaria is on the rise. in africa alone, malaria kills about one million people each year. pregnant women and young children are the hardest hit. the seriousness of the situation cannot be overstated. the death rate from this disease is still rising and will continue to do so. it is clear that the least developed countries are the most affected and that is why we are talking about poverty diseases. they restrict social and economic development. in many developing countries, society is being completely disrupted. the death rate is falling to a large extent, and businesses and organisations are running out of manpower. the education system in many countries is no longer functioning because of the mortality of teachers, and as mentioned, the number of orphans is exploding. what future do they have? a whole generation is in danger of being lost. the diseases are more demanding on health care than it can handle. the action programme can be considered a sound one, but it could be improved on a number of points. the evaluation of european development policy shows that the current programmes are not having enough effect. the european union has set itself the millennium development goals. one of the targets is to stop the upward trend of the spread of aids by 2015, but we're still not doing enough to achieve these targets. the commission has already proposed that the programme be extended to cover the whole of the community, and that the research on the causes and control of the diseases be intensified. the biggest problem with malaria, as we know, is that increasing resistance means that new drugs have to be developed all the time. i think the report reflects well the commitment of parliament to the importance of, as has already been said: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, including the establishment of a european centre for the development of vocational training (ecvet) and the first and most affected. in the 15-24 age group, almost twice as many women as men are victims. poor access to education and health care, a biologically higher risk of infection and a difficult social position contribute to this. this population group therefore deserves special attention and policies. this is all the more so since, for example, projects in the field of health care have been found to be more successful when women are involved. investing in girls has been shown to reduce maternal and child mortality, increase food security and improve poverty reduction. the unicef study development goals. moreover, it turns out that when more girls go to school, it's not just the girls who benefit, but the boys and the countries as well. finally, mr president, i would like to thank and congratulate the rapporteur, who is an expert in this field and has once again produced a good report. there is still a lot to be done for the european union and for the individual member states.

in writing. (nl) i am in favour of a europe in which the economy is gradually brought more under democratic control. this requires a completely different course from the one we are pursuing today with the green paper on universal service, which is making europe even more neoliberal. the stability pact is being used as a big stick to force member states to liberalise and privatise important economic sectors at the expense of democratic influence and control over the social and public sectors. although it is recognised that certain services should be accessible and affordable to all because social cohesion and solidarity depend on it, virtually no part of the public sector is protected. this will enable higher education, public health, social services and drinking water to be transferred to private hands. in addition, the old sectoral liberalisation plans for postal services, gas and electricity supply and public transport are being closely followed. the results of the liberalisation programmes are wrongly glorified, as is shown by the experience of the netherlands and other countries where the liberalisation of public transport and energy supply has been to the great detriment of the inhabitants. contrary to the claims made in the green paper, these services have become more expensive. the supply of services has declined and employment has remained the same. my party is in favour of a course which is diametrically opposed to that described here and will therefore vote against the proposal.
the european union is doing a great deal to involve the young generation in projects which aim to link european unity with progressive thinking. these projects risk moving to the edge of propaganda rather than education. by channelling money to organisations that promote the 'european idea' by defending the common european interest, the eu seeks to create a more cohesive european elite. in the worst-case scenario, the eu could develop into a power centre where power ends up in the hands of a pan-european english-speaking elite and where anyone who does not speak the language at home or does not have a good command of it is placed at a disadvantage in terms of employment and in terms of their ability to participate in political life, for example. different national and regional customs would then be considered a form of disability. that is why it is good that we are now at last seeking to defend cultural diversity against trade and centralistic forms of government. i fully agree with the rapporteur's assertion that cultural services and products, and in particular differences of opinion, are not consumer goods which can be simply handed over to the market. i support mrs prets' wish to exclude all related matters from the wto/gatt trade liberalization and to ensure their protection through a convention concluded under the auspices of unesco.

- mr president, i would like to take this debate to ask the council and the commission to put the situation in vietnam on the agenda for the forthcoming 60th session of the united nations commission on human rights. the picture emerges from several reliable sources, including local ones, of increasing government repression in vietnam. this is all the more true for the second half of 2003. to illustrate, a few details on the rural situation. even the official figures show a doubling of the number of death sentences for 2003 compared to 2002, especially for drugs and economic crimes. citizens are also arrested for alleged spying or criticism of the government via the internet. particularly hard hit are the protestants in the central highlands of vietnam. they are subject to permanent ethnic and religious persecution, with the ultimate sanction of the state being the deprivation of their livelihoods and their lands. and incidentally, hanoi strictly forbids these mountain dwellers to have any contact with the outside world. gatherings of more than two persons are also taboo. no wonder so-called mountain dwellers try to flee their heavily militarized residential areas, resulting in dramatic refugee conditions. mr president, all in all, shocking violations of human rights. i trust that you will address the vietnamese authorities to this effect through the global forum of the united nations commission on human rights. it is of practical importance that the united nations in hanoi demand access to the central highlands for their special rapporteurs on religious freedom and refugees. the council and the commission must also make their case in geneva.

european level. finally, mr president, i hope that this directive will be adopted by a large majority so that we can make the internal market even better than it is as soon as possible.
unlike previous speakers, i shall concentrate on the directive for which mrs kauppi is the rapporteur. tax evasion and avoidance is widespread and has a strong cross-border dimension. cigarette smuggling, vat carousels, black bank accounts, these are just a few examples from the arsenal of those who do not want to give the emperor what is the emperor's. in the european union, but also with switzerland and the balkans, for example, joint efforts to monitor effectively taxpayers operating in several member states should be sought diligently. the possibilities for controls by two or more member states provided for in this directive are therefore of great importance in conjunction with one another. we are therefore talking about improving cooperation between the tax authorities of each member state. this is entirely consistent with a national prerogative for both tax legislation and taxation. tax legislation is a national responsibility and should remain so given the closer interconnection with the social order and culture within each of the member states. in this context, it is extremely curious that the debate is not about an effective approach to tax fraud but about the question of what legal basis this directive should have. the european parliament has rightly accepted article 95 as the basis for the application of the codecision procedure in similar cases. this is not a question of the content of tax legislation but of implementing provisions relating to administrative cooperation which is necessary in the internal market. what mrs kauppi predicted in the explanatory statement to her report has now happened: the council has recently submitted a formal proposal to amend the legal basis. i would call this a pavlov reaction, again the council rejects codecision without clear reasoning. now that parliament's position has been confirmed on 2 september, it is feared that this proposal will also be referred back to the court of justice. unnecessary effort and expense and, above all, possible long delay in this much needed improved cooperation between tax authorities. i dare the council to leap over its own shadow. back to the real issue, more effective controls on taxpayers operating across borders. the tax-avoiding fast guys come and go at a certain establishment at a fast pace. traditional intelligence - that is, data exchange - certainly cannot keep up. if improper use and abuse of tax legislation is detected by a joint team of auditors from the countries concerned, a 'lick-on-stake' approach is possible. with the accession of 10 new member states, the need for transnational inspection teams will undoubtedly increase further. the open borders to countries with still fragile administrative machinery will unfortunately attract fortune-seekers with few scruples, including in the fiscal field. the sooner the tax authorities can act jointly against this, the better.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. however, it will not vote for all the amendments tabled, some of which violate the principle of subsidiarity. an example of this is european interference in local elections.
i would like to join all those who have thanked mrs echerer so much. i would like to do the same with regard to the way in which we cooperate. perhaps there is a role for us in her tv programs in the future. who knows? i hope that they are well protected in terms of intellectual property rights. the fact that the report was adopted unanimously by the legal affairs committee shows that everyone agrees. the commissioner's encouraging words will also, i believe, lead to improved regulation, which is particularly important in terms of transparency and legal certainty, and in particular democratic control, because clarity prevents misunderstanding. we are all talking about the rights holders, but i think it is also very important to mention the rights users. i can still remember an example from last year when many companies in the netherlands suddenly received a letter from the reprorecht foundation that they had to pay a hefty bill for the copies they made. but without that, it was explained what that actually meant. it creates a chilling effect and unrest. i think that this legislation can make it clearer on what certain costs are based, why certain costs have to be made and for whom they are intended. the commissioner has already indicated that 5 to 7% of the gross domestic product - and that is a lot - is the result of products and services defined, governed and protected by copyright and related rights. that means that we are talking about a substantial part of our internal market activities. we must be clear about this and give it structure. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i think that this problem can be solved with these opinions, if they are codified. enlargement, as mr medina ortega has already pointed out, is also an important aspect of this issue. the political history in the accession countries has never led to clear protection of copyright holders and therefore i think it is important that support is provided on the ground. the rapporteur has also stressed this in her oral amendment. we, the liberals, support her in this. in conclusion, we think this is a good initiative and we hope that the commission will also adopt the amendment i have tabled, in particular the one to propose a clear evaluation after three years to see whether the legislation actually works and whether protection is provided. mr president, i am going to be in exactly three minutes and i will not be able to continue this afternoon.

i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. it's a good report. i share his objections to the most extraordinary procedure which has been followed here to have a debate in this parliament on competition policy. i believe that a debate on such an important part of community law should be conducted through the front door and not the back door. the commission has made a major effort to change competition policy, but unfortunately not for the better. as rapporteur on merger control, i have already pointed out that i think it is wrong to equate the commission's powers of investigation under the merger control regulation with those under the cartel regulations. mergers are not necessarily criminal, cartel formation is. i hope the commission will consider reversing this decision. the commission's persistence in shifting from the market dominance test to the slc test, despite the opinion of the european parliament, is also very regrettable. the market also needs clear and unambiguous competition legislation and a reduction in administrative burdens. the commission has also been active in the area of the environment. if we look at the current development of the distribution regulation in the automobile sector in the netherlands, i see a trend towards dominance by certain parties, in particular importers. of course, that could never have been the intention, which is why the impact assessment can be very important in advance. i am currently working on a report on institutionalised impact assessments for european regulation and i expect that in the future the european commission will also be carrying out impact assessments of competition law. president. - the rapporteur. mr president, the elections are coming up and i am being asked more and more: the european parliament is actually proposing something, do you have anything to say? i think this is a case in point and i will go into it a little more. the council and the commission initially had very high ambitions, especially after 9/11. but as time went on, it faded away like snow before the sun. there were all sorts of sensitivities about sovereignty, about legal obstacles, in short, the council did not dare to take a firm step and the european parliament was clearly of a different opinion. we have a clear objective, namely progressive integration into european airspace in the interests of the citizen. that is necessary and in consultation with the council we have finally achieved a clear commitment from the council to achieve that objective. that's a first step in the right direction. the main points we have agreed with the council in the conciliation are of course on implementation. the service providers must meet high standards and must be monitored. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the air transport sector is not affected by the crisis. i think it is important to be able to monitor this from the european parliament. we have also reached agreement on the charging regimes. the incentives can be used to encourage better use of airspace. the subsidy must be reduced and, moreover, everything must be made more transparent. we have also made a commitment to clarity as regards the future. when will we see legislation on the licensing of air traffic controllers? what is the point at which the ideas of the single sky in the upper air strata might also apply to the lower air strata? we want to be sure of that. we also welcome the fact that eurocontrol has established itself there. they have worked very hard on this and the debate on this dossier has, i think, also made everyone aware of it and that is good. a second point of difference was, of course, civil-military cooperation. i think that the greatest merit of my colleague mr fava is that he has succeeded in getting a clear reference in the text to the need for civil-military cooperation. parliament is committed to this and has always stressed this in the negotiations. it may also be a step towards better and more military cooperation in the future. we have also succeeded in making the importance of the exchange of information clear in parliament. and that goes for all service providers, including the military. we also welcome the commitment of the member states to cooperate with the concept of flexible use of airspace across borders; that will save a lot of delays. my colleague mr fava has already dealt with this in detail. finally, the point where mediation almost stumbled, the cross-border airspace blocks. i think we have reached a reasonable compromise with article 5 (6). for me personally, that was one of the most important points, because in order to make progress something had to be included in the text to provide for conflict mediation, otherwise it is too easy to hide behind someone else's back. i have put a lot of effort into this on behalf of my colleagues and i am quite satisfied with the outcome. member states are obliged to take into account the opinion of the commission and to use that opinion as a basis for resolving conflicts concerning the creation of cross-border airspace blocks. what has helped us to get through, commissioner, is the commission's promise, in the form of a written statement, to take action to review the five-year procedure if no clear progress is made in the re-division of airspace. this provides the crucial impetus needed to make european airspace safer and more efficient. this is important for the service providers, for the airspace users and, of course, for passengers. it is also badly needed with air traffic increasing by more than 2 per cent a year at the moment. commissioner, you can be sure that the european parliament has acted in the interests of the citizen and that we will closely monitor the progress made and take action if the member states fail to do so. i have to thank my colleagues for the excellent cooperation and the services they have provided. the negotiations with the council have also always been conducted in a pleasant and constructive atmosphere, with a lot of hard work and understanding of each other's points of view, but that is how it should be. i have considered it an honour to contribute my part to a safer and more efficient aviation in the interests of the citizen. i would also like to point out how important the role of the european parliament has been, because i think we have come up with a very good proposal together with the council.
the rapporteur. president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are about to approve the revision of the packaging directive. on 4 december parliament's conciliation committee approved the agreement with the council by a large majority. as rapporteur, i am very pleased with this and i will explain briefly why. there were two major issues and five minor ones. i'll start with the minor points of dispute. first, the definition of packaging. you remember the lobby and the discussion about flower pots, cd boxes, mascara holders and makeup jars. parliament wanted to avoid continuing the debate on flower pots. the solution that was found is pragmatic. no new products in the annexes, but the commission asks to give priority to products which are the subject of legal dispute: cd boxes and flower pots, toilet rolls and that sort of thing. the council accepted the substance of parliament's amendment on the basic definition of packaging. the second point was prevention, and that was an important point for parliament. the environmental impact of packaging should be minimised. the main thrust of this was the introduction of a packaging environment indicator. parliament wants to encourage pilot projects. the amendment tabled by parliament at second reading has been almost completely accepted. in the debate on the thematic strategy on recycling and waste prevention, which has already begun, it will certainly build on this. the third point is the report. the parliament's amendment on the scope of the commission's report on the implementation of the directive has been almost completely accepted by the council. the fourth point: the voluntary agreements. the council has fully accepted the possibility of reducing the transposition of certain provisions by means of agreements between the competent authorities and the sectors of industry concerned. this is a point of great importance for the netherlands. the last item in the series of small items concerns the target dates. the existing packaging directive provides for a limited deferral for greece, ireland and portugal. parliament was willing to grant a delay from 2008 to 2010, the council wanted 2012 and we agreed on 2011. the two most difficult points were the amendment on the new member states and the recent judgments of the court of justice of the european communities. firstly, the new member states. the question was who would set the target dates for the acceding countries. parliament wanted the commission to come forward with a proposal which would then be decided upon by parliament and the council. the council wanted to deal with this issue by means of a declaration on bilateral arrangements with each acceding country on the basis of article 57 of the accession treaty. parliament vigorously defended its position at second reading. the result is that the directive contains a clear statement of reasons. the requests for exemption from the rules of the countries concerned are listed in the report and the final decision will be taken in accordance with the relevant legal procedures. in the view of parliament this represents a considerable improvement on the original council position. a particularly difficult problem was the impact of recent court of justice judgments on the directive's recovery objective. the interpretation of the concept of recovery by the court of justice in recent judgments has been the subject of considerable debate. in these judgments the court of justice concluded that waste incineration with energy recovery is not recovery if this is not the main objective of the operation, with the consequence that some member states may now or in the future unexpectedly find themselves in a situation where they infringe the directive. there was a difference of opinion on the question of how to deal with the matter from a procedural point of view. the consequences of the judgments were not addressed in the common position or in parliament's amendments at second reading. the compromise reached was to amend the relevant parts of the text of the original directive. it was clarified that waste incinerated in incineration plants with energy recovery could be included in the objective of the directive. i think that, in general, parliament can be very satisfied with this result and i would of course recommend that everyone vote in favour of the agreement. the vote tomorrow will bring an end to the revision of the packaging directive, but it will not bring an end to the debate on the wider context of recycling, waste prevention and the use of natural resources. the debate in this house has already begun on the thematic strategies put forward by the commission. this agreement on the packaging directive points the way to a new system in the legislation. parliament wanted to put more emphasis on the environmental impact of legislation and a flexible way of working. if all 25 member states impose the same target on recycling and recovery, it will no longer be possible in an enlarged and more diverse union. our discussion of environmental performance in the first reading and of economic costs in the second reading almost inevitably leads to the conclusion that the optimal recycling rates can vary considerably from country to country, and ultimately we must go in that direction. but for that we must first set clear recycling targets, because that is ultimately the aim of this legislation. the aim of this legislation is further sustainable development, a concept which we must give full play to. today, with the revised directive, we are taking a step towards modernising packaging policy. a packaging environmental indicator can encourage the use of more sustainable packaging. packaging does not have to be an environmental problem in the long term, but contributes to sustainable development. continuous innovation is the best guarantee of keeping jobs in the packaging sector and i hope that the revision of the packaging directive will make a small contribution to this. finally, i would like to thank all the groups for their cooperation and to thank the excellent mediation team, led by the very competent vice-president, mr renzo imbeni.
mr president, in march 2003 when the committee on budgetary control was about to vote on the resolution giving discharge to the committee of the regions for 2001, i received a telephone call from the internal auditor of the committee of the regions, mr robert mccoy, as did several of my colleagues. mr mccoy had alarming news for us: the information provided to parliament by the committee of the regions in the course of the discharge procedure was incorrect. the next day, the budgetary control committee decided that the vote should be postponed to give mr mccoy a chance to present his views. he did so, and issued what he called a cry for help. he said that his views had been misrepresented by the secretary-general and the finance director of the committee of the regions, and that, despite a series of reforms, the cor still flouted basic financial rules, in particular in relation to tendering procedures and members’ expenses. as a result of mr mccoy’s intervention, the budgetary control committee, and subsequently plenary, decided to postpone discharge for the cor and called on that institution to commission – and i quote – 'a detailed, full and independent audit, to be carried out by an external and recognised institution – preferably by the european court of auditors – on the committee's overall budget implementation, as well as the financial and administrative management; considers that the audit should examine, inter alia, the areas listed above and attest to the sound financial management of the institution'. at the same time i decided to refer the case to olaf. the results of these investigations were shocking. in the case of the court of auditors it was shocking because we received a one-page document consisting of 300 words which basically gave the cor a clean bill of health. this was not what we had asked for. the olaf report painted a quite different picture. it had found an endemic culture of unprofessionalism and improvisation. it uncovered evidence of tampering with tendering procedures by cor officials and false expenses claims by members – including former presidents – of the cor. it went on to say that the efforts of the administration had been focused on discouraging or destabilising the messenger of the bad news, in this case the financial controller, rather than to change things for the benefit of the cor. mr mccoy was therefore right when he came to see us and he deserves an unreserved apology for the treatment he has been subjected to, and applause for his courage in coming forward. the cor has now begun to improve things: the secretary-general has been removed from his post for different reasons – although the bungled process of his appointment was also likely to be a result of the administrative chaos at the cor; the president of the cor has announced a preliminary enquiry into the opening of disciplinary proceedings; a reform process has been set in motion involving all cor staff who wish to be involved and a former member of the court of auditors is to advise the cor on the adequacy of the reforms. for these reasons the cor and its president deserve the benefit of the doubt. that is why my group will be voting to grant discharge tomorrow, but will do so with very little enthusiasm. (applause)
president. - the previous speaker, mr michiel van hulten, has already explained the history of this affair of not granting discharge to the committee of the regions in april, and i think that was also done in the resolution by mrs avilés perea. she did a very good job. following the non-discharge, investigations were carried out by two bodies: by the european court of auditors and by olaf. i must say that i am somewhat disappointed by the court's inquiry. if thorough research can be expressed in one or two sentences, i don't know what thorough research is. it's been a, to my taste, a bit of a superficial investigation. i have more admiration for the olaf report, which has looked at this more thoroughly. there have been a number of abuses; the big hope is that they're going to go away now. in this respect i agree with previous speakers who say that the role of the financial controller is essential. it was a clear sign that, when he had brought the abuses to light, one of the first actions of the committee of the regions administration was to remove an employee from his staff. i am therefore very pleased that this parliament has approved an amendment by the liberals in the 2004 budget to give two additional staff to that financial controller for the coming financial year. we believe that the role of the financial controller is essential in any european institution. it cannot be stressed enough that this person must act with complete independence, apart from the hierarchy, in order to express his judgment. we will support the critical tone of mrs avilés perea's resolution, because we think she has done a good job. my group will not, however, vote for the discharge to be given to the committee of the regions. we feel that in a year in which so much has gone wrong, we can justify doing so.

so it was not without reason that many people were very excited by these revelations. we think that the commission has generally been very diligent in trying to solve the problems of eurostat. but there are still a number of problems and several people have already pointed them out. first of all, how should the role of olaf be handled? i have always found it strange that we heard from the secretary-general that we were asked by olaf to keep it secret and not to tell anyone. i know of few officials who have to keep something in this area a secret from their political masters. there is no such thing in any administration. officials should inform their political superiors fully and i think that is clearly the lesson we have to learn from this affair. the liberal group is committed to political responsibility. the fact that a commissioner did not know something does not mean that he is not politically responsible. we wanted to put that in an amendment to the resolution. we shall vote on this tomorrow and we also think that if the hearings on the new commissioners are held later - but it will depend on the new parliament - we should question them on this point. the explanation given by president prodi at the beginning of his term of office on the individual responsibility of commissioners has not, in our view, been entirely satisfactory in this case. and we hope that this will come out in the new hearings, as i said, because we must be able to explain to the great european politicians that someone in europe is responsible for something.
president, last year the edd group did not agree to grant the european commission discharge for its budgetary management in 2001. the facts now confirm unequivocally that this was right. a few weeks after the discharge vote, information about the eurostat affair which the commission had withheld came to light. combined with a flawed accounting system, slow administrative reforms and insufficient internal controls, the situation was full. like president prodi, commissioners solbes and schreyer showed in word and deed that they had not learnt enough from the forced resignation of their predecessors five years ago. the commission is trying to impress the european parliament with its fine words, its big reports and its big promises, but there is no visible result in the internal reform of the european commission. it was only in the second half of 2003, when it became clear that a longer delay was not possible, that the commission finally took action. the conclusion must be that this commission is running on empty in its budgetary management and is not taking up its political responsibility. finally, i would remind you that we as a parliament owe a great deal to the courageous whistleblowers van buitenen and mrs andreasen in the european commission. they are entitled to full restitution. we do not forget that there are many dedicated and unbending officials in the commission. but unfortunately, they are thwarted by an internal hierarchy that wants to serve itself rather than the european citizens. a few bad apples in the basket are a disgrace to all the good fruit.
mr president, the textile sector will be transformed next year when quotas will disappear after more than thirty years. the european union's import tariffs are now among the lowest in the world. this requires other countries with a large textile sector and a large market to open up their markets in a similar way. the rapporteur and commissioner lamy are right to advocate this. secondly, after the abolition of quantitative restrictions, there will be a temptation to restrict imports in an alternative way for protectionist reasons. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. how does the commissioner intend to prevent this? it is also necessary to reduce non-tariff barriers, such as unnecessary bureaucratic import procedures. thirdly, labelling and certification. the report also calls for the establishment of a european network of national laboratories for the development of new methods of laboratory testing. what concrete steps does the commissioner envisage to make this more effective, particularly as regards the production method? finally, mr president, a comment on the trading position of developing countries. it is not just access to the european market that is needed to improve their trading position. the european union must also stop disrupting the commodity market by dumping cotton. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. ergo: the world price of a bale of cotton has fallen by half since 1990.
the new world order, mr president, requires a stronger united nations, but at the same time prevents it from being established. more than ever, major global problems require a global approach and therefore effective decision-making and implementation. the un needs to be fundamentally reformed to reflect the new relationships and to adequately fulfil its many tasks. everyone agrees on this by now and then it doesn't happen because everyone disagrees on how it should happen. it is hypocritical to embrace the un millennium declaration if we are not then prepared to give these hands and feet and to risk our necks for it. un reforms are not just technical, but often involve a redistribution of power, and that is where things go wrong all the time. for example, the reform of the security council has been discussed for more than 10 years without any result. the united states is by far the most powerful player on the world stage and has little need for multilateralism because it sees it as a limitation of its power. other countries, such as france and the united kingdom, refuse to give up their privileges from the old world order. potential permanent members shall not grant each other a privileged position. fixed seats for continents only work if the regional powers can agree on a rotation mechanism. the proposed double veto system is better than the current one, but it does not offer a real solution, especially if the number of permanent seats is still being increased. it would be better to use a european model of differential voting weighting and thus abolish the veto system. i do not think that the political coordination of the european union in the un is as bad as mr laschet suggests in his excellent report. in the general assembly, there is usually a consensus on the eu. nevertheless, europe is hopelessly divided in major crises such as the iraq affair. the main reason, i believe, is the increased rivalry between france, the united kingdom and germany. a legal entity or even an eu minister cannot create a common foreign policy as long as the major european states continue to compete for the leadership of the european union. the laschet report contains many interesting recommendations for intensifying relations between the european union and the un. some are very valuable, but together they may be very much for the short term. we must be careful not to create a new un/eu bureaucracy. i think that our parliament is there primarily to control and not so much to conduct diplomacy itself. the enlarged european union can and must make a substantial contribution to the much needed reform of the united nations. political necessity is unquestionable, but what we still lack is the political courage to shape the new world order effectively within the un.
mr president, the present report by our esteemed colleague mr laschet is intended to push mankind towards a kind of world government. only in this way would it be possible to create peace, prosperity and justice with the un as the central, vital and decisive centre. multilateralism is being embraced as the new political gospel. is not this vision of the future based on a gross overestimation of mankind? after all, humans, you and i, are prone to all kinds of evil. true peace and justice do not come from humans. they are to be gained only by a sincere faith in and unconditional obedience to god, in gracious obedience to his beneficial commands as set forth in the bible, the word of god. jesus christ bore the punishment that brings us true peace. that is a point which is all too easily forgotten and which i should therefore like to make the first point. the rapporteur, following his high-minded approach, calls cooperation between the european union and the un a cornerstone of the eu's foreign policy. in the institutionally reformed un of the future, he is certain that the eu will have a legal personality in the future. the future eu foreign minister will, under the future european constitution, have to fulfil the future mandate on behalf of the eu. the security council will also have to be enlarged with permanent members, one for each continent except australia. that's weird! why is this vast continent excluded? perhaps because of the fear of a double veto? instead of britain and france, the eu should be represented on behalf of europe. mr president, this is an extremely premature approach. the eu has no constitution, no legal personality at the un and no mandated european foreign minister. the recent past has also made it clear that both in the igc and in the security council european unity seems further away than ever. so it won't work. i therefore call on my colleagues to support amendments nos 1 and 10 and thus return to the reality of everyday life. in conclusion, mr president, i have two comments. firstly, only once in paragraph 17, paragraph 2, and then in brackets, does the most effective and proven transatlantic security organization, nato, appear. you know as well as i do that when it really comes down to it we would rather see our security guaranteed by a reliable and determined nato than entrust it to an ever-wavering often indecisive and divided un security council. nato is a better cornerstone for the eu's foreign and security policy than the un. in conclusion, multilateralism is good but never unconditional. we have seen too often that nation states, under the guise of multilateralism, have pursued their own interests.
