mr president, my speech is about the safety of members and the measures taken by the european parliament. on 19 january i left the parliament in brussels at about 8 p.m. by the wiertzstraat exit, heading towards rue de wavre. i was less than 100 metres from parliament when i stopped at the traffic lights. i heard a huge bang and my windshield was smashed in the passenger side. my bag, which was on the floor, was grabbed. i can assure you, a lady's purse really does have it all. tickets, passports and so on. this is a matter of course which could happen anywhere, but to have it happen so close to parliament is extremely serious. what i find incomprehensible is that this happens repeatedly. i can mention a few female dutch parliamentarians who have been through it. i went back to the european parliament, where i was very well received. i have no complaints about that. the security came, but - you probably don't know - i couldn't make international calls after that. so i couldn't call to block my credit cards, which i think is a first requirement, you'll understand. then the police were called and i was allowed to go with them to the police station. i just wish i could follow the police with my broken-down car. i came to the police station, where there was a policeman who spoke flemish, but a flemish that did not resemble dutch. nice guy, nothing wrong with him, but that was very complicated. of course, since i had nothing left - no money, no driver's license - i was promised a ride to carglass, but it never showed up. so at about noon, i went to my hotel, where the reception was a little more efficient. the damage is enormous and i would ask you now to ensure that in such a case, at the exits of parliament, calls to foreign countries can be made immediately if a member so wishes. that there is assistance to take me to the police station, because i would have been a dane who could not understand dutch at all or a national from any other country. of course, it is also very useful if you have some money to take a taxi. i also believe that it is extremely necessary to improve the protection of the safety of members of parliament. as i have already said, the damage is enormous. i will not say the amount, but i do expect the european parliament to ensure full reimbursement.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are in a somewhat complicated situation. i think it is very remarkable that some speakers from the kingdom in particular are over-emphasizing the debate. that is not strange either, and i shall come back to that in a moment. i would like to begin by thanking our rapporteur for having written an excellent report. everyone knows that our committee voted very narrowly and that the vote could have been different. the commission is aware that there are differences of opinion in this parliament too. but we have to look at each of these ideas very carefully. it is only an owninitiative report, but it was necessary. we were actually here to discuss a revised directive. i think there is no doubt that the revision of this directive is very much needed. i'll also argue why. the commission is rather late and i support the comments made by mr hughes on the communication submitted. the material submitted and the published study are informative but not conclusive. people who had been doing this for a long time knew what was going on. i would like to say that i am not against the commission's proposal, but i am against the commission's proposal. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules are not applied in the member states. of course, not least of all are the employers and especially the whole heavy lobbying that is taking place at the moment. i was called by the british minister for social affairs last thursday afternoon and asked how i had the nerve to table some very strong amendments. i think that's going a little far. i am pleased that he is approaching me, but i do not do business between the potato and the soup, especially since it ignores the existing problems. first of all, with regard to the general individual opt-out. the question we have to ask ourselves is: we want minimum social standards at european level and especially on very important points where even the ilo - i think it was in 1919 - said: you think 48 hours is enough? do we want a minimum? are these arrangements designed to protect safety and health? or do we want to move towards a situation which is slowly but surely beginning to emerge and which i describe as social policy competition between member states? that's what this is about. i'll give you one example. at the moment - and the dutch council of ministers is also represented - there is a debate in the netherlands on the adaptation of dutch legislation on working time to the european minimum system. that is to say, it is also proposed in the netherlands that the 60 hours should no longer be included in a consultation procedure but should be included as a standard procedure. that's going a long way. i know of other countries where this debate is taking place. i think we should not leave this debate to the social partners. they can be consulted on this point, but they should not be decisive, as some amendments suggest on this point. i'll be very brief about the guard duty. that's the problem we have to try to solve. i welcome the commission's proposals, but there is still a lot more to be done. it is not only the doctors, but also the fire brigades, who are currently facing a lot of problems in a number of member states, and i can mention a number of other services. as far as i'm concerned, we need to do business with each other quickly and clearly. i call on the irish and the dutch to give full attention to this thorny problem which we are unlikely to deal with again in the form of a revised directive during this parliamentary term.
to mr titford's relief, the dutch government is not following the example of the united kingdom. it is a different problem. that's what this is about, actually. on the one hand, we have the whole annoying thing of almost becoming a sort of house of commons here, namely that we are only concerned with english, indeed indeed not even uk problems, but english problems. this is of course very annoying, especially for those who are taking part in this debate and who are not from this country. then there is the second difficult point, namely the question of the cases mentioned earlier, the jaeger case, and so on. i really think that the commission has not shown enough leadership so far. it should have been seen that the combination of the interpretation of those cases and the existence of that opt-out would lead to difficulties and undermine the objectives of the directive. the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. and then there are other things, like people who think, we can use that and actually create additional difficulties, contrary to the directive's purpose. all of that can happen, and then the whole thing starts to unravel. i am not saying that we should not be able to do this. we must first of all deal with those points which concern the hospitals, the fire service and a few other things of that kind which are not confined to the united kingdom. then we can talk about the opt-out. that is not reflected sufficiently in our report at the moment and it is certainly not reflected sufficiently in what the commission is saying. it's just a list of options. that seems to indicate that we have a very long process but by then the member states have already made a decision. they've already used that opt-out, and you just don't know what's going on. finally, i myself always consider an opt-out a sign of failure. then you haven't been able to get it all the way around. so that's the problem behind this. i still think we should look at alternatives to that opt-out. but you cannot say that you cannot see this as a separate issue if you do not offer alternatives. if you have no alternative, then you shouldn't say that opt-out is wrong. you have to say: that opt-out is wrong, but i have these alternatives. that is what we should be striving for in the context of this report. this is certainly something that should be pursued in the context of further consultation. first, however, we must close as quickly as possible the gap in the directive, or at least where the member states think it is. we have to be able to take the necessary measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. and then we can look at that last bit of that second opt-out.
mr president, aid for the development of industry in developing countries is intended to improve the trading position of those countries. i therefore wholeheartedly support the european commission's efforts to achieve a predictable legal framework and to reduce corruption. the commission and the modrow report are less convincing on the reform of state-owned enterprises. firstly, it over-reaches both the policy framework and the range of tasks of the european commission. the commission states that all options must be examined before a developing country chooses to reform a soe. but it itself recognizes that the considerations for reform are political decisions. it is therefore unrealistic to require an objective assessment of the various possibilities. secondly, social policy. the commission's communication and the rapporteur put the demand for social measures in parallel with reforms. that sounds sympathetic, but it's not appropriate. the european community has no competence in the field of employment and social policy in the developing countries. these are outside the scope of technical assistance and european development cooperation. thirdly, mr president, the relationship with the policies of the member states. both the commission communication and the modrow report make little reference to development cooperation in the member states in this area. the sustainable development relationship between a developing country and a member state could be the ideal framework for, for example, providing assistance in the event of job losses. finally, the relationship between state enterprises and poverty reduction. the commission intends to use poverty reduction as a criterion for assessing aid to state-owned enterprises. this is not in keeping with the neutral position of the european commission with regard to private or public property. the criterion should either apply equally to private undertakings or not be applied. in conclusion, mr president, i agree with support for companies in developing countries, but the approach to state-owned enterprises raises important questions.
i would also like to thank the commissioner for his statement. he is now a commissioner with a great deal of experience in the field of epidemics, animal diseases. the big question is: what have we learned from the past few years? one of the main conclusions of the commission for the control of foot-and-mouth disease was that the external borders should be protected. what happened to that conclusion? when i go into, say, amsterdam or dublin i still see a big difference with, say, the united states of america. i don't think the controls have changed much in the last few years. i may be wrong, but i would like to hear from the commissioner what exactly happened. how is it that i read in the newspaper that at schiphol in amsterdam, mats with disinfectant are laid down, but that this is not the case anywhere else in europe? how can it be that belgium is taking different measures? what does the commission officially recommend to the member states and how is that advice followed up? imports from thailand and other countries have been stopped. there were rumours in the dutch press this weekend that chicken meat from the newly acceding countries is entering the european union through a side door. is that true or not? mrs roth-behrendt has already spoken about the need for further research, vaccines and test methods, et cetera. the point is that this parliament has given the commission twice in the last two years the opportunity to use that extra money. not in category 3 for research, but extra in category 1a of the agricultural budget. the commission has taken the liberty of not using that. why not, despite the fact that the same commissioner has stated in this plenary that this would be the case? it was a failure to carry out the wish of the budgetary authority and i wonder why the commission ignored that wish? the following are the main types of crisis management: the commission has already said that there is an emergency plan. i understand that there are indeed certain plans in preparation, but what about human health? the flu vaccine that could help humans is limited. has the commission consulted the member states on how it will be used from the different countries if in another country there is not enough of these resources available? finally, something for the oie. trade is based on trust and cannot be controlled by the food and veterinary office in dublin alone. it depends on the information that countries provide to the oie. it has now emerged that indonesia, vietnam and possibly china have probably waited several months to provide information to the oie. what does the commission intend to do in this context to improve the provision of information so that trade can take place under fair conditions and we do not run the risk of suddenly being confronted with illegal imports of these kinds of products?
commissioner byrne, thank you for your factual statement, but i must say that i am very much concerned about what we are doing. we must, like the liberals, recognize that we do not have one external border, but 15 different ones, where various measures are being taken to prevent the possible importation of avian influenza. we have seen this with foot-and-mouth disease and we must take measures to prevent a similar situation in the future. next, i must have something else of the heart. if our elderly are at risk of getting sick with the flu, there are mass vaccines available. in this situation too, we know that vaccines are needed, but there is no work being done or far too little work being done. the only country that's working on vaccines is italy. i think that the european union as such should make an effort to develop vaccines so that we can also deal with this disease in a humane way.

i would like to congratulate mr garot on his report. he is one of the colleagues in this parliament whom i hold in high esteem. but if i look at his report, my criticism in this case is that the socialist angle is too dominant. there has been too much emphasis on premiums and too little on the competitiveness of agriculture and horticulture. the general trend is that the european debate on agricultural income is dominated too much by the question of how much premium is received and too little by the question of what market problems there are. to start with the premiums. the netherlands receives far too little of the european budget. the netherlands accounts for 7% of agricultural production in the european union but receives only 2.1% of the agricultural budget and 1% of the rural development budget. and then there's the whole unbalanced thing. of course, there is a cause to be found. the negotiations in berlin in 1999 were conducted by kok and zalm. the fact that they had no feeling for agriculture and horticulture is not only the reason why the dutch countryside does not get what it is entitled to, it is also the real reason for the high net dutch contribution to the european union. the best remedy for this excessive contribution is that the netherlands should receive a substantially higher amount for agriculture and rural development. there is also a second point which has not been highlighted in the development of agricultural incomes, namely the position of the so-called free products. the high environmental, food safety and animal welfare standards in europe affect the cost of meat, vegetables and potatoes, for example, and leave european agriculture behind in terms of cost worldwide. that is the real reason for a number of crises, for example in the pig industry at the moment. my proposal is that all new european legislative proposals affecting agriculture and horticulture should be subject to an income test to see what the real impact of that legislation is on rural workers.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on agriculture, fisheries and rural development, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). the rapporteur, mr garot, rightly points out in his report that all these fine words are worth nothing at the end of the day if we do not provide a reasonable income for the farmer. the interpretation of statistical income data is not simple. mr garot concludes that agricultural incomes in the union have risen by 7% in recent years. mr president, i have asked the researchers who provided these figures whether they are correct. they state that these figures do not take into account production levies, interest payable, rent and labour costs. if you look at what the farmer actually feels in his pocket, between 1995 and 2001 agricultural incomes in the eu did not increase but fell by 6%. the resolution rightly calls for the maintenance of price and market policies and the control of production that goes with them. in addition, the expansion of supply is not by definition beneficial to agricultural income. the extension of the milk quota is further reducing the price of milk and putting unnecessary pressure on the incomes of dairy farmers. the wto summit in cancún has failed. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. the eu than from unrestricted free trade. it is a pity that a socialist, who naturally believes in a society which he can control, should accept the liberalization of agriculture as a natural value. if we want to preserve the family business and reject unbridled expansion, we must adapt our policies accordingly. this means that high farm subsidies must be capped and that small and mediumsized enterprises must be systematically protected. that is what the european farmer gets out of it at the end of the day!
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the countries concerned would automatically experience economic prosperity if such companies were given the freedom to take over loss-making activities and export raw materials. it's a short-term assumption. the economies of the countries concerned have been controlled by the northern countries for centuries and have been forced to produce cheap goods for the markets of the rich countries. the internal economy and services to the countries' own population have had to be neglected. services in areas such as education, health care, public transport, housing, postal services, electricity, drinking water, telephone and sewage have largely had to be developed and expanded by the colonial government itself. due to high development costs and the prospect of limited profits, these sectors were initially largely uninteresting to foreign private companies. we have also seen how, in the 19th and 20th centuries, the first stage of japanese industrial development was only possible thanks to public enterprises which were privatized only when they were profitable. it was therefore also positive that the committee on development and cooperation was not only concerned with privatisation and full liberalisation but also with services, the fight against poverty, small businesses, cooperatives and sustainable government initiatives. unfortunately, the christian democrats are blocking today in the plenary the way to a majority for these instruments to combat poverty and underdevelopment.
mr president, better a lie than a half-truth, so i would paraphrase a well-known expression. i've been experiencing the validity of that spell for the last few days. one of my colleagues whispered to the press that the dutch members of the christian-democratic group and my group never pay back any of their travel expenses. radio, tv and newspapers picked up this message without any proper investigation. since then, my colleagues and i have been inundated with calls and e-mails asking why i withhold money. that is weird. i'm not returning any money because i don't report trips i didn't make. i am aware that many fishermen are in a similar situation. they must not defend themselves against a lie but against a half-truth. the situation on the northern slope is not rosy, but it is not so bad that a recovery plan is necessary. the european commission, based on unspecified data, is proposing a drastic reduction in the amount of fish to be caught. recent data from biologists indicate that the hedgehog is not in such bad shape that drastic intervention is needed. i therefore wholeheartedly support mr souchet's report and call on the commission to adjust its plans. a management plan for the northern hake with adequate control and enforcement is sufficient to provide this species with a future perspective. the lack of scientific research is already hurting fishermen too much. it is time for sound scientific research that helps both fish and fishermen.

(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) a good argument for maintaining european agricultural subsidies is that small farmers are enabled to survive the fall in the price of their products and that they contribute to maintaining the quality of life in rural areas. this argument falls apart if tax money is misused to line the pockets of rich farmers and agricultural multinationals. a recent study by the development organisation oxfam shows that eu agricultural subsidies in the uk benefit landowners in particular. thus the richest of them, the duke of westminster, is estimated to receive about £38 an hour in farm subsidies. the rapporteur, mr garot, who, unfortunately, as a social democrat, nevertheless regards the liberalization of agriculture as a natural phenomenon, now also notes that there is something wrong with agricultural subsidies. between 1995 and 2002, agricultural income increased by 7%, but the number of farms decreased by 15.7%. the remaining conquests became larger and more intense. agricultural aid is unevenly distributed. 20% of farms receive 73% of the direct aid for 59% of the area and only 25% of the jobs. the rapporteur is particularly concerned about farms which produce little but which are still receiving aid. i am thinking more of a ceiling, a maximum limit for the aid per holding. without such a measure, the agricultural funds become a source of useless bureaucracy and unfair redistributions.
in writing. - (nl) the i have neither voted for nor against mr garot's report, despite the fact that it supports the development and reform of an agricultural policy with separate premiums and a strengthened policy for rural areas. the report does not, however, take sufficient account of the limitations on the financing of the common agricultural policy. the financial room for manoeuvre also influences the policy pursued. i would refer in particular to paragraph 25, which rejects cofinancing as an instrument for financing agricultural policy and rural development policy. in my view, compulsory cofinancing is one of the important instruments for the future implementation of the common agricultural policy.
in the first place, the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens and mutagens, which is the subject of a proposal from the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, is not acceptable. the compromise incorporates a points system based on duration, with quality points being awarded for each course. this system ensures that not only the duration but also the quality of the training is important for the evaluation of the training. the compromise also strengthens the rights and obligations of the professions, which are subject to an additional duty to protect consumers, by means of compulsory registration and insurance when services are temporarily provided in another member state. the same right of access to the labour market remains for women. a discriminatory provision which allowed the retention of a qualification only if one had worked continuously for two years in the preceding five years has been abolished. the directive is vital to increase labour market mobility, which is an essential prerequisite for achieving the lisbon objectives. a transparent qualification system will become more important, especially in the light of enlargement, which will lead to a further increase in cross-border immigration.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) young people are told that it is becoming easier for them to work abroad. some enjoy working abroad for a while to gain experience. it is different if it becomes a duty, because your profession is dying out in your own country and you can only get such a job far away. and people are disappointed if they have to work abroad for a lower salary and without recognition of the diplomas they have obtained. the eu's propaganda includes the idea that everyone can work in any member state for the normal wage that is common there and that diplomas are mutually recognized. however, the practice is very different from this ideal. foreign teachers are not eligible for equal work in france and italy. according to petitions recently received, greece does not recognise diplomas obtained by greeks at universities abroad, and there are also problems in baden-württemberg and scotland. i do not have the impression that the proposed amendment to 15 directives really solves these problems. the most important of these is greater freedom of establishment for the liberal professions, which means that foreigners no longer have to comply with the licensing and registration requirements which are still compulsory for their colleagues from the host country. in the opinion of the specialists, the commission has not done a very good job of comparing their diplomas. the complaints will continue.
in the case of the european parliament, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right of initiative. these are all minimum provisions which could be tightened up. in particular, it is necessary to prevent the governments of the member states of the european union from starting to regard this european minimum as normal and to reduce their national legislation to this level, as the dutch minister de geus seems to want. in practice, the directive is being eroded by a concession made to the then british conservative government. the opt-in clause means that individual british workers who have to sign their employment contract are asked to give up their rights. those who do not often have to leave the job to someone else who is willing to do so. an additional problem is that the hours of call for firefighters and health workers are often not counted as working time. now that this has to be done according to the court's judgment, the opt-out option is being applied to these people. this makes it possible for people to work extremely long hours for extended periods of time. the committee on employment and social affairs voted by a small majority to open infringement proceedings against the uk government and to abolish the individual opt-out. this proposal is being torpedoed today by a conservative majority.

- mr president, this parliament has always been strongly in favour of the promotion of a sustainable freight transport system. each modality has a role to play. in theory, these roles are clearly identifiable. the practice is more resilient. we can see that some modalities are not really coming out of the blue. one problem is the wide variation in charging units, which have often arisen in everyday practice. some are widely used, others less so. it all depends on the market situation. the latter is a dominant factor in freight transport. changes are only meaningful when they are supported and stimulated by the market. this will only happen if there are real benefits to be gained. the commission proposal still has a few gaps in this respect. the introduction of a new charging unit is a good idea, provided that it can actually be used in all modes without any modifications. it must be applied in practice. all the commotion and commentary surrounding this proposal suggests otherwise. we therefore welcome the fact that the present report makes the proposal more market-oriented. the eilu is no longer the ultimate solution to all existing problems, but an option for future use, embedded in current operational activities. the vote tomorrow will be taken on a point of order. amendment no 16 is not acceptable. if the measures mentioned in this amendment are adopted, this would in fact mean that the concept is eliminated. in order to preserve the positive aspects of the eilu - more pallets in one unit - it seems to me that it would be useful to leave the actual dimensions to the market so that the companies that do want to use the unit also have the space to do so technically. mr president, all in all, mr stockmann has done a good job.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the community. afghanistan is seen as a collection of isolated valleys between mountain ranges, with different languages and cultures, which have little in common except religion, islam, women's oppression, and that in the 19th century they were combined to form a buffer zone between the areas under british and russian influence respectively. while tajik, uzbek and baluch people also live in neighbouring countries, the question of banning their languages within the borders of afghanistan is being discussed in the context of the drafting of the afghan constitution. the outside world imposes an all-powerful president and a unified army to bring about unity, but in reality neither the president nor the army has any power outside the capital. such illusions of unity force the outside world into a permanent military presence in a conflict between peoples who do not want such unity at all. the first prospect of lasting peace is a swiss-style state based on largely autonomous cantons with different languages. the text before us shows that the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy is beginning to understand this and is calling for decentralization, a strengthening of parliament and independent courts. i'm very pleased. i can support the proposal, with the exception of the amendments tabled by the ppe-de group which go against what has just been said.

president. - europe and america have long sought to exert a major influence on developments in iran, iraq and afghanistan. often, that influence leads to results that are quite different from what was previously expected. the muslim fundamentalists in afghanistan and the regime of saddam hussein in iraq could not survive until recently because they were supported by the west against others. in iran too, there has been a long-standing attempt to keep controversial friends in the saddle. half a century ago, mossadegh's regime was purged because it was too left-wing and might cooperate with its then russian northern neighbours. an authoritarian ruler, the shah, returned to power but then, through his vanity of grandeur and neglect of his people, made more and more enemies at home. when left-wing nationalism and socialism no longer had a chance to become the alternative, a medieval variant of islam was given new opportunities to organize popular resistance. a quarter of a century ago, a popular uprising brought to an end a regime that had always sought military and economic cooperation with the west. the outside world has never trusted the new rulers. after a few years of great enthusiasm, social policies and democratic experiments, this regime has also created hatred within itself. the large young generation does not want the exclusion of politicians with views different from those of the conservative clergy, nor restrictive rules for women, the expulsion of students, the frequent amputation, hanging or stoning of people who do not fit the world view of those in power. many have fled the country of their birth, often to europe. nevertheless, in recent years europe has sought to align itself with this regime on the basis of economic interests. america president, in five years, ten months and four days, 2010 will begin, the year in which, according to the lisbon agreements, the european union must be the most competitive and dynamic knowledge-based economy in the world. that is not enough time for an ambitious target which has been the subject of much discussion up to now. and when i read the reports by my colleagues on the state of the current economy, there is reason to be somewhat wary of that short period. in this combined reading we are discussing two reports on economic policy and public finances in emu and a resolution for the forthcoming spring european council. in the two reports we focus on what has happened so far and what is not going well. in the resolution for the forthcoming spring summit we shall express our expectations and wishes. right now, those expectations are mostly paper deals. economic growth is moderate, member states are facing budgetary problems and the united states economy is now growing faster than that of the european union. so we are far from being the most competitive region in the world. the motion for a resolution tabled by the epp and the eldr has my support in part. the objectives of the lisbon process are too ambitious and not sufficiently clearly defined. that's why it's so important to update now. firstly, the lisbon objectives should be reconsidered and better defined. then we might get some attainable goals. and you will understand that this parliament too can make its views on the progress of this process even clearer if it sets achievable objectives. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the basis of the results of the consultations. it is not just talking about how things can be improved, but also doing something about what is being said here today.
president, ladies and gentlemen, president of the council and commissioners, when the spring summit takes place next month, it will be exactly four years since our european economic ambitions were set and welcomed in lisbon. unfortunately, in the past four years, the necessary progress has not been made to achieve these ambitions. the problem is obvious: the member states are not doing enough to make the european economy competitive and dynamic. in the run-up to the spring summit, mr chirac, mr schröder and mr blair have joined forces, ostensibly to revive the european project. such a disagreement rarely leads to positive consequences for our european economy. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. our european economy is currently stagnating because of the poorly functioning labour markets in germany and france. and were it not france and germany who burdened us with agreements on the agricultural budget, which now means that we are facing a huge increase in the european budget after enlargement? and who's gonna pay for that? britain certainly does not. they're clinging to their rebate. the liberal group believes that the strongest shoulders should bear the heaviest burdens, but all strong shoulders should. ladies and gentlemen, with this resolution we call on the member states to work towards the lisbon objectives and to keep their economies healthy. but we in the european parliament must not burden industry with unnecessarily heavy administrative and financial burdens, such as the proposed reach chemicals policy. but it is only the member states that can ensure that europe remains globally competitive. it is important that the stability pact be maintained and that everyone keep their budget in order. only this can result in the prosperity and job growth so badly needed by our citizens.
i would like to begin by thanking mr roche very much for his speech from the council, and particularly for what he said about the elderly. if we could create a better labour market for older people, then i think we could achieve the lisbon objectives in the field of employment. i was therefore very disappointed that the commission is only talking about abolishing the financial instruments for the elderly. we might as well take away the old people's right to vote. what the commission is saying is totally unacceptable. the creation of a labour market must provide opportunities to reduce the need for such schemes. secondly, mr president, the legislation itself. the commission has rightly pointed out that the legislation for the internal market must be introduced quickly. but there are also proposals from the european commission which are questionable as to whether they will make a significant contribution to employment. i'm afraid sometimes the opposite is true. and yet i am afraid that in the field of financial services, as in the takeover directive, people are following what they think is happening in the united states. i think you should look very carefully at the employment implications of this. and then, of course, the environment. it is true that the evaluation of existing and the creation of new environmental legislation must also take much more account of the employment effects than has been the case so far. it is too easy to minimize that, as mr langen does. the last point is productivity. i think the question of productivity is still very much underestimated. productivity is what brings everything together. if we improve productivity, we can do more, we can get better jobs. but it is often the other side of this house that stands in the way of improving productivity, because too much is being done to cling to old ideas that have little resonance in this day and age.
the european commission should draw up a roadmap for the member states, showing who is going to achieve what, when and how. the european council in berlin has once again shown that there is agreement on the objectives, but not on the measures. so we're not getting anywhere with that. a roadmap with precise targets to replace the current vague agreements should help government leaders to set out on the path to boosting the knowledge economy and making the european labour market more flexible. the european commission must also state at the spring break how far it has come in the abolition of unnecessary legislation. the commission has promised to cut 25 per cent for european industry. the reduction in administrative burdens will enable businesses to re-enter the market and thus generate economic growth. a european supercommissioner for economic affairs is not the answer. chancellor schröder could have known this, because the german super-minister for economic affairs and labour, wolfgang clement, has not been able to prevent the german economy from continuing to have one of the lowest growth rates in europe.

(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the cities of macau and hong kong were the last remaining settlements on the chinese coast that were governed from europe. by the end of the last century, their long colonial status had rightly come to an end. when they were returned to china, it was agreed that a capitalist economy should be maintained in these cities, but for democracy and human rights there are no such clear guarantees. such agreements are well suited to developments in present-day china, where the heirs of the communist party continue to rule. they do not allow any political opposition and from their protected position of monopoly of power they contribute to the restoration of capitalism. it was foreseeable that the european agreements to return areas to china would lead to the departure of inhabitants who had in the meantime developed european expectations of democracy. these people can no longer legally enter portugal or the united kingdom, and if they do, they are treated as criminals. an agreement with china and other dictatorial countries to allow refugees to return will not solve the problem. unfortunately, once again it seems that europe is more concerned with driving away refugees and expecting to benefit from economic relations with dictatorships than with defending democracy and human rights, for which campaigns have often been launched.
in writing. - (nl) my party, the socialist party of the netherlands, has always supported the efforts to obtain a statute for members of parliament. nevertheless, already at the end of 2002 we asked the dutch government in the council to reject the high monthly salary of eur 8 600 for members of parliament, which was set by parliament and is ultimately part of the statute. such a salary attracts the wrong people to the role of member of parliament. an increase is not at all justifiable at a time when many people are losing their jobs and when social security is being reduced. i am surprised at the greed with which the social democrats and the greens are putting forward amendments which seek to get the member states to accept the statute of members of parliament without first expressing the view that the salaries proposed in it are too high. i am also absolutely not in favour of parliament's money being used to secure the funding of european political groups in order to avoid the commission's objections. the european union still has a surplus of money at its disposal for the time being if we put an end to the vast under-consumption, the useless prestige project galileo, which has been taken over by competitors, the waste of agricultural aid to large farms, the military plans and the costly frauds. this does not justify politicians claiming the right to use these surpluses on themselves.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the eu has two extremes in its relations with russia. on the one hand, there are those who want to take revenge on the old enemy, the soviet union, and who regard the present russia in particular as a conquered territory which will continue to supply oil and natural gas for a long time to come. on the other hand, there are those who want russia as a lasting friend because it is the nearest neighbour, because it has a large population and because it deserves support as a developing country. these two strongly opposed views ultimately have the same negative consequence. they prefer, for reasons of their own economic interests or selfless solidarity, to ignore the shortcomings in the fields of environmental protection, human rights and democracy. if they get their way, russia can continue unchecked to develop into a state which intimidates opponents into withdrawing from all political activity, which deprives parliament of its role, which crushes national minorities by military means, and which completely subordinates the environment to the expectation of short-term economic gain. if we continue to ignore the current problems in russia, the eu will be responsible for creating a monster next door. the member states must not seek conflict with russia, but certainly must not avoid tensions with the russian government at any cost. it is therefore necessary for parliament to issue a critical opinion.

madam president, last december the council rejected a proposal by parliament for a broad definition of the family. as one of the initiators of this proposal, i am naturally pained by this. the council has nevertheless opened the door to free movement for all eu citizens, regardless of the form of relationship they choose. in countries where registered partnerships exist in the national legal system, citizens from other countries with the same relationship should be recognised. member states are expected to facilitate entry and residence for partners in a durable relationship and if member states refuse to admit a family member, they should provide justification. the big step forward, in my view, is that registered partnerships are now recognised as a form of relationship in eu law for the first time. it is also becoming clear that de facto relations are a problem in the eu's free movement and that countries can no longer ignore this fact. it will be interesting to follow the case law on the basis of this new directive. it's certainly not the end of the fight. i'm just saying that after mrs. lambert. a well-known question in political deliberation is always: is the glass half full or is it half empty? in this case, i think, given the discussion in the council and the current political context, that this is the most feasible thing we could have done for eu citizens. i do not see this as giving in to the council's blackmail, as our friends turco and cappato did in their minority position, but rather as counting my blessings. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on his report.

president. - i speak on behalf of mrs dorette corbey and i would like to make five comments. firstly, transparency and control of food safety is of course of great importance. more rules and more controls do not in themselves guarantee better food safety. the only way to end food riots is to demand transparency. information on companies that are not as strict about food safety should be public. you mess around, you lose customers. transparency is an important condition for food safety and we are pleased with the compromise reached. secondly, the financing of the checks must be fair. we must prevent unfair subsidies to companies and unfair competition. small firms can get a discount, so that we can provide some assistance to this category of firms, which is very important in the food sector. a third point concerns the correct and fair application of this legislation. our group has called for uniform checks and uniform methods throughout the union. it is not possible for the consignment to be rejected in rotterdam on grounds of food safety and then to enter the union by another route. our question to the commission: can the commission give a commitment that it will, in the interests of the european citizen and in the interests of fair competition, monitor closely the correct implementation of this legislation? fourthly, our group is very pleased with the support which the union is going to give to the developing countries to help them build up their capacity to comply with european food safety rules. that's how the knife cuts on two sides. the union is increasing the food safety of imported products and developing countries are receiving significant support to take measures that can improve their food safety situation and thus their trading position. food security must not be a cover for protectionism. commissioner, we must also keep an eye on the current situation. in view of the outbreak of avian influenza in asia, it is important that food safety is controlled in the countries of origin. does the commission agree that this and other community legislation provides the union with good and adequate protection against the introduction of infectious diseases such as avian influenza? finally, a general comment. i would like to thank mrs paulsen again, on behalf of our group, the social democrats, and also the council and the commission for their constructive work. we hope that this milestone will mark the completion of an important piece of work over the last five years and we look forward to the commission's replies.
i would like to thank mrs fourtou, all the shadow rapporteurs and also the commission and the council for the structural and constructive cooperation we have had over the last three months, because the way in which a compromise has been reached on this directive is, in my view, exceptional. the committee on legal affairs and the internal market thought a little differently, but in the end i think that this compromise is a start and i hope that it will be adopted. mr president, intellectual property rights are finally being recognized throughout europe. there were already various directives and legislation, including on copyright, electronic commerce, software, etc., but none of them spoke of enforcement, or at least not enough, and this is why we are left with a patchwork quilt in europe. in this respect, it is good that we now have a kind of umbrella legislation for the harmonised implementation throughout the union of all the existing directives in the field of intellectual property rights. as has been said by many, counterfeiting and piracy are increasing hand in hand in europe. we see that criminal organizations are moving from drug trafficking to counterfeiting because the chances of getting caught are minimal and the profits are as high or maybe even higher. i think we should call a halt to this. i think, mr president, that we need a harmonised approach, not only because criminal organisations are making money illegally from these products, but also to protect our economy, to achieve the lisbon objectives and, ultimately, above all to protect consumers. - why? - why? we're not just talking about, as some people think, copied cds, but a wide range of products that are being copied. i call toys, for example. i've seen toys that contain cadmium. the cadmium directive prohibits the use of cadmium in toys produced in europe, but counterfeit products may still contain it because they are imported illegally. medication was also mentioned. there are examples of painkillers that contain talc powder and i don't think this is possible. there is a lot of money to be made in this, but i think the consumer must be protected. even aircraft parts are counterfeited because of the high profits that can be made from them. it is good that something is being done to put an end to this. i was the one who suggested that we should include a reference to criminal law in the green paper phase, because we do not buy a stolen bicycle because it was stolen, we have so many standards and values, but we would like to buy a fake breitling watch or another fake product because it is cheap. if this is incorporated into criminal law and possession of a more expensive counterfeit product is made a criminal offence, then there is no longer a market for it and it is no longer interesting for organisations to produce counterfeit products. it is a pity that certain strict rules have not been included, as mr berenguer fuster has already pointed out, but i am satisfied with the compromise reached. a lot of the ambiguities that lobbyists used to argue against are no longer in the proposal. i believe that this proposal is the beginning of the end of counterfeiting and i hope that it will develop into a mature directive with mature means of enforcement.
president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the enforcement directive we are discussing today is completely unnecessary. there is already a directive on the harmonisation of copyright and, in addition, there is also the directive on electronic commerce, which regulates, among other things, the rights and obligations of service providers. this new directive threatens to disrupt the obligation to monitor what users do. the compromise now before us therefore expressly states that earlier directives will remain in force indefinitely, and that is a good thing. why else would we be legislating here? we do not want american states where legislation is made on the whim of the day. this new enforcement directive officially declares illegal copyists thieves, but everyone has long known that the illegal copying of software, cds and dvds is a criminal offence. there is absolutely no need for a new directive, and certainly no directive which does not define the scope of intellectual property law. we have different criminal justice systems in europe with different sentencing levels and we cannot harmonize the legal systems with a directive, certainly not without a proper debate in this parliament. i am glad that the compromise now limits the scope of the directive to piracy for commercial purposes. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented. i shall nevertheless vote against the directive.
president, ladies and gentlemen, for many years now, the european union and this parliament have been complaining about products from the israeli-occupied territories being unjustly given the same preferential treatment as products from israel itself. israel does not want to make the distinction, but the european union does. the heart of that debate is political. the occupation of the areas in question is illegal in the eyes of the european union and that is why we have made this matter such a problem. the debate, which is thus politically oriented, is, however, in danger of being overshadowed by a number of practical and legal aspects. the problem is - and this is the reason for asking these questions now - that the commission and the council, tired of years of back and forth, are now threatening to agree to the current practice. if that happens, an illegal practice will be legitimized and the member states will lose the legal basis to continue to distinguish between products from israel and products from the occupied territories. that is why i have two questions to the commission. first of all: what steps does the commission intend to take before november this year, when a new commission takes office, to put an end to the current practice and when exactly? secondly: can the commissioner guarantee that any decision he takes which may have consequences for the spirit, the letter and the practice of the association agreement will first be submitted to this parliament for its opinion?
mr president, commissioner, we are talking about the 1995 association agreement. i have no objection to this agreement as such. it contains fundamental norms and values which are respected and which israel as a democracy can rely on. that's not the problem. what i find incomprehensible, however, and why i have asked these questions, is that the european union has for years been allowing israel to violate the rules of origin of the convention. under these rules of origin - which are, of course, a piece of cake for you - israel is not allowed to sell products from israeli settlements in the occupied palestinian territories as if they were from israel itself. otherwise, the products of the illegal settlements will enjoy unduly favourable import duties in europe. as mr lagendijk has said, this is a political matter. i am also aware that the european commission has addressed israel on this matter and has been given the ironclad statement that israel regards the settlements as israeli territory. the european governments are not reacting and are thus agreeing to this violation of the agreement which they themselves have approved. this makes us partly responsible for the continuation of the colonization policy, one of the greatest obstacles to peace. that, commissioner, is the crux of the matter. we cannot be partly responsible for this. the member states are obliged under the ec treaty, the community customs code and the association agreement to refuse preferential treatment on the importation of products from the settlements. as commissioner and guardian of the treaties, you must ensure that the member states comply with these obligations. mr president, these problems must be solved, and immediately, and i therefore ask the commissioner if he really wants to put an end to these cases and come up with a real legal solution so that a uniform verification procedure can be followed in the member states. the customs authorities of the member states can then simply collect import duties on settlement products without the importers being able to challenge this. president. - i call mr glinne. the only thing is that we do not want to be partly responsible for the settlement problem and i ask you to put forward any possible solutions to parliament.
mr president, as the debate shows, this is a complicated and sensitive subject. i can agree with the previous speakers, and in particular with my colleague armin laschet. mr president, there are obviously serious questions about the preferential access to the community market for products from the israeli settlements in the west bank and the gaza strip. as i understand it, preferential imports from the areas mentioned would be in breach of community law. now i understand that the european union wants to amend the association agreement with israel by adding pan-european cumulation of origin and implementing jordanian-israeli cumulation of origin. but the question is whether such a system of cumulation is possible without a solution to the problem of preferential imports of products from the occupied territories. i think it is of the utmost importance that the european parliament is consulted in good time before any part of the existing agreement is amended. i join with previous speakers: it is important that this should be done, if possible, before the end of the mandate of this commission. we very much appreciate the commission's clear statement of these problems. does the commission see any way of resolving the problems with israel within a reasonable time? i thank the commissioner for his first comment and look forward to his second. as the previous speaker has already pointed out, it is not a question of a measure for or against israel.
mr president, i hope to set an example of dutch sobriety. on 4 september 2003, this house was already debating the correct application of the ec-israel association agreement. on that occasion i quoted the statement of the dutch minister for foreign affairs that 'for the vast majority of products bearing the israeli certificate of origin, the netherlands can assume that the israeli customs determinations of origin have been validly made, that is to say that they meet all the conditions of the origin protocol'. last year i asked the commission if it could endorse this hague statement for the other member states of the european union. an answer remained then. perhaps the commission representative will be a little more vocal today. but i'm not going to give up and ask you a second question. is the collection of customs duties from importers of products suspected of originating in israeli settlements a unilateral policy? what is the commission's view on this?
the amendments to the budget, which will be voted on shortly, relate to the accession of the new member states on 1 may this year. the committee on budgets has already unanimously approved this budget and parliament as a whole also approved everything in principle last december. for legal reasons, we have to make this official now, and that could happen soon. i would point out that the observers in the committee on budgets played a very active and constructive role in the adoption of the budget for the new countries and that we in the committee on budgets increased certain payment appropriations. some in the council always think that parliament is too generous with money and has a hole in its hand, so to speak. i would point out that with the enlargement of ten new countries the percentage of gross national income spent on the european budget is 0.98%, the lowest since 1990. so i think parliament is very good with money. if you vote yes in the near future, and i hope you do so in large numbers, then there will be no financial obstacle to the admission of all the new member states to the european union on 1 may this year.

the commission's proposal for a regulation on the application of the principle of equal treatment for men and women in matters of employment, vocational training and promotion, and equal treatment for men and women in matters of employment, and the proposal for a regulation on the application of the principle of equal treatment for women and men in matters of employment, vocational training and promotion, and vocational training, and the proposal for a regulation on the application of the principle of equal treatment for men and women in matters of employment, vocational training and promotion, and vocational training, and vocational training, and vocational training and retraining, is a very important one. is this a signal from the commission that the countries of the western balkans are now suddenly counted among the candidate countries, even though they are not officially candidates yet? i would applaud it, let there be no misunderstanding about it. it's remarkable in every way. as regards the proposals themselves, the commissioner has already referred to their source, the thessaloniki summit last year. at that summit, under strong pressure from the greek presidency, the european council wanted to make a gesture to the countries of the western balkans, with the main political message that they had indeed embarked on the road to europe and that the eu wanted to help them on their journey by offering them an additional instrument in the form of the european partnerships. the reason for this initiative was that there is no doubt, not even in this house, that these countries will ever become members. the only question is when and under what conditions. but if i look at the commission's proposal to implement the fine decision of thessaloniki, mr president, commissioner, i have to say that i have serious doubts whether that decision was wise. what is the added value of introducing a new instrument, namely the european partnerships, at this time? we are, as the european union, and you know this better than we are, involved in the extremely complex process of the stabilisation and association agreements being concluded with all the countries of the region. at least that's the goal. we have succeeded with croatia, we have succeeded with macedonia and we are still negotiating with the other three countries. this process is going to be bumpy. it's not easy. in three of the five countries, major problems remain and much work remains to be done. now, suddenly, in the middle of that process, a new instrument is emerging, the european partnerships. they are not coming after the stabilisation and association agreements but through them. i must say that i do not understand this commission very well, but i think that the commission, like parliament, is bound by the mandate of the council, even if it cannot say it out loud like we can. i fear that a new instrument has been created which no one needs and no one is waiting for. it creates uncertainty in the region and i think we must be very careful not to offer the countries there an empty box with a nice ribbon around it, when we ourselves do not know exactly what we want with this initiative. this is a matter of symbolism, ladies and gentlemen, and we must be very careful about this. if we're going to continue this, there are two things i'd like to point out. firstly, the finances which normally go with such partnerships - look at the great example of these european partnerships - namely the partnerships prior to the status of candidate country. so if we're creating a new instrument, why not add new money? your colleague, mr patten, has already said here on several occasions that he has no difficulty in spending the money he has now and that he would like to have more money from parliament to help those countries. i can therefore accept the idea of european partnerships, but then it must be clear how much money we have available for this. if no more money is spent than is already available, the whole initiative is an empty box with a nice bow around it. it's the terms that matter. the new instruments must not impose new conditions. we have already set very strict conditions for the european union's aid to these countries: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the return of refugees, the fight against corruption and cooperation with the hague tribunal. please let's keep it that way. i am glad that the commission has said that the existing conditions will not be changed, but please make this sufficiently clear! the new instruments shall not contain any new conditions. i am passionate about the european union's strict adherence to the existing conditions, because i do see the tendency in the council at times to go a little bit beyond that and not stick to the criteria that have been set in the past. this is necessary, though. the tactic must be rooted in the fact that the problems in this region are serious and if we give the impression that the countries in question will ever automatically join, it will end badly. i am in favour, let there be no misunderstanding, of going ahead, of wholeheartedly supporting the reforms and the reformers. the european union has no other option than to support these countries and the people who are leading them in reform. if we need new instruments for that, fine, let's introduce them. but if we have already come a long way with the instruments we have and if new instruments only create ambiguity, i reject the new instruments.
president, commissioner wallström, rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, the heavy metals and pahs mentioned have been known for many years to be harmful to the environment and to human health to a greater or lesser degree. these substances are not meant to be in the air. the impact studies do not add much to this in my view. these studies cost a lot of money and leave us as politicians with the same amount of uncertainty. i believe that we must do everything we can to remove as many of these harmful substances as possible from our air. the european commission is only interested in monitoring pollution. i think this is too limited and i have a fundamental objection to this approach. mapping pollution alone does not improve air quality. the other subsidiary air quality directives do propose binding limit values. i don't understand why this wasn't done here. on the other hand, i also realise that we must be realistic, perhaps the limit values in the previous subsidiary directives were too strict. this does not mean, however, that we should now opt for non-binding targets. in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection i supported the approach of the rapporteur, mr kronberger. mr kronberger is now negotiating an agreement at first reading with the council. i think this is a good cause. as the outcome of these negotiations is not yet known, i cannot take a position on this at this stage. i would agree with mr kronberger that we should not, of course, set binding limit values below the background concentration. i am a strong advocate of realistic limits. for example, it is known that emissions are largely caused by diffuse sources, such as heating of homes and traffic. these sources are very difficult to deal with. however, there are some results in several sectors of industry. we are not thinking only of the metal industry, but also of power stations, waste incineration plants and the waste recycling sector. the incineration of waste releases a considerable amount of heavy metals. how much is not known precisely, because the obligations for measuring emissions are very minimal. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to meet its own energy needs. i hope that we can reach an agreement with the council on this.
the greens/efa group fully supports the report by mr kronberger. limits are necessary, for example for arsenic. this is a real problem in my country. the town of coevorden, near the german border, has embarked on a plan to burn virtually all of europe's old railway carriages. the arsenic from those buttocks is being blown into the air by this. that's completely wrong. that's why we need strict limits. if they cannot be immediately available because the political majority is not in favour, that is extremely regrettable, but these heavy metals are carcinogenic and their use must be restricted. if that is to be achieved in the short term by means of targets, i am in agreement with a shriek of approval. but in the longer term, there should be limit values, based on one extra case of cancer in a million cases. we have taken the same approach with the drinking water directive. it was accepted by the commission, which had proposed it, the council and parliament. why don't we do the same for arsenic, cadmium, mercury and nickel? that would be the way to a possible compromise.
president. - on saturday, the day before the cancún failure, the alliance of african and low income countries gave its opinion. they felt that the united states in particular had completely ignored them. the united states is supporting south carolina's cotton farmers with as much as 3.5 billion euros. europe, ladies and gentlemen, is supporting its cotton sector with only 800 million euros. the african cotton states are being threatened by this. this is how active poverty is created. the lowest-income countries rightly protest against this. this is confirmed by the resolution adopted at the acp-eu joint parliamentary assembly in february. as aids drugs are the symbol of the breakthrough in doha, cotton is the symbol of the failure in cancún. the cotton aid in europe fits into an agricultural policy with money-wasting subsidies for cereals, meat and milk. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. from quantity to quality. but the conservative lobby in the european parliament has blocked that. now the commission is coming up with a modest proposal to decouple 60% and then shift a large part of that subsidy to rural development in the region concerned. but the majority of the european parliament is hiding behind the us and behind the unknown of the consequences and therefore advocates a 20% disengagement. i can understand my spanish colleagues if they want to tackle not only cotton but also grain, meat and milk, but i regret that we have not acted more decisively and shown that we have understood the lessons of cancún. by weakening the commission's proposals, we are not sending enough signals to those people in the world who want fair trade. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i hope that we are sending a clear signal to the european commission. i urge the commissioner to persevere.
mr president, the disconnection of aid from production would give european agriculture an advantage in the world trade organisation negotiations. that is what commissioner fischler has always said. the european agricultural policy has been adjusted, but there is no sign of any change within the world trade organisation. worse still, other trading blocs do not seem to be planning to end their know from experience that this argument doesn't hold up. the european union accounts for less than 3% of world cotton production, while it imports 48% of its cotton duty-free. the claim that european - mr president, some major bottlenecks in the transeuropean transport network have blocked the movement of people and goods at the junction with the alps, the pyrenees and some sea lanes. not only the hauliers but also the inhabitants of mountain, valley and coastal towns complain of tremendous traffic congestion. no one felt called to build tunnels and bridges to remove these bottlenecks. moreover, for many years rail transport has not kept pace with the need for fast connections between the major cities in different countries. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the transport services provided by the member states. it would be shortsighted to measure our prosperity, our well-being and our progress by the growth of transport, or by the surface of asphalt and concrete for highways and airports. on the contrary, the avoidance of unnecessary transport contributes to a better environment and to the well-being of humans and animals. my group believes that an adequate level of public spending is essential for public services, social security and the environment. on the other hand, waste of public money and megalomania do not solve any problem. we are opposed to the idea that endless investment of community money in asphalt is the best way of creating and maintaining jobs or of helping the new member states to progress. nor are we happy with the idea of offering excess capacity to operators so that they can choose again and again which infrastructure financed by community funds can be used by them at the moment most cheaply. the van miert plan, presented at the end of june last year, was immediately hailed by the italian minister for transport as the answer of transport to the euro. does he view traffic as an uncontrollable natural phenomenon that makes people dependent on it? in our vision, people and the environment are above traffic. that is why we think that the van miert plan is too large, too dangerous for the environment and far too expensive. a more selective plan, focusing on more direct and faster rail transport, is preferable. that will be our starting point when we amend this proposal.

mr president, it is generally assumed that all health care systems in the european union are based on the principles of solidarity, fairness and universality. these include ensuring that every person can receive appropriate, high-quality care when ill or in need of care, regardless of income, ability or age. the charter of fundamental rights of the european union, drawn up in 2000 and to be incorporated into the european constitution, guarantees an elderly person a life of dignity and independence and the right of every person to access health care and medical treatment. the draft constitution also provides for a high level of social protection. so everyone can rest assured that everything is perfectly arranged on paper. however, in practice there are increasing complaints about unacceptably large distances between home and hospital due to the expansion of scale, waiting lists for admission to a hospital, a shortage of care for elderly people in need of care, a shortage of medical and nursing staff, a reduction in available medicines and the need to divert across the border. the lack of care leads some people to an early death and others to such a low quality of life that they desire death by voluntary euthanasia long before they enter the natural death stage. of course, there are all kinds of excuses for such mistreatment. people are living longer and longer on average. they have fewer children than in the past and those children are being consumed by the ever-increasing compulsion to work for men and women. help from neighbors and children is becoming less and less available. in such circumstances it is irresponsible to reduce taxes and premiums on the grounds that we need less. as this is happening, there is a growing public concern about the affordability of health care, elderly care and pensions in an ageing society. increasingly, too, there is no staff available for care work, for in times of economic growth, these people are drawn away for higher wages to the most profitable sectors of production at the time. when they lose their jobs as a result of an economic crisis, they do not have the appropriate training to cope with care tasks. the drain on the labour force from countries with lower living standards is exacerbating the problems there. in the applicant country romania, a large proportion of the population is now uninsured, i.e. dependent on the charity of doctors. the transfer of insurance and care to private companies, which claim to have lower costs and yet want to make a profit, further undermines accessibility, quality and equal treatment. the european union must act as a brake on this development rather than a catalyst. if we are to achieve all the good intentions that are written on paper, we need a collective approach on a larger scale than is possible within families through volunteer work. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
