(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) at the first reading on 15 december 2003, when i was rapporteur for this report, i concluded that there were major disadvantages to the privatisation of postal services in terms of quality, punctuality of services, prices to be paid by consumers and working conditions for staff. in addition, there are now added disadvantages, which mean that persons who post letters have to pay an additional fee. i was therefore pleased that a majority in this house shared my concern and rejected the report. unfortunately, the state-owned french postal service has jeopardized its previously good record by putting pressure on the french left. la poste considers that its interests are best served by welcoming the tax, which it can recover from the state. to my surprise, my colleague, philippe herzog, defended this position yesterday afternoon, and today the french members of the group of the european socialists have prevented the report from being rejected again. tomorrow my group will, despite our sympathy with the particular situation in a particular country, stick to the original rejection which i spoke for. the damage has been done, however. the european citizens are again being taxed in a way that is not proportionate to their income or wealth.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. although the decisions taken last year confirmed enlargement, the brok report lists 148 areas of concern, to which are added the points added by amendments, including my own. it is apparent from this text that some members are mainly concerned with the inadequate privatisations, the gradual removal of all obstacles to the completion of the internal market and what they consider to be a disproportionately high level of public expenditure. i strongly disagree with them. in fact, these countries have already yielded to many unjustified and unreasonable demands, which means that their difficulties can only increase in the future. the price they have had to pay for joining the eu will prevent them from meeting the expectations of their people, which will be a threat to democracy. other meps are more concerned about the desperate plight of millions of roma, the marginalisation of the russian minority in the baltic countries, the continued discrimination against homosexuals, the introduction of electoral rules that exclude minor political movements and the serious disregard for environmental concerns. i support this criticism and will continue to do so even after these countries have joined.
in the light of the above, the commission considers that the new rules should be applied to all the member states, and that the new rules should be applied to all the member states. yesterday i heard the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and the european democrats use the argument that too few companies have been privatised in romania in recent years. there were at least as many reasons for serious criticism of romania under the country's former right-wing government as under the present one, which describes itself as left-wing. children beg and sniff glue, the poor have no right to medical treatment, public buildings and facilities are decaying, democracy is flawed, and corruption and the total collapse of public services are as much a part of the country today as they were then. i even recently received a complaint from a romanian party, the socialist workers' party, which now has to change its name to socialist alliance in order to remain legal, which started out with 4.6% of the vote in the 1990s elections but later in the official results was awarded 3.9% and consequently fell below the 4% threshold for membership of parliament. the commission has also taken note of the fact that the romanian authorities have not yet taken the necessary steps to ensure that the acquis is fully implemented. this could be a valid reason for not speeding up its accession in relation to turkey, which also has major problems.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union is able to compete in the world market for the information it needs. if this is to be possible, the european economy must be given the appropriate conditions. these conditions are provided by the amendment to paragraph 33 adopted by the committee on legal affairs and the internal market, which stresses the need for deregulation and reduction of administrative burdens on businesses as well as the need to set up an independent advisory body to examine the applicability and impact of eu legislation. eu legislation is becoming increasingly detailed and at times intrusive and disruptive. this creates a lot of discontent in business and the public, which undermines support for the eu and has proved to be one of the reasons for the low turnout in the european parliament elections. the distortion of competition resulting from the amount of administrative burdens imposed on european companies means that economic regions such as the united states and china pose an economic threat to our jobs and prosperity. the vvd delegation, as part of the group of the european liberal, democratic and reformist party, voted against amendment no 12 by the group of the party of european socialists, not because the liberals are in any way opposed to the principle of equality, but rather because amendment no 12 involved a change in the wording of paragraph 33, which we wanted to retain.
mr president, the deep structural crisis in haiti calls for a long-term international commitment. otherwise, there is a risk of further political and socioeconomic decline, with all the dissuasive consequences for the population of these countries. the seriousness of the situation in haiti has not changed, as i recently read in confidential domestic reports. the rehabilitation of the haitian national police is a prerequisite for the restoration of public order. the reliable core of this force deserves the full support of the temporary international force. the only way to achieve the mass disarmament of all the irregular armed groups is to do so. so far, the international military force has been too passive. free play for criminals and armed gangs is the result. this will continue to cause bloodshed in haiti, warn informants on the ground. that is not possible, commissioner. i strongly advocate international action in haiti to stabilize the situation. what else will happen to the provision of humanitarian emergency aid? meanwhile, the remaining foreign aid workers are under tremendous workload to alleviate the distress. i quote one of them: "yet i remain personally convinced of the value of our work here, or i would have left the country long ago as requested by my worried family. i think that in these difficult moments, more than ever, it is necessary not to leave the haitian people alone in their struggle for autonomy and democracy and to support them without directing or forcing them". the european union has a duty to help haiti, and to help it to do so. what about the tireless efforts of courageous haitian relief workers? they don't want to leave, and they can't leave anyway. i therefore ask you, commission, in anticipation of a new government in haiti, to do everything possible to resume the current activities as soon as possible. we must not lose time again.
president, under the slogan "freedom of speech", an estimated 5,000 to 7,000 ukrainian citizens demonstrated in the country's capital kiev the day before yesterday. the demonstration is based on the increasingly repressive media policy of president kuchma's regime. it is not without reason that the neue zürcher zeitung headlined last saturday that the opposition media in ukraine should fear for their survival. the situation is over. exactly one week ago, nikola tomenko, chairman of the ukrainian parliamentary committee on freedom of the media - note the name - gave a devastating prop lecture in the verkhovna rada, the parliament. in this year of presidential elections, kuchma is trying to suppress dissenting opinions by all available means. the death of yuri chechik, the director of the private radio station, last wednesday is currently very controversial in this regard. chetchik was killed in a traffic accident. he was on his way from his home town of poltava to kiev for a meeting with radio svoboda, the ukrainian branch of radio liberty. this circumstance alone raises suspicion about chech's unexpected changes. only a few hours after his death, ukrainian security forces stormed the kiev-based radio continent station and confiscated all broadcasting equipment. this station had apparently aroused the displeasure of the ukrainian authorities by taking over programs from radio liberty. meanwhile, the director of radio continent has left the country, purely out of self-preservation, as he says. the same radio continent also employed journalist georgi gongadze, who was first reported missing in 2000 and then found beheaded. the obvious campaign against independent media being waged today by president kuchma and his associates contradicts the official goal of kiev to promote democracy in the country and seek closer ties with europe. mr president, in the new york times earlier this week, former us secretary of state madeleine allbright called for transatlantic action to defend political and press freedom in ukraine. i hope that washington and brussels are really joining forces to protect and extend the frontiers of freedom, as allbright says.
president. - in europe, venezuela was formerly known only as a supplier of crude oil and iron ore and as a base for european adventurers. should that country go back to the way it was because an unyielding president ignores the democratic will of the people? i would like to share with this parliament my contrary experience in that country. half a century ago, i was one of the children in the venezuelan capital of caracas who did not go to school but wandered through sewers and among mountain-side neighborhoods with orange-box houses and corrugated iron roofs. a small elite owned big cars, luxury homes and golf courses, but on the streets, next to the luxury office towers and shops, i saw sick beggars with no legs. the hospital where my father died was a mess. the limited public transport was run by several companies, each with its own coloured buses but no good connections. in other parts of the country, like the second city of marakaibo, i saw one of the most excluded, poor indian populations. the road from the capital to the coast was full of crosses of people who had fallen into the depths, and on other roads dust storms would form as you drove over them. the dictatorial president had his police force, recruited from italian emigrants, hang opponents from lampposts. and those experiences gave me all the hope and expectation that people would not accept this outrageous combination of dictatorship with extreme wealth, extreme poverty and extreme neglect forever. forty years later, the people's despondency was over and they finally had hope for security, for building and for solidarity. they voted democratically by a large majority for a different parliament, a new constitution and a very different kind of president. the fact that president chávez has relied too much on the military and has not handled the aid to victims of the great natural disaster does not justify the coup in which he was kidnapped and temporarily replaced by the president of the employers' organization. nor do his shortcomings justify the falsification of huge numbers of signatures in order to obtain sufficient support for a referendum, so that he can be suspended again. while america recognizes the current venezuelan government as legal, the two largest groups in the european parliament are guided by their contacts with former governing parties that were voted out by the venezuelan electorate. it certainly can and should be better in venezuela. but a return to the past will not help. if the european union supports the new venezuela, that is also the best way to deter the government and political opposition from provocations and violence. unjustified nostalgia, on the other hand, only leads to misfortune. president. - mr president, i have just received a request from the commissioner for information. this is primarily due to the transparency directive and the criticism of the requirement for quarterly reports. with the compromise that's been reached, i can get out of here. what i regret is that there are no more substantive requirements for reporting. i had made three suggestions on this. the first was to provide information on management salaries and bonuses in annual reports. the commission has now announced that it will come forward with a separate proposal on this, so that was a successful action. the second was on the reporting of payments to public authorities by extractive industries in particular. this was a wish of ngos - like the publish-what-you-pay campaign - and also of the benevolent part of those companies themselves. these companies are keen to submit to an obligation and thereby avoid unfair competition from competitors who do not yet voluntarily report such payments or bribes. a recommendation on this subject is now included in a recital to the directive. that's a half-success worth following up on. the third point was to report in a verifiable and transparent way what a company says it does about corporate social responsibility. unfortunately, this point has not yet been included in the text, but i would like to draw attention to it again. it is not a question of obliging companies to be socially responsible. however, when a company is praised for all kinds of good things, this must be shown in a transparent and clear way in a controlled report, as in the case of financial trading and trading. i am quite satisfied with the isd directive. from the outset i have tried to bridge the gap between the opposing opinions and interests, using my own touchstone. in house matching by large banks, which breaks the monopoly of the stock exchange, is from my point of view a positive development and can, under the conditions we are now creating in the compromise, provide additional opportunities for investors and for entrepreneurs who need capital. the compromise has not become a purely english city directive, nor a stock exchange directive which, by theoretically abolishing the merger rule, would nevertheless lead to a de facto monopoly for the stock exchange. it is a middle way that has led to the adoption of the proposals i made in the first reading. i think that there are additional things that have been included from the council on the subject of supervision, which i also welcome. so this directive has been given the green light in that respect too.
the rapporteur. president. - mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, today we are faced with the choice of whether we find the so-called light agreement which the european commission and the council intend to conclude with the american authorities to be adequate. i would remind you that in march last year parliament expressed its view that the present situation of unlimited data transfer to the united states should be brought to an end. parliament repeated this last october, asking the european commission to specify what data could be transmitted legally and without risk. parliament also called for the immediate replacement of the current pull system by a push system and for negotiations on an international agreement which would offer european citizens real guarantees and the same legal protection as american citizens. that's the key, and that's the point. what is now on the table is a so-called light agreement, an agreement in which the european parliament is basically excluded. we are not asked for our consent, we can only give an opinion. president. - ladies and gentlemen, i think that a light agreement was deliberately chosen, whereas we had asked for a heavy agreement. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. now we're partially out of the game. the article 29 committee, the group of experts, has said that this agreement is not adequate. i, as rapporteur, propose that parliament follow the opinion of the article 29 committee. what is the case? article 1 of the agreement itself says that the existing situation of unlimited data transfer to the united states can continue as long as a push system is not developed. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we have been informed of the time it might take to get a push system and i have also put that in my resolution. we could have a push system in three months. in fact, there could have been a push system by now, because in december we were told that such a system was coming. so what we are doing now is legitimizing what we have twice labeled as illegitimate. in the meantime, we are being pressed hard by both the united states and the european commission to approve the agreement. this is despite the fact that it is not clear what the agreement is for. on the one hand it is said that it is used to combat terrorism, on the other hand it is also used to combat serious crime. however, these are not defined. i find the latter extremely difficult. parliament has called for an agreement which clearly defines the role of airlines, how they are protected and what guarantees passengers have that they can correct their data. we have also asked for an agreement to regulate the liability of airlines and public authorities in the event of transcription or coding errors. in the meantime we have gained some experience with the united states. mr sörensen of my group, after he had flown to the united states, asked to see his data and to correct them if necessary. after two months he got a reply, and the answer was that he could not see his data, because that would mean that the strategy of the united states would become clear. if we look at the legislation in the united states, we have to say that the privacy act should provide some protection. i have asked the united states to tell me exactly what this protection would consist of and i have not received a reply to date. another thing that comes into play is the patriot act. this law allows for the prosecution of virtually any citizen suspected of terrorism. he has no access to the courts. in short, ladies and gentlemen, the judicial system in the united states is not sufficiently regulated. the protection of our citizens in the united states is not adequately regulated. there is no equality between european and american citizens. so this is a case in which we would be completely lacking if we approved it. it is not a question - as has been suggested - of our not being prepared to fight terrorists. on the contrary, we have already asked the commission three times to come up with a proposal that could also be valid in europe. it is not about bad relations with the united states. that is completely out of the question and, in my view, should not be part of the debate at all. the point is that we must protect our citizens and they must expect that from us, just as the citizens of the united states must expect that from their government.
first of all, mr president, i should like to congratulate mrs boogerd-quaak on her excellent and clear political analysis and her perseverance. we're going to need that kind of perseverance. mr president, i repeat once again that the fight against terrorism is necessary, but not all means are justified. the agreement we are discussing today is one of those instruments which goes beyond the objective of combating terrorism. the commission has concluded in its adequacy finding that the united states provides an adequate level of protection for the passenger data transferred. i am not so sure about that, however. the current agreement does not provide sufficient guarantees for the citizens of the european union. there are no adequate legal remedies for citizens who get into trouble, as has been shown by the examples given by a number of colleagues. in addition, it is explicitly stated that the data may also be used for other purposes, such as domestic crime prevention in the united states. it is not clear to which third party in the united states the data is being transmitted. control of this is very difficult. the article 29 working party has unanimously decided that this agreement is contrary to european law. the council also cautiously suggested in february that the us measures might conflict with european national legislation and this doubt was not removed, as mr watson has already pointed out in connection with the recent european council - on the contrary. it therefore appears that the commission is the only body which still maintains its conclusion on the appropriate level of protection. perhaps it is time for the commission to admit its mistake and to admit that it has exceeded its powers. the current proposal is not sufficient and therefore the commission must come up with a new proposal which includes adequate protection mechanisms for european citizens. under the guise of fighting terrorism, european legislation has been violated for almost a year now. if the commission is so convinced that this agreement is in conformity with european law and not contrary to the treaty, i really do not understand why it is not prepared to apply the procedure of article 300 of the ec treaty and to have the court of justice examine this. i am therefore glad that this possibility is still maintained in the resolution before us. the battle is far from over.
i would like to thank you all very much for your comments, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, but i would like to add something to them. i am a little confused by what mr bolkestein has said. he said: "we're still negotiating and then we'll determine if what we're doing is adequate". however, i believe there was an adequacy finding which you said: "it's all right". it 's two to one . it's okay or it's not okay. i would say to mr pirker that according to the dutch data protection board there are 1800 law enforcement agencies in the united states that can request data. there is still a lot of uncertainty and if even the european commission is uncertain, why would parliament give its consent at this time? i am absolutely of the opinion that if this continues, we should ask the court of justice of the european communities for an opinion, an opinion on whether this agreement is adequate and whether a light agreement can be concluded which interferes so far with the data of the citizens of the european union. this is the only way open to us and we must take it, because a light agreement has been reached. i would ask you, commissioner, to withdraw this agreement and to come up with a better one.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. the 2002 annual report shows a sharp increase in the number of fraud cases reported. this is not a good thing in itself, but this increase also means that more attention is being paid to combating fraud and irregularities. more attention is good, but then more must be done in practice, both in terms of repression and prevention. the commission is lagging behind in improving direct management. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the establishment of a european agency for the evaluation of medicinal products for human use. as long as managers want to control their controllers, a transparent climate in which people take responsibility and discharge cannot be created. the amendments tabled to delete paragraphs 1 and 2 from the resolution are a matter of concern to our group. is this parliament created to protect the commission? even when she makes mistakes or fails to do so? such an uncritical attitude rightly arouses distrust among the electorate, because this parliament has a primary task of control and must carry it out with the utmost seriousness by means of a consistent fight against irregularities and fraud, including in its own house. this is a first step towards increasing the structurally low turnout in the european elections. finally, the failure to recover amounts paid in excess or wrongly. is there a consistent record of this in the accounting system? why is the commission only recovering a small part? how do you explain that to the citizens of the member states? is it in their interest that you recover as little as possible?
the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal for a directive on the protection of individuals with regard to the processing of personal data by the community institutions and bodies. mr president, as the rapporteur has said, this directive puts an end to a persistent form of sex discrimination, namely the so-called statistical discrimination. in statistical discrimination, the average differences observed at a given time between groups, in this case groups of men and groups of women, are absolutized into permanent assessment criteria for individuals. unfortunately, this statistical discrimination in the private insurance sector is still very common in a number of countries. the essence of insurance, however, is risk sharing. to rely on people for a characteristic that they cannot influence themselves is to be considered discrimination. the apparent logic of the insurance industry's gender mainstreaming is a relic of bygone days - falling back on the easy way. we must fight it on principle. the opponents are engaged in rearguard combat. i am therefore pleased that the report by mrs prets has been adopted by such a large majority in the committee on women's rights, and i hope that this will also be the case in the plenary. but it is waiting for the next step, which will put an end to the so-called hierarchy of equality. european anti-discrimination legislation is a patchwork of laws which itself discriminate between the different grounds of discrimination. different groups are thus afforded different degrees of protection. why is racial discrimination in education and social services prohibited by eu law, but sex discrimination in these areas not addressed? and why is there no provision in this area and in that of social security for discrimination on grounds of age, disability and social orientation? we look forward with great anticipation to the european commission's promised green paper on the future of anti-discrimination legislation as a whole, and then to new reparation legislation. the european union, which claims to be a community of values, cannot accept a hierarchy of equality.
the proposal concerns equal treatment for women and men in the access to and supply of services, and in particular the actuarial problems in insurance services. it is therefore not logical that the committee on women's rights should have been given this report. this committee has abolished the principle of equal treatment and replaced it with equality between men and women, but that is not the point here. this amendment creates a lack of coherence in the text. the amendment now prohibits sex discrimination, less favourable treatment, including on the grounds of paternity. equal treatment should also be ensured in the insurance sector. life expectancy by sex alone should therefore no longer be a factor in the calculation of insurance premiums. life expectancy risk calculation is based on outdated data from a different male and female role model. the premiums must therefore be based on solidarity between men and women. there is more variation within the group of men or women than between men and women. although there is a statistically significant difference between men and women, it can be explained by the past. it is not likely that this statistically significant difference will persist in the future, due to the decreasing differences in lifestyle and occupation.
the committee on development and cooperation has adopted a report on the proposal for a council decision on the conclusion of the agreement on the european economic area. mr president, the two commission proposals concern agreements on political dialogue and cooperation and not so much an association agreement. that is why we do not include in these proposals an integrated strategy for economic and social development. this region certainly deserves it. firstly, a partnership agreement on cooperation with the andean countries can help to ease the explosive political and social situation in the region, for example in colombia. over the past 15 years, more than two million people have been displaced from their homes and there is a huge humanitarian need. it is necessary to work harder to find a political solution to the armed conflicts and to restore peace and social justice. the social situation in the andean countries deserves more attention. many of their inhabitants still live in extreme poverty. two extremes: in colombia, the supposedly richest country, a quarter of the population must survive on less than two dollars a day. in ecuador, the poorest country in the region, the figure is even above 50 percent. in the countries of central america, on the average, a third of the population must survive on less than two dollars a day. in nicaragua, just four years ago, the poorest 10 percent of nicaraguans had less than 1 percent of total consumption, while the richest 10 percent of the country's inhabitants held more than half of total consumption. malnutrition has been a major problem in these countries for years and has even increased in guatemala. the commission's political dialogue, mr president, continues to focus on governments and the public sector. this is necessary, but it is very important to involve the parliaments and civil society in the region in the dialogue. this is a better service to human rights, democracy and transparency than if we focused solely on the diplomatic route. the union must therefore make it a priority not only to broaden the political dialogue with a number of partners but also to complement it by integrating the social and economic strategy.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is customary for us to start our contribution with a kind word about the good cooperation with the rapporteur. mr schnellhardt, i appreciate your work and your efforts, but to be honest, i am a little disappointed in your way of working. it is not very elegant to negotiate with the council on your own and to pay attention only to your own amendments. i personally think that it is not honourable for parliament to agree a compromise with the council and at the same time want to accept the original amendments. that's not a credible way to do it. ladies and gentlemen, today i would like to look back on five years of food safety and almost three years of food hygiene. in the general food law we have jointly decided that the producers of food are primarily responsible for the safety of their products and that this is controlled by the public authorities. we are now about to turn back the clock. i do not agree with this position, but i am not speaking for my group on this point. how did it get to this point? i think we should start by putting our hands in our own bosom. the food hygiene dossier is a complex whole with hundreds of amendments on all kinds of details. as a result, only a few spokesmen are involved in this matter and it is very easy for outsiders to spread misinformation. what's this all about? the central question is who controls the food producers. in the light of the general food law, the answer to this question seems simple. the producer ensures that his products are in order and the government supervises the final control. the commission, in accordance with the principle it has adopted, is seeking to give meat producers, particularly pig and calf farms, the opportunity, under public supervision, to assume their responsibility and carry out the controls themselves. some in the meat sector want to take on this responsibility and end abuses themselves. there's a lot of resistance to the idea of the butcher chewing his own meat. and rightly so, because the government must monitor and has a clear role in control. it is good, however, to give the butcher more responsibility. a majority of our group rejects the idea that the meat sector itself bears responsibility. they claimed that the staff of the meat establishments were not able or in a position to monitor the quality. personally, i don't think so. i think we need to get rid of authoritarian companies that do not tolerate criticism of the quality of their products from their employees. i think that the european parliament should not regard the workers in the meat sector as frightened, immature and ignorant creatures. as long as the government is in control, companies can be held accountable. i therefore call on everyone to support the compromise we have reached with the council and to prevent us from adopting the amendments of the committee on the environment, because that is not the right way of doing things.
i would like to thank the president-in-office for his reply. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. today is a good opportunity to complete the whole food legislation package, from safety assessment to the final step, control. there are a large number of directives which can be withdrawn with what we are agreeing today. we are going to put an end to the whole question of the controllability of food production, the whole chain approach, or the stable-to-table principle. mr president, there are some problems with the compromise package which was put forward by the council and the commission and which parliament agreed to. however, at the last minute, some sand is thrown in the wheelhouse, because there seems to be some misunderstanding. i'll say the same thing to david bowe. what's the matter? it is a question of defending the compromise proposed by the council and it is a question of allowing some extra responsibility in the company where the meat is produced, of ensuring that what is happening to the meat is properly controlled and that we then say whether we approve or disapprove of the meat, under the final responsibility of the official veterinarian and the government which appointed the veterinarian. in the netherlands this system has been in operation for some years to everyone's satisfaction. mr bowe has just said that there was a problem with red meat in scotland a few years ago, whereas they have a different system. what we have to do is to ensure that responsibility is placed where it can be absolutely borne. this means that flexibility in the system is necessary and that as much bureaucracy as possible should be eliminated, but that it should be ensured that the consumer can rely on the safety of the food he is receiving. i would therefore urge you, mr president, to support the council's compromise.
the rapporteur. mr president, i thank the commissioner for his first contribution. however, before i comment on the new animal transport directive, i would like to reflect for a moment on an event in my own country, namely the passing of our queen mother, princess juliana. during her reign she has always shown a particular interest in the agricultural sector in my country and also in livestock farming. to her, every human and every animal mattered. i commemorate her today at the start of this debate with gratitude and my thoughts are with her family and all those who will miss her. the revision of directives 64/432/eec and 93/119/ec on the transport of animals has been a long time coming. i appreciate the contribution of commissioner byrne, because after our debate on the 2001 resolution he did take up the glove. in addition to animal welfare, the sme crisis and the outbreaks of swine fever were the main reasons for me, as rapporteur, to make a thorough review of the legislation. i must conclude, however, that the commission's proposal is not a real choice. given the proposed time limits of nine hours transport and twelve hours rest, with unlimited repetition, and the very large increase in area per animal, it is safe to say that transport beyond nine hours would become unaffordable. however, as rapporteur, i am somewhat dissatisfied with this approach, because it does not do justice to the already difficult income situation in livestock farming and because it does not sufficiently reflect the reduction of the risks of animal diseases and the regularisation of slaughter. in addition, an excess of area per animal creates an unnecessary risk of injury during transport. i have therefore chosen the following line. firstly, transport up to 100 km should not be covered by the directive, to avoid making what is known as farmer transport for slaughter and use unnecessarily expensive. moreover, these are mainly regional livestock systems and there is rarely or never a problem of animal welfare in these transports. secondly, no major adjustments to the area requirements for slaughter. the limitation of transport of slaughter animals to nine hours or five hundred kilometres, and that covers 97 per cent of the transport of slaughter animals, is already in force. however, transitional periods should be provided for regions with few slaughterhouses or special market problems. i would also like to call for the establishment of slaughterhouses in regions where animals are kept to be promoted in a targeted manner. why is milk and sugar generally processed in the region where they are produced and not slaughtered? thirdly, i propose that for livestock and traditional systems for fattening animals a transport limit of 30 hours be introduced, including eight hours rest, but without unloading at stopping places. i also advocate a separate system for breeding and sport horses, with strict application of resting times and feeding rules. in general, however, transport conditions for this group are already excellent. fourthly, more and better targeted controls on international transport. this is the achilles' heel of current practice in the european union. fifthly, linking the rest and driving times in the existing eu directives for the transport sector to the new legislation. the gps system should also be introduced for international transport, so as to avoid unnecessary bureaucracy and to make controls much more effective. the fact that this last point in particular is not an unnecessary luxury has been shown, for example, by unannounced road checks in austria, in which more than 50% of the transport operators of animals were found to be in breach. the export of 221,000 live cattle a year to lebanon also raises questions. for every two families in lebanon, a whole cow on an annual basis! i believe that there is every reason to adapt the rules, particularly as regards the transport of animals for slaughter. i therefore ask members to vote in favour of amendments nos 1 to 97, 109, 110, 111, 112 and 115. that is the best balance between animal welfare and an economically acceptable position for livestock farming. if the council adopts this proposal, it will not drive livestock farming out of the market in europe and will structurally improve animal welfare. i would ask the commission and the council to adopt a provisional position at this stage. in conclusion, i would like to thank the various groups for the excellent cooperation they have shown in the preparation and examination of this report.
mr president, every day there are more and more long, brutal animal transports on our continent. dutch pigs are taken to spain and italy in 30 to 40 hours, sheep go from ireland to greece and even to the middle east. transports of 90 hours or more are common. that is beastly. horses from eastern europe are being transported to italy and greece. that should be stopped, but the european commission says it is not and unfortunately mr maat's report does not put an end to this kind of long, beastly transport. more than one million dutch pigs cross the alps and the pyrenees to italy and spain to be fattened. my party, the green left, wants to put an end to that. mr maat, however, has signed the written declaration with 333 colleagues to put an end to all animal transport which takes more than eight hours. unfortunately, he's coming back to that now. unfortunately, typically christian-democratic, economic interests are put before animal welfare. my party is the exact opposite and many dutch and european people agree with us. the debate in the council, it must be said, is even worse. there's not even any ending being made. we must stand our ground, send a clear signal and not give in now, otherwise the council will make an even bigger mess of things. i'm sorry to hear that.

mr president, in all the years that parliament and the council have been jointly adopting legislation, it has not often been the case that a proposal from the european commission has been rejected on the basis of the principle of subsidiarity. i think, and many of my colleagues do, that the proposal now before us - and i am talking about access to justice, the schörling report - must meet that fate. it is extremely strange that the european commission is proposing to transpose the aarhus convention into community law by means of a directive, when all the member states and nine of the ten acceding countries have signed the convention and some have even ratified it. the instrument of a directive to force member states to incorporate the aarhus convention into their national legislation is therefore disproportionate. in addition, this proposal is based on article 175, whereas in fact it is about the harmonization of the legal systems of the member states. that is not possible under article 175. the text of the proposal also differs from the text of the aarhus convention, which will of course cause major problems of interpretation. i am of course in favour of the aarhus convention, but i cannot help but conclude that the member states must implement it themselves. they have committed themselves to this. so the commission's present proposal is both technically incorrect and unnecessary. in conclusion, my group has very strong objections to this proposal. we have therefore tabled an amendment to reject the proposal and i would urge members to support it. the committee on the environment rejected such an amendment by one vote, which was not surprising, because just before the meeting of the committee on the environment it was assumed that my amendment would not be declared admissible. so nobody really prepared for that vote. i think we'll have a chance to do it again tomorrow.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to begin by thanking all those who have contributed constructively to the compromise and the directive, which i hope, and i am almost certain, will be result. why are we satisfied with the compromise reached? because for the first time after decades of debate, a legal title has been found which will ensure that the costs of repairing environmental damage can be recovered from the polluter. the liberal principle that the polluter pays is the guiding principle. as a liberal, i am naturally very happy with that. this is new, it's a cornerstone, a bridge that connects economic and environmental law. i am not entirely satisfied with that, but as i said in my press conference after the compromise was reached with the council, we must regard this as a seed that has now been sown and we hope that it will grow into full-fledged legislation. what's going on? what's going on? i think that because of the many 'powers' in this directive there is still a fairly high chance that a kind of patchwork quilt will emerge in europe and that the member states will put the economy before environmental protection, particularly in economic times of weakness. i therefore regret that my final proposal to use article 95, maximum harmonisation, was not adopted and we have relied on article 195. environmental organizations, environmental ngos, have also played a special role in this. they do not complain that there will be too little harmonisation and they did not support maximum harmonisation during the procedure. i regret that very much. on had to convene a conciliation meeting three times before that. in the end, i think, the compromise was successful. this was also shown by the outcome of the vote in parliament's conciliation - mr president, commissioner, this is not the first time that this parliament has asked for the european development fund to be included in the eu budget. that question has been on the agenda for the last 25 years with regularity. but given the situation we are in, europe cannot and must not let this go. it will not be up to us here in parliament. if the council takes parliament's advice, the european union will be one step closer to a truly democratic and transparent policy. the edf is no longer a budgetary authority. it is also out of date for parliament to have no say in the level of funding, programming or allocations by country, region or sector. we are talking about half the total eu development aid budget! the edf is now almost completely beyond our parliamentary control and this lack of control is far from being compensated by the only power parliament has over the edf, namely the discharge. apart from this democratic deficit, there is a second disadvantage of keeping the edf off the budget, namely the duplications which arise in the annual budgets of the union and the edf. the financing of food aid, humanitarian aid and thematic operations in acp countries is often provided from the community budget. this duplication means that one cash flow of support from the same donor goes to the same recipient on the basis of two different procedures. that is what the council should be convinced of. this is also contrary to the reforms which started a few years ago in the commission's external relations sector. budgeting the edf requires a change in administrative procedures and a change in mentality. i ask the european commission and the commissioner to guarantee that this will be done with care and that the cotonou agreement will remain the framework for the budgeted edf, so that we do not lose the advantages of the present system, such as mutual involvement of the acp countries and europe and the multiannual programming of the budget. however, given the advantages of budgeting the edf, this should not prevent us from seizing this opportunity. in substance, the inclusion of the edf in the community budget means that we as a parliament can really work towards achieving the millennium goals. again, half of our entire budget is in the edf. the acp budgets should stipulate that at least 35% of the resources should go to social services, 20% of which should go to education and health. we must be careful that country and regional specific allocations remain the basis and that any shift in commitments is only made in consultation with the acp countries. finally, i would point out that the principle of coherence must be strictly respected when the edf is included in the budget, so that aid and resources are used to combat poverty. president and commissioner, this commission proposal is ideally suited to making europe's overall effort on behalf of the poorest countries, the acp countries, extraordinarily much more effective, more serious and more democratically controlled. i hope that with our support you will succeed in the council. i expect all countries to see the benefits, which i have listed here again.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this is particularly the case with human rights, freedom of culture and the media, and the limitation of the political role of the army. we are following the negotiations on the future of cyprus with great interest, because they are an essential part of the road to the union. we had initially thought that turkey's participation in these negotiations would be particularly problematic because of the opposition of the army to compromise and because of the senior administrators in the turkish government. unfortunately, we must also note that the greek cypriot side is not cooperating optimally in the search for a solution to the cyprus problem, to put it mildly. of course, that's not the final word. we think that the negotiations will continue, because i cannot imagine that the greek cypriot government will dare to join the european union on such extremely bad terms. i think this government would stick to that for years and years. despite all the changes on paper in turkey and the commitment of the departments to give freedom to the organized society, the implementation of the reforms has proved very difficult. the government itself recognises this and has indicated its seriousness by setting up a monitoring system. i think that is an important position taken by the government, because for us in particular the implementation of all these changes is the essential point. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. the obstacles to european union can hardly come as a surprise. the structure and philosophy of the state are reflected in the mentality of the population and of public servants. if they are thus on a track which is incompatible with the european union, it is not to be expected that this will change in the short term. the changes we are calling for are revolutionary. the main thrust of the report is confirmed by these observations. the european union must be strict with regard to the rule of law and democracy for all candidates, or states that wish to become so. this is first and foremost a matter of principle, but it is also a matter of the ability of the european union to function as such and it is a matter of persuasiveness vis-à-vis our own people, who must also find a basis for accepting this enlargement. the point is that the candidates are in line with the european union in terms of the political values they defend. until that is the case, membership should not be considered. it is not just a question of correcting a number of concrete abuses, but of the legal and social basis from which these abuses arise. it is not just the release of leyla zana, for example, who has been sentenced to many years in prison for conduct for which she has been awarded the sakharov prize by this parliament. the point is that such convictions will be unthinkable in the future. if you ask the highest courts in turkey about this, they, to my surprise, unfortunately still have to answer guilty. the same applies to the cultural rights of minorities. it must be inconceivable that they cannot be exercised because of obstruction. a real separation of church and state should not be a cause for panic, nor should religious freedom for groups not explicitly mentioned in the lausanne convention. it seems that this is a very difficult point for turkey. another very difficult issue is the position of the army. the military, as history shows, enjoys the confidence of a large part of the population, but politics will have to regain its self-confidence. the government is working on changes, but cannot go much further than circumstances allow. the union must nevertheless insist that the military should not have more political influence than is customary in the member states of the european union. i therefore reject amendments which would relax the union's demands on turkey. the fulfilment of the copenhagen political criteria must precede the business as usual of accompanying the adoption of the acquis. that is a lesson from previous enlargement processes and the experience with, for example, romania. it is also a signal to countries that are aspiring to membership. i also believe that the turkish government needs our pressure as a back-up against the forces of opposition in society and in parts of the government. elements of the acquis are non-negotiable. this also applies to values and their application in the european union. these are non-negotiable. they must be embraced and, if one really wants to be a member, simply adopted. on the other hand, i believe that the union should maintain the prospect of full membership for turkey as soon as the political, economic and other criteria are actually met. it is understandable that some amendments call for close involvement of the european parliament. but it is equally necessary that we do our homework to be able to process the accession of countries to the european union. that means that the results of the convention must be accepted, otherwise we cannot function. the principle of deepening and then expanding remains valid. the possibility, mr president, of participating in the good neighbour policy, as expressed in the napolitano report, can also be maintained, but amendments which merely grant turkey a place outside the union do not fit in with my report. the strong emphasis on fulfilling the political criteria is partly related to the desire to find sufficient support among the population. i hope, mr president, that the adoption of the main lines of this report will send a clear signal to the turkish government and society as well as to our own citizens in the union. the subject is one of principle and cannot be tolerated. i suggest that we continue the dialogue with turkey with an open mind. the turkish government is used to that by now. (applause from the left)
we have now reached the point in the debate on turkey where, fortunately, there is a great deal of agreement on the remaining problems. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the present oostlander report contains three minor points which i think should be dealt with. first of all, paragraph 8. the turkish position, as we have seen in recent days in cyprus, is a reflection of the power of the army. colleagues, we cannot be serious about this. what has just happened in cyprus is that the army has endorsed what the government said should be done. that is an example of reform and not an example of stagnation. let's take that part out of the report. that is possible with amendment no 10. secondly, in the light of the events of the past few days, can mr oostlander not seriously believe that the greek cypriots have shown that they are seriously committed to the annan plan? they will have to show that in the coming weeks. so i think that needs to change, too. finally, the third dirt. i would be in favour of lowering the voting threshold in the elections and have tabled an amendment to that effect. this is a wish of parliament which we must pass on to the turks, because i believe it is better that the turkish parliament should be more pluralist than it is now, with a 10% threshold. the major problem with the oostlander report, however, is in paragraph 7. the rapporteur has acknowledged, or perhaps should have acknowledged, that it is inevitable that if the current reforms continue, the member states will decide in december to open negotiations. what does paragraph 7 say about that? the interpretation of mr oostlander is that negotiations are inevitable, but we are not really going to negotiate. we are not going to negotiate the 31 chapters of the acquis, as we have done with all the other applicant countries. what is proposed in paragraph 7 is that we first complete all the political criteria. that sounds good, but what does that mean in practice? an example. the same paragraph says that a new constitution must be written. that is what mr oostlander is for and that is what i am for. if done in a decent way, it will take, i think, two to three years. the european union is doing this. another example. mr oostlander has repeatedly said that what we want, namely a change of mentality, is a long-term process. does he really think that this has to be all done before we can deal with the chapters? you can't be serious. does parliament, and does the socialist group, for example, really think that we should negotiate with turkey with an arm tied behind our back because we are not yet allowed to discuss 31 chapters of the acquis? my view, which is expressed in amendment no 9, is different. i'm in favor of doing two things after december. firstly, to continue to stress the importance of political criteria, with a special monitoring system if necessary, because that is important and will remain important, but at the same time to start the so-called normal negotiations. i do not think we can say to turkey in december that we are going to negotiate and follow mr oostlander's interpretation. that interpretation is now in paragraph 7 and it therefore makes it possible for us to, let me put it this way, first do only groundwork for years and then really negotiate. paragraph 7 is now a vague, i would almost say diplomatic, compromise that covers a lot. the alternative is a clear but nuanced position of parliament, and that is what turkey is entitled to.
mr president, in line with his previous report, mr oostlander is again presenting a very clear report to this house today on turkey's very steep path towards the european union. the rapporteur rightly notes in paragraph 32 that the country still interprets the secular state differently from the way it is used in the european union. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the public authorities. i would like to strengthen the word 'state control' with the word 'promotion', because under the auspices of the presidency for religious affairs, which reports directly to prime minister erdogan, the turkish republic provides the sunni mainstream of islam with ample opportunities for social development. try the christian churches in turkey. to this day, although they are covered by the provisions of the lausanne convention of 1923, they must be deprived of the essential status of legal personality. in short, commissioner, there is work to be done, but i had already heard that from you. i also took note of paragraph 10 of the report and agreed with it. in this report, mr oostlander is advocating the independence and quality of higher education and scientific institutions in turkey. a very topical plea, given the persistent attempts of the reformist erdogan himself to subordinate the country's academic level to the, i quote, "islamist conservative political ideology of the cabinet". this urgent warning, from a member of the turkish academy of sciences about a month ago, was published in the frankfurter allgemeine. another signal to brussels of the real state of affairs in ankara.
turkey is making a great effort to meet the political criteria and has already taken various reform measures. the erdogan government is doing its best. that is a positive development, but there is still a great deal to be done if the negotiations are to be opened after december. torture and the restriction of various civil liberties remain a problem and the gap between paper and practice remains too wide. this problem is not confined to us in the ivory towers of brussels and strasbourg. ordinary europeans are also concerned and we must therefore be quite sure that we are going to make the right decision. i am therefore very surprised that mr lagendijk says that he thinks that turkey should negotiate with an arm behind its back if the political criteria are not first to be met and that this can also be done in parallel with the negotiations on the acquis. but that's the world upside down. it is as if we were doing something distasteful when we ask an acceding country to first meet our fundamental values. on that point too, an agreement must be an agreement. turkey must be judged fairly in the future on the basis of the political criteria of copenhagen. i have heard commissioner verheugen use the word zero tolerance and that appeals to me. the turks have a right to it and the people of the european union have a right to it. in short, the fixing of a date for negotiations in december is not a fait accompli.
the european parliament has also called for the establishment of a european union-wide network of information centres for the protection of the environment. now that prime minister sharon himself is calling for a withdrawal from the occupied territories and the removal of settlements, the eu should support a new policy in this direction. a boycott of israel would further reduce europe's already very limited influence in the country and would constitute a serious obstacle to communication. the text of paragraph 40 is also unbalanced in mr van den bos's view, since it does not condemn the terrorist attack on ashdod, whereas the killing of the hamas leader has been condemned.
in the case of kosovo, the situation is not the same as in the case of the former yugoslavia. the people of kosovo remember the serbs in particular for their soldiers, police, tax collectors, prison guards, unfriendly officials and unwelcome rulers. they do not believe that the remaining 5% of serbs will acquire the albanian language and adapt to it. this minority is suspected of actually wanting to return to their former privileged position. their position is similar to that of the turks in greece and bulgaria, the hungarians in slovakia and romania and the russians in the baltic states, except that the memories of kosovo are more recent and marked by greater bitterness. the area designated as the bilingual autonomous province of kosovo-metohija within serbia after world war ii has in practice been divided into a northern serbian part - with a few scattered serb villages under military surveillance - and an otherwise purely albanian part. the few contacts between serbs and albanians result in violent conflicts. we should now work towards equality between peoples and good neighbourliness. i expect that the albanian intolerance towards the serbs, turks and roma - which is very regrettable - will disappear as soon as they are sure that they can take over the running of the country and will not be subjected to another power again.
the european parliament has been a very active participant in the process of european integration. moreover, turkish emigrants and their descendants have formed a significant population in western europe over the last 40 years. it has made the question of whether turkey belongs to europe or whether it would be more appropriate to sign association agreements with the middle east, central asia or iran ever more theoretical. the forces which want to keep turkey out of the eu on the grounds that it is not geographically located in europe and is not based on the judeo-christian traditions have long since lost the debate. but that does not mean that the other extreme is necessarily right. in its present form, turkey does indeed fit into the authoritarian europe of the first half of the 20th century, but not yet into a union of parliamentary democracies. this will only happen when the country stops banning political parties, abolishes an electoral system aimed at exclusion, stops taking political prisoners and abolishes press censorship, the suppression of regional languages and the political influence of the army. it is not enough to change the legislation if it does not lead to changes in practice. anyone who wants turkish membership for military, economic or idealistic reasons should be aware of the negative consequences of a hasty accession. it will not benefit democracy, human rights, peace or the environment in the eu.
the rapporteur. mr president, i must say that as rapporteur i was extremely surprised by the 350 amendments in the plenary, whereas we had a very good debate in committee, with far fewer amendments than there are now. nevertheless, i have looked at the amendments and we will look tonight in the committee concerned at what we can do with all those amendments and make sure that there is an opinion for parliament. i am, however, extremely surprised that we now have to do in plenary what has not been done in the committee concerned. i would also point out that there is europe-wide support for the content of this report. if you think that there are parts of it which are not in accordance with the rules of procedure, i would like to hear that from you, because then we might be able to withdraw those parts. it is an excellent report, at least that is what friends and foes in the media say. so i do not see why we should now withdraw it for these kinds of reasons. i'd like to hear that, too.
the rapporteur. mr president, i must say that i am not very satisfied with the commissioner's remark that there is no breach of regulation 95/46. the agreement itself states, in paragraph 4, that all data of european passengers will be processed in accordance with the constitutional requirements of the united states. one of the laws in the united states that should apply is the privacy act, but this law does not apply to persons from third countries, to give just one example. article 6 of the agreement states that there will be reciprocity to the extent feasible and on the basis of strict reciprocity. i do not want to debate this now, but i can easily give you ten more examples of things that are not true and that i believe are in violation of our privacy laws. that is why we should bring this matter before the court. the commissioner said in the previous debate on the transfer to third parties: "we are still negotiating with the united states". i heard that right, the last time and again. it is really beyond words that i, as rapporteur, had to present a report on adequacy finding, when the commission itself still considered that the whole thing was not adequate. i would like to bring that up in the debate. i thank all the members who spoke before the court. i think the question to the court of justice whether we can conclude international agreements which exclude both the european parliament and the american congress is extremely interesting. i have understood from consultations with members of congress that if a similar agreement were to be presented to the us congress, it would never, if ever, pass. let us show that we as a european parliament know how to stand up for our citizens and not be pressured by anyone. we're the only ones who can do this. i hope that parliament will follow the legal committee in this.
mr president, for more than a year and a half this parliament has been working on the european commission's proposal to harmonize the provisions on consumer credit. we believe that this is far too long, also because the advisory committee's views were already outlined before the summer of 2003. it seems that the european commission, in proposing maximum harmonization, has misjudged the political feasibility. my group, on the other hand, can agree to minimum harmonization of the provisions on consumer credit. in this way, the rights acquired in the member states are preserved. one of the acquired rights in my country, the netherlands, is that consumers without extensive contracts with a bank may temporarily have a debt on their current account. we call that "red" standing. it is rightly proposed that article 3 (2) (c) be deleted by means of amendment no 52. i would like to hear from the commissioner whether he accepts this amendment. can the existing practice of overdraft be maintained under the new directive without additional administrative action? secondly, i would like to draw attention to the european commission's proposal on unfair trading practices. article 4 of the directive states that a trader is only required to comply with the requirements of his home country. from the consumer's point of view, this is incomprehensible. a consumer wants the protection he enjoys in his own country. this article is a major obstacle to effective consumer protection. a trader simply has to comply with the requirements of the country where he markets his products. our group has therefore tabled amendment no 95 to solve this problem. i hope that the members will support this. i would like to hear from the commissioner whether this amendment can also be accepted.
first of all, mr president, i would like to express my appreciation for mr sturdy, for his work and for his willingness to compromise. there are wide differences in the permitted residues of plant protection products between member states. that is why it is a good idea to set maximum residue limits. but we are very concerned about the amendments to the text. the commission proposal is too little concerned with consumer protection and too much with the protection of agricultural interests. i would like to draw your attention to three points. firstly, maximum residue levels should be set at the lowest possible level. public health and best agricultural practices must be the guiding principles. non-chemical controls should be given priority over chemical controls. i would like to record that now. second, children are especially vulnerable because their livers are not yet fully developed. research by the emma children's hospital in amsterdam has shown that certain residues can seriously retard the development of children. it is therefore of the utmost importance that children be adequately protected. i therefore ask you all to support amendment no 67. thirdly, clear and strict regulation would eliminate the current distortions of competition in the authorization of the use of plant protection products. that would be a good thing.
