the rapporteur. madam president, i would like to begin by thanking my fellow shadow rapporteurs: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. thank you for your unity and support. i thank the irish presidency for its constructive approach to the negotiations. thank you also for the european commission. you've been very cooperative, even though you're not formally required to do so. i would like to thank commissioner wallström. we have reached an agreement at first reading. i will deal with nine points. i think the result is good. point one. this linkage directive will enter into force as early as 2005. companies can start now, they get legal certainty now. point two. this linking directive is independent of the entry into force of the kyoto protocol. the emissions trading act is also independent of the kyoto protocol. for us in europe, kyoto is now starting to work. we are not going to wait for russia to ratify kyoto. one hundred and twenty countries, two-thirds of the world's population, have already ratified kyoto. point three. energy companies and large corporations can now earn emission allowances by investing in the conversion of chinese coal-fired power plants to gas, in making coal-fired power plants more efficient, in solar power plants in indonesia, in wind farms off the coasts of developing countries or in capturing waste gas from landfills. there are many, many more ways to reduce co2 and other greenhouse gases. point four. as in the kyoto protocol, we have decided that the construction of nuclear power stations is not allowed. point five. carbon sinks are out of the question, because in the eyes of the european parliament, growing trees is only a temporary solution. trees blowing over or burning down. even when trees die a natural death, they give up all the carbon they have taken in. on the other hand, biomass or biofuels are possible. governments can also gain experience with the sinks. point number six. large hydropower plants can only be built if the criteria of the world commission on dams are respected. it is true that hydropower makes a real contribution to combating climate change, but ecological and social criteria must be taken into account. we are also more stringent than the kyoto protocol on this point. point 7 of the report. the agreement that member states should do half of their kyoto efforts at home and half abroad has been reaffirmed. agreements to limit companies to the ceiling alone are pointless, because that will lead to governments, at least some governments, putting 50% and their businesses also putting another 25% of their investments abroad. then the end of the story is that only 25 percent is invested in one's own country and 75 percent outside. we would be putting our ecological credibility at risk. point 8 of the report. there is a great deal of interest outside europe in our emissions trading system and in this linkage directive. fifteen states in the united states and four provinces in australia are planning to set up similar emissions trading schemes. the european commission will examine whether they can be integrated into our european system. the same applies to canada and hopefully in the future to japan. in this way, the implementation of the kyoto protocol will begin before the kyoto protocol officially enters into force. point 9 of the report. we will have to extend the scope of the emissions trading system to aviation and later to the road sector. this is better than the path of domestic ji projects, as some have suggested. finally, the french government and the french industry do not like this linkage directive. neither does greenpeace. that's old school dogma. i am glad that all the political groups, the council of ministers and the european commission have a better understanding of this and want to support this compromise. climate change is getting worse every day, and we need to act together. for climate change, it doesn't matter where the greenhouse gas is produced. with this linking directive in hand, we are now taking the fight to solve the climate problem on a global scale.
madam president, last year our colleague at the time, mr jorge moreira da silva, reached an agreement with the council on trade in greenhouse gases. this was a difficult task, since the agreements already made with industry in a number of countries to reduce greenhouse gases had to be incorporated into european policy. today, tomorrow after the vote, i believe a new agreement has been reached and new agreements have been made to extend emissions trading to third countries. i am proud that we, as the european union, are showing that we mean what we say about kyoto and that we are not only making commitments but also keeping them. we in europe have pursued a defensive policy on the environment for too long. with the new cdm instrument and the joint implementation, we can take the lead globally again. with the development of clean technology that is exported, we are developing a new market for cleaner production processes. our european business community can benefit from this. but i expect that the transfer of environmentally friendly techniques will also help the developing countries. it helps them to introduce sustainability and it will also bring them more prosperity. the trade projects that can now take place with developed countries or with countries in transition to a market economy will increase the market on which emissions trading will operate and thus also reduce the cost per tonne of greenhouse gas reduced. high environmental performance at the lowest possible cost. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the environment. since we have now established at european level that joint implementation and the cdm are too complementary, we must be satisfied with the revision that has been agreed. what matters now is that the corporate world takes what's there. alexander the red did his best. i congratulate him on that. he's made good negotiations possible. thank you for that.
commission. madam president, i wish to begin by thanking the european parliament, the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy and in particular the rapporteur, mr blokland, for his report. the commission proposal for a new battery directive seeks to extend the scope of the existing community legislation on batteries and accumulators containing certain dangerous substances to all batteries and accumulators placed on the community market. today the existing legislation covers only 7% of all the portable batteries sold each year in the european union. this has hindered the setting up of efficient national collection and recycling schemes. consumers have also been confused as to what is to be collected, and what not, and have therefore not participated effectively in the national collection schemes. consequently, today many batteries still end up in the environment. in 2002, for example, approximately 45% of the total volume of portable batteries sold in the 15-member eu was landfilled or incinerated. the current proposal aims to achieve a high level of environmental protection while at the same time contributing to proper functioning of the internal market for batteries. in this respect the commission's proposal has two main goals: creating a closed loop system for all batteries and accumulators, and introducing a producer responsibility scheme. the proposed closed loop system requires member states to take all necessary measures to prevent final disposal of spent batteries and achieve a high level of battery collection and recycling. i am confident that the proposed collection targets for portable batteries, in combination with the proposed high recycling targets, will protect our environment against the risks involved in the disposal of those batteries. moreover, the proposal addresses the issue of the uncontrolled disposal of hazardous batteries containing mercury, cadmium or lead. in this respect, in addition to the existing restrictions on the use of those substances, established by the end-of-life vehicles directive, the following additional measures would be introduced: a ban on landfilling and incineration of all industrial and automotive batteries, which covers most batteries containing lead or cadmium, and an additional collection target for portable nickel-cadmium batteries, which requires monitoring of the waste stream to determine the volume of discarded portable nicad batteries. the extended impact assessment has identified the proposed policy options as the most sustainable from an economic, social and environmental viewpoint. i turn now to the introduction of a producer responsibility scheme. the application of the producer responsibility principle is an important step towards incorporating the external environmental costs into the production phase and, at the same time, towards encouraging producers to market more environmentally friendly products. all batteries and accumulators should be diverted from the waste stream and recycled in order to avoid environmental damage and recover the various metals they contain. to achieve this goal, we need to create a closed-loop system and make battery producers responsible for the products they make.
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, i should like to explain my report on batteries and accumulators. after much insistence on the part of the european parliament, there is now at last a proposal from the european commission. the commission proposal has a number of positive points. first, all batteries are covered by the directive. secondly, binding collection objectives. thirdly, binding recycling rates and recycling efficiencies and fourthly, financing mechanisms in which producer responsibility plays a major role. we have had a very long debate on batteries in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection. the main subjects discussed were the prevention of heavy metals and collection targets. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has reached agreement with all the groups on most of the points. even on the collection targets for the batteries we have reached a broadly supported compromise. as the sales of batteries vary greatly from one member state to another, especially after 1 may, it was decided to propose a percentage of sales as the collection target. the prevention of heavy metals has been developing for many years. eu legislation now prohibits the heavy metals mercury, lead and cadmium in materials and parts of vehicles placed on the market after 1 july 2003 and in new electrical and electronic equipment placed on the market after 1 july 2006. the main problem with batteries and accumulators is the presence of the heavy metals mercury, lead and cadmium. the explanatory memorandum to the european commission's proposal, on pages 9 to 12, clearly describes the harmful effects of the heavy metals mercury, cadmium and lead on the environment and health. for this reason, most mercury batteries were already banned in 1998. the concepts of the revised battery directive of the european commission have always been based on a ban on nickel-cadmium batteries. the commission's current proposal, strangely enough, does not call for a ban on nickel-cadmium batteries but for a so-called closed loop for cadmium. however, it appears that there is no closed loop, as only 80% of the portable nickel-cadmium batteries have to be collected. so 20 percent of portable nickel-cadmium batteries end up in landfills or in incinerators. since cadmium is a volatile metal, it is irreversibly released into the air during waste incineration. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has therefore adopted a ban on the heavy metals mercury, cadmium and lead. in addition, we have included an extensive list of exceptions for cases where no suitable substitutes are yet available. this includes batteries for hearing aids, emergency lighting, industrial batteries, lead-acid batteries and wireless tools. although i personally do not think the last exception is necessary, i have added it to meet the objections of some colleagues. the replacement of nickel-cadmium batteries, namely nickel-metal hydride batteries, operates at a temperature of minus 20° celsius to plus 60° celsius. i can therefore assume that these batteries are usable throughout the european union. finally, madam president, i would like to respond to a comic strip by the european portable battery association which falsely suggests that all kinds of applications could no longer be used if the report of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection were adopted. i have a four-and-a-half-year-old cell phone. this old phone has a lithium-ion battery. i'm pretty sure all your cell phones have a nickel-metal hydride or lithium-ion battery. in short, if nickel-cadmium batteries are banned for these applications, there is no problem. another example from the comic is the lead batteries in cars. these lead-acid batteries are not covered by the ban in the car wreckage directive. they are also not covered by the ban under the battery directive, because, as you have just heard, they are included in the list of exceptions. the same is true of all the button cells in hearing aids and watches, wireless tools, emergency lighting and all industrial applications. even the european consumer organisation beuc is in favour of a ban on nickel-cadmium batteries for consumer applications. as you know, the alternatives are wide open. i have, however, encountered opposition from the industry to the fact that they do not want a ban on principle. this goes so far that it is still opposed, even though all the exceptions requested have been honoured. i believe that such arguments should not be a factor in decision-making. i suspect a dangerous tactic behind that. it is noted that the industry, which is so strongly against a ban on nickel-cadmium batteries, promotes the alternatives to them on its own website. let us therefore promote these alternatives too, by removing as much as possible of the harmful heavy metals from batteries.
madam president, cadmium is extremely toxic. in the framework directive on water, we, that is to say the council, parliament and the commission, decided to phase out cadmium. why don't we do this here? the commission proposes a closed loop system, but the rapporteur has already pointed out that only 80% is recovered, which means that 20% inevitably disappears into the environment. the commission proposal has another problem, namely the removal of cadmium from the waste stream. a very small amount of cadmium from a very large waste stream. that is a very inefficient way of working. it is therefore better to work towards a ban and then allow a number of exceptions. lithium-ion and nickel-hybrid batteries are already on the market. in the future, there will also be fuel cells. so the alternative is there. in fact, the alternative's market share is already growing. it is therefore a great pity that the commission has succumbed to the pressure of a section of industry. european policy must reward the leaders in industry and not the laggards, as the commission does. unfortunately, the christian democrats and the commission are doing exactly that, rewarding those who fall behind. i hope that the majority of this house will be wiser.

to replace the rapporteur. mr president, it all started in november 2001. the prestige sank, and tens of thousands of tons of oil spilled into the sea off the coasts of galicia, spain, and france. even the british and belgian coasts were stained with traces of crude oil. it was a real european drama and yet another drama we were not expecting. the european parliament appointed dirk sterckx as rapporteur, but because of circumstances in his own country he asked me to replace him. so i'll be taking his place on behalf of the liberals and say a few words. in the european parliament, there is great disappointment, because after the erika disaster we had already worked constructively with the commission to tighten up the policy. the results were two erika packages, but still, another disaster had to happen first. in september 2003, the european parliament adopted a first report by mr sterckx. this report dealt in detail with what had happened and made a number of recommendations. the main points of the report were: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the temporary committee for the improvement of safety at sea (mare) was set up in november 2003 and its mandate was first to investigate recent shipping accidents, especially with the prestige and the erika; secondly, to analyse the social and economic consequences; thirdly, to evaluate existing european and international rules; fourthly, to examine how the recommendations of the first resolution have been implemented. finally, if necessary, additional proposals will be made. we have now completed six months of hard work and a balanced report. it has not always been easy to look at the situation rationally, but fortunately everyone has worked well together and has been constructive so far, so that the adoption of this report will hopefully be a serious step towards safer seas, because that has been the focus of the work of the last six months. shipping safety is in everyone's interest, because no one should think that another prestige or an erika will go down any shore. on behalf of mr sterckx, i would like to thank mr jarzembowski, chairman of the mare committee, the people who spoke to us - including the captain of the prestige, who received a delegation from the committee - all the coordinators and all the colleagues. mr sterckx regrets that he cannot be there because of local problems. now the report. step one, clearing the wreckage. according to the spanish authorities, 43 000 tonnes of crude oil have been recovered. repsol, the company that will extract the remaining oil from the wreck, says there's still 14,000 tons in the wreck. so, as the report says, there are still 20,000 tonnes unaccounted for. so these 20,000 tons are still a threat. the cleaned oil is stored somewhere, but it still needs to be processed. secondly, the meeting with the captain exposes a contradiction between what the spanish authorities say, that 10,000 tonnes of oil had been released into the sea at the very beginning, and the statement by the captain that only 2,000 to 3,000 tonnes were lost on the first day through one of the two openings. and by the way, the way the captain is being treated doesn't really encourage people to go into the maritime profession. it is a real task, and it is very difficult when you are personally put under pressure in this way. and so do a lot of the men this captain works with. the prestige is not alone in this, as witness what has happened to other shipwrecks involving other crew members. there's still no report on the prestige of the flag state, the bahamas. that is unfortunate and, in fact, quite inappropriate, considering what we have all done to contribute to the work. i have a few more proposals i'd like to say something about. first, compliance with existing law remains a priority. we must first and foremost apply what already exists. we are pleased to note that the member states have made up for the delays in the directive on port state control and classification agencies. that is why we propose to update the text of paragraph 7 and to reject the second part, since this question seems to have been resolved. the european coast guard has been the subject of a good compromise in the committee. i have found that the last part of the compromise is confusing and contrary to our request for an independent decision-making and command structure at the level of the member states, which can impose the necessary decisions. the visit of the briton robert middleton to our committee was very instructive. i therefore propose that the contradiction in amendment no 26 be removed and that paragraph 13 be brought into line with paragraph 10. then there is the matter of labelling and tracing containers with dangerous cargo. this point is included in the report, but i think a quick scan from the commission is needed to indicate what level of transmitters we should set up. possibly passive, but possibly active, heavy-duty transmitters. i would ask mrs palacio to look at that again. there are very simple, inexpensive transmitters that can be attached to any load. we can introduce it the day after tomorrow, the market is ready. i think it's important to have this kind of thing well-ordered. i look forward to your answer.
president. - i also congratulate mr sterckx on his excellent report. it contains many good recommendations which will hopefully be implemented so that we will be even better able to prevent what we have experienced a number of times in the recent period, namely unacceptable accidents with oil tankers. it is particularly important now to carry out what has been agreed. in particular, by investing much more in monitoring existing measures. we must invest in global cooperation to tackle international maritime security problems outside the european union. consultation with and action against flag states which do not take such stringent safety measures is also of great importance. i think we all want so much to happen in the years to come that we never have to have a debate like this again in parliament and that the risk of accidents of this kind will be reduced considerably, partly because of what we are doing as the european union. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the report also contains many proposals on this point. we must learn from each other and we must try to follow the same procedures. the experience of and the discussion with the english coordinator have just been mentioned. it was found that the united kingdom has a very clear command line in the event of accidents. that too must be regulated at european level. we therefore support the proposal to establish a european coastguard. that's a fair goal. it is important, however, that we first look at how these can be set up through research, projects and experiments. we are strongly in favour of giving emsa, this new body, an important role in the further development of policy in this area, including controls and the like.
mr president, commissioner, as other members have already said, we can be satisfied with the work done by the rapporteur. i would like to take this opportunity to congratulate mr sterckx on his work i am glad, mr president, that the rapporteur has managed to find good compromises in this parliament after a very long period. thank you for that. this proposal to price heavy goods transport is an improvement on the current system. it gives member states more flexibility as regards the nature of their systems. the rates can be adjusted to the time, day, weight and euro emissions. it therefore makes the tariffs more flexible and more market-oriented than under the old regime, and that is profit. i also think it is important that the general conditions for the pricing of heavy goods vehicles be regulated at european level. this is a systematic matter. the question is how a toll should be levied and arranged by a member state. it is important not to allow unnecessary new tariffs for heavy goods traffic to have a disruptive effect, which would lead to a race from the top down, as a serious consequence of a normal logistical flow of goods. we don't want to make it a competition. the proposal that is now before us is not yet fully completed. i myself welcome amendment no 50 by mr simpson and mr swoboda. i too am concerned about the current lack of clarity on how to internalize the external costs, environmental costs, social costs and accident costs. this should be done on the basis of generally accepted calculation methods. it is good that the commission is coming back with this in two years' time to indicate how we are going to shape it. finally, i am pleased that every reference to passenger transport has been removed from this report. it is simply too far to make agreements at european level at the moment. that is why passenger transport, often by car, is so large and why in very important member states this volume problem - car traffic often means local traffic for them - and local pricing can be tackled. it is particularly important, however, to ensure that there is an alternative.
president, commissioner, children learn rules, but they try to go over the edge again and again. corrections must be made to prevent worse. the same is true of adults. something pushes them to the limits of what is permissible, and often a correction is needed to keep them on the right path. in road transport, the need to compete in order to compete can be gained by sharing a breach of the other. i am convinced that we can reduce such situations by adequate controls on legislation, so that infringement does not pay off. in his report, mr markov makes good proposals to tighten up these checks. however, there are also less meaningful comments and some things are missing. the good points concern the paragraphs on equal interpretation and application of existing schemes and the aim of non-discrimination of the carriers to be checked. the inclusion of the driver's licence in the checks is a useful addition. however, in our view, the comments on so-called 'serious' infringements in their present form are not appropriate. the limited margin of error allows everyday traffic situations to offend drivers and businesses alike, with no intention whatsoever. only repeated infringements should constitute a serious infringement. the same applies to the inclusion of the working time directive in the scope of the checks. national freedoms make it difficult to achieve a clear enforcement at the moment. this creates a lot of confusion and annoyance. it is advisable to agree on a common interpretation of the provisions before incorporating them. a crucial gap at the moment is the position of the auditors. in some member states they have not been given the powers necessary to carry out their assigned tasks satisfactorily. that's gonna have to change. president. - despite these reservations, i should like to thank mr markov for the good report. i hope that the honourable member will reconsider my remarks.
in the netherlands, the dutch media have given the impression that mr doorn, who is the interim leader of the cda party in the netherlands, has found a way to save 10 billion euros in the netherlands. the superficial reader thinks he's going to use this money to lower taxes for the eu. mr doorn does not provide any solution to the problem of the eu wasting 20% of its budget every year on prestige projects, on pumping money between the rich member states and on fraud. he is concerned only with freeing business from what he considers a stifling bureaucracy. he also called for the eu to be more active in the fight against terrorism and for the eu to be more active in the fight against terrorism. this is in line with the efforts of the dutch minister of finance, mr zalms, to reduce the administrative burden on businesses from eur 17 billion to eur 14 billion a year. he believes that half of these are due to european rules. mr zalms' calculations show that this is not 10 billion, but only 1.5 billion. i am in favour of extending the possibilities for assessing the negative consequences of european legislation at the earliest possible stage, but i think mr doorn wants to prevent rules which are quite different from mine.
in the case of the commission, the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. before a public debate is possible, serious differences of opinion are already cleared up. in this case, the business community is satisfied that co2 emissions trading can start as early as 2005 instead of 2008 thanks to the energetic efforts of the green rapporteur. it is in line with the liberal view that reducing emissions is too much for animals at home and can therefore be done better in countries where it is cheaper. the expected benefits of this are faster achievement of results worldwide and an incentive for developing countries to use environmental technology developed in europe immediately in their industrialization. the disadvantages are that no action is taken at home, that eastern european countries are selling their rights to rebuild their industry and that third world countries may have to abandon industrialization permanently. however, it is positive that at least 50% of the reduction must be achieved within the eu. the advantage of this proposal is that production forests which are intended to be felled or which can be burned can no longer be included, through fraud, in the list of carbon sources which remove carbon dioxide from the air and convert it into oxygen.
the european parliament has been a party to the treaty of rome for many years. in the meantime, such a constitution is being prepared, which will permanently establish genuine free competition, ever-increasing armaments and the undemocratic power of the council. this constitution and article 7 of the current eu treaty allow for measures against member states which cease to be democracies and which seriously and persistently violate the rule of law. it is important for the undemocratic spain of the aznar government, which lost the elections, and for the possible future member states romania and turkey. neither these provisions nor the commission's communication on them make it clear whether they also apply to serious failure to act in respect of human rights violations. i appreciate that in his proposal mr voggenhuber will interpret it on the basis of openness, equality, plurality of opinions and confidence that the union will not exercise permanent supervision over the member states. i also agree with him that the european parliament must play an important role in suspensions and that this cannot be left to the council's power politics. what bothers me, however, is that his proposals are laced with idealism. unfortunately, the real values of the european union are the market, the currency, free competition and armaments, rather than democracy, human rights, the environment, peace and solidarity.
the commission has already submitted a proposal for a regulation to the council. president, the committee on budgetary control has made the following comments on the commission's proposals in general terms. firstly, we would like budgets to be more realistic in the future than they have been in the past. each year we have seen large surpluses. there is nothing wrong with that in itself, but why should it be put in the budget first? the second point, which is very important to us, is that some 80 to 85 per cent of all the money is spent by the member states. as an additional check, would it not be possible to have each member state issue a declaration each year stating that the funds have been well spent? if it is wrong afterwards, the commission is legally stronger to take action against the member state concerned. national declarations of good conduct, then. the third point is that we would like, but this is well known, that once in many years there should be a positive das. it is not the case that on the eve of the elections the commission is still unable, after nine years, to obtain a positive declaration of expenditure from the european court of auditors.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. mr president, we in the committee on employment and social affairs are very pleased with the report by mr garriga polledo. he has the most difficult reporting role we have because it is during the period when parliament is temporarily absent. at such times there are always various forces, particularly in the council, who try to take up the subject, but i think it is up to mr garriga polledo to resist these attacks. we had three main priorities from our committee. one of them is fully covered in the report, and that is lisbon. lisbon is the key to better competitiveness, better employment and a better economy. we must emphasize this even more than in the past. the social partners must also be mentioned if lisbon is to succeed. it is a very small sum, but it is very important. it would be good if that were somehow still to be dealt with in the resolution. another point is safety and health, and this will become very important in the coming period, especially with regard to the new member states.

rapporteur. – mr president, since i am leaving parliament this is my last speech and i would like to take this occasion to thank everyone who has helped me here over the past five years, in particular my own staff, my assistants and the secretariat of the committee on budgetary control. i would like to congratulate the secretary-general on the result that we are hopefully going to deliver tomorrow, which would be a positive discharge for the european parliament. the cooperation has been excellent and the secretary-general, together with all his staff, has done a very good job of running the parliament over the last five years in the face of mounting challenges, not least of which is enlargement. the criticism contained in my report is not aimed at parliament's staff or the secretary-general. my criticism is aimed at us, the politicians, who give political direction to parliament. in 1999, i was elected in the netherlands with a very low turnout of just 29%. the main reason why people did not go and vote in the european elections was that they did not have faith in the eu institutions. this is bad in itself, but it is also a great danger to european integration because if people do not have faith in the institutions it is very unlikely that they will support further steps in european integration. my mission for the last five years has been to try to reform the institutions to make them more legitimate and credible in the eyes of our citizens. we have come a long way, in particular with regard to the european commission. here i would like to pay tribute to commissioner schreyer and her colleagues who have done a great job over the last five years, again in the face of very difficult challenges. parliament has made progress. people who say that this institution is just a 'gravy train' are wrong. we have made excellent progress in modernising the institution, in modernising the administration and even the rules governing members. but two big eyesores remain. one is our system of expenses, which is still not based on the actual costs incurred. this system is indefensible to the outside world. no matter how big the differences in salary between meps from different countries, and no matter how unfair that is – especially when the new member states come in – we cannot continue to justify this system. the vote tomorrow will be an opportunity for this house to show that we are in touch with the real world, that we are prepared to put our own house in order and to clean up our act. the second question concerns strasbourg. this is a beautiful assembly and an even more beautiful city, but we should not meet here for only four days per week. the commission and the council are based in brussels. if we are to be a credible democratic assembly, defending the interests of the citizens of europe, then we should be where the other powerful institutions are – with the council and the commission. it was natural to come to strasbourg when this parliament was still a part-time parliament, when the memories of the second world war were still fresh and we needed a symbol of reconciliation. but today europe's citizens are not looking for signs of reconciliation between france and germany. i do not believe that anyone doubts those two countries get on together – perhaps a little too well at times! the citizens are looking for efficient, modern institutions that are capable of managing european integration and the policies we have agreed together. without a doubt, the time is right for a move to brussels. clearly we must find a purpose for strasbourg. it has been built on european institutions and we must give them something in return. we have suggested the idea of the first european university. the buildings would be perfect for that. another suggestion is to let the european council meet here rather than in brussels. these are the key issues that we need to address. i hope that tomorrow we will also give president cox a mandate to go to the intergovernmental conference and say that we need to change the treaty in this respect. we need to give parliament the right to decide its own seat. today a number of meps held a press conference organised by the campaign for parliamentary reform. we signed a reform pledge which commits the signatories to the reform of our expenses system and to work for a single seat for parliament in brussels. i hope that when we vote tomorrow we will all sign this pledge by voting for the amendments that have been tabled by a number of groups. i am grateful to them for having done so.
president. - since there is so little time, two very brief questions. firstly, the reservations of the british and dutch governments have been mentioned. i know the persuasiveness of mr patten and mr verheugen, so i assume they are also listened to in the hague and london. can i still assume that there will be no negotiations with croatia until the stabilisation and association agreement has been ratified? i would like to put this question to the commission just to be sure. secondly, i am glad that the lead prosecutor, mrs del ponte, is positive now, but i think it is important that she remains positive in the future. is the commission prepared to recommend that this very specific tribunal, the lead prosecutor herself, should continue to be involved in monitoring croatia's cooperation with this tribunal in the future?
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i should like to thank her in advance for the openness with which she has met this parliament over the years. as regards the reporting for the agencies, which i am responsible for, i can only say that, on the basis of the information i have at present, there is no reason to refuse to grant discharge to the agencies. of course, this does not mean that there is nothing to improve. we have noted a number of issues in the agencies over the past year which may need more attention. firstly, it is their personnel policy. we want more transparency in this area in general. how are people recruited and how do they get promoted? there are indications that this is different from the commission, whereas these people, once they are officials in an agency, have the same rights and duties as commission officials. we think that this should be rectified as much as possible. we also believe that olaf has every right to intervene in any problems that may arise in agencies. finally, we think that the commission's internal audit service should pay more attention to what is happening in the agencies. so far, this department has had neither the time nor the inclination to intervene. we think that this must happen in the future. some agencies have their own revenue and there is a problem with carryovers from one financial year to another. agencies may have their own revenue but, according to the rules of the financial regulation, they may not use it in the following financial year. for one agency, the london agency, a solution has been found. we believe that this solution should also apply to the other agencies. in addition, we would generally encourage agencies to generate more of their own revenue where possible, so that it is cheaper for the budget. i would also like to say something about the other agencies, but in a personal capacity and therefore no longer as rapporteur. we support mr bayona's decision to grant discharge to the commission. i can almost repeat the comments made last year and the years before that. it remains extremely unsatisfactory that there is no positive declaration of reliability by the court of auditors. everything must be done to obtain it. we can only conclude that if we were to refuse to grant discharge at this time, it would not serve any good purpose, because the political consequences of it are not in the interests of europe at the moment. i would also like to say a few words about the eurostat affair. there has already been a statement from the commission this afternoon. as far as i am concerned, that explanation is not sufficient. i have not heard anything in this statement from the commission about the essence of political responsibility. we heard mr prodi say when he became president of the commission that there would be political responsibility. we think that the commission is not giving enough substance to this. a commissioner is responsible for the task assigned to him and must be accountable for it. if something goes wrong, he or she has to take the fall for it. we regret that three months ago, following the kasaka follow-up report on the 2001 discharge, an amendment by the liberals was adopted by only one vote. in this amendment we said that we regretted that commissioner solbes had not taken political responsibility from day one. it is obvious that the atmosphere in this house is different now, given the many amendments i see on the bayona report. i can only say that the resistance of the large groups in this parliament was not as strong two or three months ago as it is now. in view of the hearings for the new commission at the end of the year, one of the important points we must emphasize is the individual responsibility of the commissioners for the implementation of the budget. if we make every official in the commission responsible for his own area of work, then surely the commissioners should be too.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. president. - the next item is the joint debate on the following motions for resolutions: it concerns a number of agencies that we have looked at and it concerns in particular the european agency for safety and health at work in bilbao. i was extremely surprised when i read the court of auditors' report, because the court of auditors accuses this agency of having transferred far too much. further research showed that this was linked to a sme programme which we had decided to introduce here in parliament. it took some time to agree with the commission on where to implement this programme, but it was finally decided to host it at the agency in bilbao. of course, this is not the fault of the agency in bilbao, but the court of auditors has a very negative opinion on this. of course, she could have easily checked all that. you know, sometimes you wonder, - is that right? - yeah. we all knew it here and the rapporteur, mr mulder, has also said it in his final report, although less sharply than i do. it would be good if the court of auditors did not just look at the first level, but went a little deeper. that is my view on this matter, but otherwise there are no problems with the agencies with which we are dealing. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. there is still a problem with this, and that is that member states always overestimate how much they can take from the structural funds. that leads to an unbalanced budget and that leads to non-spending at the end of the year. this is particularly true at the beginning of a period. i think the commission should be a little less naive in this respect. so far, when a new period has begun, we have received virtually nothing from the ground in the first year. of course, it would be nice if this were to improve in the future. that point should be better regulated in future than it is now, otherwise it means that history is never learned.
mr president, this parliament had just given the commission a discharge for 2001 last year when the eurostat affair came to light. that confirmed the position taken by my group that a delay in the discharge was necessary. the commission still refuses to take responsibility for the fraud at eurostat, as we have seen today. this shows the commission's arrogant and contemptuous attitude towards parliament and the citizens, whose taxpayers' money is at stake. a second example of maladministration concerns the improperly organised recovery of sums wrongly paid. it is truly shocking that the commission is still paying out money to the member states, knowing that the control and management systems are not in order in 15% of cases. when will the commission stop this and suspend part of the payment? when will the commission have a complete and sound register of debtors in its books? so far, she's been moping with the faucet open. i would ask the commission, mr president, to promise that it will submit to parliament before 1 may next, within 14 days, an evaluation of the aid to the lessfavoured areas, irrespective of whether all the member states have complied with their obligation to provide information. it is then immediately clear which member states need special attention and we can have our colleagues in the national parliaments put pressure on their governments. there's a lot wrong with those problem areas. the validity of the classification is questionable and the set of indicators effectively gives member states carte blanche. farmers working in similar conditions receive unequal compensation and so on. there's enough work in store for you and your successors to make this right. finally, i would ask the commissioner to give a clear answer to the proposal to create a separate directorate-general for accounting and anti-fraud from 2005 onwards, so as to put an end to the confusion of functions in the current dg budget.
the rapporteur. thank you, mr president, thank you, mr commissioner, for your detailed explanation. we all agree that terrorists must be fought. but that should not be a license for things that we cannot account for in the end. i have to tell you, commissioner, you didn't convince me tonight. first of all, you said that you were concerned about the transfer through the customs office. the commission currently refers to transfers to other public authorities, including foreign public authorities, in recital 20 of the decision. we have heard for the first time in the last few days that you are concerned about the way this has been handled. a few months ago you asked parliament for an adequacy finding. in the meantime, we have been informed by mr bolkestein and by you that this adequacy finding is still not in fact there. then there is the question of the agreement itself. parliament is calling for a genuine international agreement. parliament called for non-discrimination between eu citizens in the use of data. parliament called for equivalence in the united states and for efficient, independent remedies for incorrect data and for a clear legal process for eu citizens who get into trouble. you said there was a reciprocity. the commissioner, article 6 of the agreement states that, as far as is feasible and on the basis of strict reciprocity, the cooperation of airlines under his jurisdiction will be actively promoted. that's not reciprocal. article 1 tells us what we have to do. cbp shall have electronic access to pnr data in the booking and departure systems of air carriers located in the territory of the member states of the european community. such shall be strictly in accordance with the decision and for as long as the decision is in force. we are thus legalising what we have not wanted all along, and that is the reason why parliament is now calling for a real agreement, which has parliament's consent. you say that this is not necessary, because the legal basis chosen by the council is directive 1995/45 and this agreement would not change the substance of the directive. how can you say that when article 2 of the agreement stipulates that everything will be dealt with in accordance with the laws of the united states? i think we are introducing legislation from the united states. this isn't a real deal. this is an agreement of which mr bolkestein says that many things are still unresolved. i have not yet mentioned the connection between this agreement and caps ii, but the undertakings also state that this agreement will in fact be linked to the new advanced caps ii system in the united states. the united states congress has said that it is not good and has sent it back to the appropriate bodies. long story short, the way this deal was done is not right. mr bolkestein promised in september that parliament would be treated as a full partner. unfortunately, he did not keep that promise. in fact, the european commission and the council have deliberately tried to sideline the european parliament. the treaty required parliament's assent, but you did not choose this. that is why i have made a strong case in parliament and recommended to mr cox, through the legal affairs committee, that article 300 (6) of the treaty be invoked to have this agreement examined by the court of justice of the european communities. that is the only way we can do anything about this agreement and protect our citizens adequately. now they are not adequately protected. i am extremely sorry to hear that. i would have liked to see you withdraw the agreement. i share your view that terrorism is terrible, but to use all of this as a license to trample on our legislation and give americans access to our data without legal protection, because the us constitution and the privacy act do not apply to european citizens, is going too far. i urge you once again to withdraw this agreement and to come to parliament with a real agreement.
mr president, the question is simple. is the agreement in order and was the european commission authorized to conclude it? my group wants a legal opinion on this. the european commission cannot oppose this, even if it is convinced that everything is ok. commissioner patten just said that the agreement is better than nothing. i don't agree with that. the european commission has been given some restrictions, but the agreement still contains too much data and it is kept for an absurdly long time. privacy is not sacred, but violations must be proportionate. i therefore think that we should continue negotiations with the united states. if the united states does not want that, we simply do not give that information. of course, the united states will ask passengers at airports for this information, but then citizens will see immediately how much information is involved and i think that this is how you organize resistance from a different angle. i think that's very important. it is a matter of political will to ensure that our own european laws are respected. i think it is the duty of the european commission to ensure that they are monitored. the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, when i took on the task of becoming rapporteur for this report, i was well aware that it would not be an easy task. i have therefore applied a number of tests which i believe should be applied in such a difficult situation. we have, for example, organised a hearing earlier this year in which many experts spoke and gave their views on freedom of expression and protection of pluralism. in addition, the institute for the media in düsseldorf, a highly respected institute, has been asked to carry out a survey in all the countries of europe. i believe that this is a study which should have been carried out by the european commission a long time ago. this study will be available to the european commission in due course. i spoke to a lot of experts, and all of them, whether they represented the dome of journalists, public media or commercial media, appreciated my recommendations. i have also made a series of recommendations to promote pluralism in europe. one of these recommendations concerns the definition of minimum conditions throughout the european union to ensure that public service broadcasters are independent and free from government interference, as recommended by the council of europe. other recommendations concern rules on transparency of media ownership, in particular cross-border ownership, and disclosure of information on significant media interests. i also ask for an examination of the need to include a pluralism test in the merger regulation and to lower thresholds for media concentrations, as well as the need to include provisions on this in national rules. i'm not going to list all my recommendations here. i only know that there are many in europe who are waiting for an update of the commission's green paper and for proposals to clarify its role in the field of freedom of expression. it is of the utmost importance in a democracy that pluralism be respected. i have expressed a number of concerns on the basis of all the discussions i have had and on the basis of the report by the institute for the media, and one of those concerns is indeed italy. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to take them up. this mandate has been given to me by the conference of presidents. it was therefore my duty to pay particular attention to italy on the basis of a petition and at the request of the conference of presidents. many of my colleagues have reinforced the attention i had already given to italy. does this report now address matters that cannot be dealt with? mr president, i do not think so. facts are presented on the basis of which we say to the european commission: do you not have a moral duty to develop policies regarding media pluralism? i believe that the commission has that duty. i have also noted in my opinions that we can make more demands on public media than on commercial media in terms of pluralism. as rapporteur, i am well aware that the market is doing its job and i have no objection to that. the question is whether the pluriformity is threatened by concentration or otherwise, because certain media are too much in one hand. that's not new, commissioner. we do this in all kinds of policy areas. it is one of our internal market tasks to ensure that excessive concentrations do not occur. all i ask is that freedom of expression be guaranteed, for the sake of a good democracy. i am very sorry that certain members who have not been involved in the debate from the start have now suddenly tabled a great many amendments to the report. i do not think that this report deserves that, i did not expect it and i do not think it is democratic.
president. - mr ceyhun's report deals with a very important subject, drug policy, and in particular with the agency, the observatory, in lisbon. my group considers this a very useful instrument, which helps it to base its policy on facts. given the politicization of all talk about drugs, and from the netherlands this politicization is very convincing, we really cannot miss such a centre any more. the centre's task, as the commissioner has just said, is to collect, standardise, analyse and compare data. i am surprised that a number of amendments now seek to extend this task to the evaluation of member states' policies. but that is not the place for a scientific and technical bureau or agency, which is far from political either. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in my group's view, it is unacceptable that this policy should be technicalised, that this political task should be delegated. - mr president, once again many fine words have been spoken about relations between europe and the united states. however, i remain a little skeptical when i look at the reality. relations between europe and the united states have been better. the us government regularly gives its transatlantic partners a hard time. the us policy is characterized by unilateralism and changing coalitions. iraq has proved to be a mushrooming splinter and a major role for the un in that country still seems a long way off. earlier this morning, there was talk of the middle east. president bush's support for prime minister sharon and his unilateral policies has rightly been badly received here. the eu is clearly not a serious partner for the us on this issue. no one is looking for a real break between the united states and the european union. we have a lot in common and we could do more together in the world. perhaps things will get better under a new american administration, but in my view the trends are deeper. we are here today to discuss an agenda for transatlantic dialogue. such a dialogue is more necessary than ever, but it must be an open dialogue and not one in which one side only says things and little is heard. we must not, above all, try to hide the differences between us and the americans. in the field of international security, we see a number of priorities, including iraq and what we can learn from it. first of all, of course, that such a thing should never happen again. we must also return to a full role for the united nations, which must be given political leadership as soon as possible in the reconstruction of the country. we must also continue to work together to resolve the middle east conflict, and stick to the road map. if president bush does not become much more critical of prime minister sharon's policy of the established facts - i mention the security wall, gaza and the killing of hamas leaders - cooperation with the eu is unlikely to achieve much. we also want an effective response to international terrorism, but not only on american terms. what we want is priority for a multilateral approach to international problems. preventive engagement, as javier solana calls it, rather than preemptive wars. we want to strengthen the un and, above all, the international legal order. we must therefore continue to support the work of the international criminal court and the support that the european union has always given to it. what we also want is a serious approach to disarmament initiatives. we must discuss all these issues with the americans, but we must also remain firm in our own strength. pragmatic, but we're pursuing our own goals. where the americans, the united states, emphasize military supremacy, we see the european union as a role reserved for it as, say, a civilian superpower.

(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) at the first reading of the debate on 10 march i expressed my rejection of the plan. the second reading now sets out, without a vote on the overall scale, an excessively long list of motorways, airports, bridges and tunnels as investment requirements for the eu. it will lead to a long-term co-financing plan and probably also make it possible to circumvent the requirement of environmental impact assessments. the package also reveals the desire to further neglect the good railway network in eastern europe by building a large number of motorways. i am opposed to the idea conceived by the berlusconi government in italy that tax revenues should be spent mainly on infrastructure and other large public works rather than on public services, environmental protection and social security. we don't have time for a serious debate today. my group will try to get the plans for a bridge across the strait of messina dropped, because it is unrealistic because of earthquakes and wind gusts, because the bridge is superfluous because of the deficiencies in the interconnected railway network, and because the construction of the bridge mainly serves the interests of the mafia. i also support all the green amendments. the rejection of all these amendments will be a heavy burden for europe and an unnecessary attack on the environment for many years to come.
in the case of the european parliament, the european council has already taken a decision on the future of the european union. today, the support for this can be measured in the 1999-2004 parliamentary term, which is about to end. on a proposal from the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and the european democrats, a majority of 31 voted against moving all meetings to brussels. the cost of this rejection amounts to eur 185 million per year and rises to eur 203 million after enlargement. the vote did not include the proposal to reimburse only travel expenses actually incurred, as a more vague proposal was adopted only after an examination of travel expenses. my group made two proposals to limit the period during which the attendance list can be signed. a proposal to limit it to regular meeting times was rejected by 99 votes to 355, and a proposal to abolish the possibility of signing it on days when there are no meetings was rejected by 151 votes to 320. only the recently introduced option of additional taxi charges was rejected by 351 votes to 146. it is an incredibly poor harvest so far in the fight to limit parliament's unnecessary expenditure. in these circumstances it is good that the council did not authorize the recent wage increase.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) for 40 years the greek cypriots have insisted that their island should be reunited. it was unacceptable to the turkish cypriot minority in the north of the island, because they feared that they would become a disadvantaged minority in their own country. in the meantime, a majority of turkish cypriots are likely to support reunification and reconciliation on the basis of the plan of the un secretary-general. the plan proposes a belgian model of state, a federation of two federal states with a single language. it now seems that a majority of the greek-speaking population will vote against the 24th of april, as they want more rights for themselves and fewer for the turkish-speaking minority. it wants to strengthen the unilateral condition that cyprus should become a greek-speaking united state in which individual turks are allowed to reside. under these conditions, no agreement will ever be reached between the two nations, and the barbed-wire border separation will remain forever. the commissioner, like most groups, now feels deceived, and rightly so. i warned against this on 5 september 2001, when we discussed the poos report on the accession of cyprus to the european union. by initially only including the greek-speaking part of the eu, the turks could be forced to choose between poverty outside the eu or subjugation in the eu member state of cyprus. after the failed referendum, we must look for another way of including the turkish-speaking part of the eu if it so wishes.

the spanish government, which was responsible for the disaster of the tanker prestige and the fuel oil on the spanish, french and portuguese coasts, has now been voted out of office by the electorate. to prevent possible oil pollution in a spanish port, the tanker's captain was ordered to leave the coast and eventually sink the ship in open water. as a result, the disaster became much worse. of the 77,000 tonnes of crude oil on board, 43,000 tonnes have been salvaged and it is estimated that 14,000 tonnes are still on board. this means that 20,000 tonnes have disappeared into the sea and that the galician coast is contaminated in the long term. the report is right to criticise the netherlands and belgium for seemingly not considering the use of port state control and classification society inspection of ships as a matter of urgency. proposals have been made for the designation of emergency ports, compulsory insurance cover to enable shipowners to be compensated for damage and expenses, the use of dispatchers to trace missing containers and stricter compliance with current environmental and safety rules. i can support these proposals. it is unfortunate that a european coast guard service, which is being planned, should not only be used for the joint protection of the environment, safety and compliance with fishing restrictions, but is also directly linked to the hunt for immigrants and possible terrorists and the further building of a european superstate.
- mr president, of course there are many things that bind europe and the united states together and of course there is the need for a dialogue between europe and the united states, because neither of them can solve the world's problems alone. but let there be no misunderstanding that this is a dialogue between partners with fundamentally different views on a number of crucial issues. i'll name a few. the core activity of europe's security strategy is conflict prevention and preventive engagement. a crucial part of the bush strategy is armed preemptive strikes. the european union's objective is to strengthen the united nations. the united states' objective is multilateralism à la carte. through the un if it can and if it suits washington and not through the un if it doesn't. the european union is committed to preventing the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction. that is officially the policy of the united states, but at the same time friendly regimes, such as israel in palestine, are being kept in the dark, while the united states itself continues to develop so-called mini nukes. the european union supports the international criminal court in order to strengthen international law. the united states is doing its utmost to make life as difficult as possible for the icc. the european union is a civilian superpower, mr president, or rather it could be if it concentrated more on strengthening the areas in which it is or could be good. i call them: the european union has a duty to help the countries of the region to develop their economic and social cohesion. the united states has learned the hard way in iraq that it cannot solve the problems of dictatorial regimes and terrorism on its own without the help of the united nations and europe. that - mr president, will we ever reach the level of civilization where large-scale human rights violations are a thing of the past? the main cause of such violations is usually the desire to retain or even to seize power at any cost. the barbaric practices of hatred for purely religious or ethnic reasons are also far from being eradicated. on the contrary, the indiscriminate blowing up of innocent civilians is increasing by the day. this week we commemorate the massacre in rwanda. the world community is collectively ashamed of its own passivity. is this for real? have we really drawn the right conclusions? is the european union's approach to human rights really as difficult in practice as the solemn declarations suggest? are we really prepared to give less weight to economic and strategic interests than to human rights? unfortunately, that is still not the case. large-scale violence, torture, structural violations such as abuse of women, and the caste system are still the reality of everyday life. mrs de keyser happily agrees with the pleas in my report last year to make the human rights dialogue with partner countries less open-ended and better structured. the question is to what extent has this been demonstrated in practice? since my report, cooperation with the council has improved significantly in a number of areas. i hope that the dutch presidency will continue to shape the strengthening of this relationship with our parliament. we are doing well to have the human rights report published at a fixed time each year so that it can give an opinion on the council's report and also on the findings of the un commission on human rights. our parliament should also focus more systematically on the follow-up of the human rights violations we have identified. the background to the de keyser report is unfortunate. nevertheless, my group supports the content, including the rights of the disabled and reproductive health. it is against every fundamental sense of justice that someone dies because of a lack of medical care. our christian-democratic friends should agree with that, even if it is a question of non-enforceable rights. finally, i would like to point out once again the increasing abuse of religions for political purposes and radicalism. given the seriousness of the threat and the reality of madrid, i repeat my plea from last year that the european union must map the problem in all its dimensions and devise a strategy to prevent this religious extremism. i also think it is high time that the euromed foundation, which was decided in valencia in 2002, finally got off the ground. the european union is proud to call itself a community of values. this means that our human rights policy must be less liberal and that means also a commitment to work towards a level of civilization where large-scale human rights violations no longer occur.
