knowledge-based society in the world by 2010, there is of course a lot of work to be done. as we have already seen in e-europe 2005, the successful implementation of broadband access depends to a large extent on the availability of e-content. you can have broadband, but if you don't have it, citizens don't care. the same applies to advanced services for mobile phone users, and i am referring to, for example, the third generation. mr president, i was in china a few weeks ago and i noticed that the chinese, along with the japanese and south koreans, are already concerned about the fourth generation of mobile telephony. this means that europe is also falling further behind in the field of third generation, as shown by nokia's figures. so one could say that the report on e-content is an extremely important report because it stimulates increased productivity and innovation, better information products and services, it has a positive impact on e-government, an area where we are also still far behind, and it leads to more projects in the field of e-health, e-business and e-learning. i am therefore glad that the commissioner is here tonight. the current commission proposal for e-content+ identifies the obstacles, particularly in the areas of public interest in europe, as well as those areas where the market is lacking adequate investment. the market cannot do everything, and governments have a legitimate role to play. the present proposal creates conditions for better access to and use of digital content and, where appropriate, for a high economic return of services based on access to and reuse of digital content. the committee on industry, external trade, research and energy has tabled some 16 amendments to the european commission's proposal. we want to ensure that the report focuses on the public sector, that it takes account of the financial perspective after 2007, that a budget of eur 55 million is set aside for the years 2005 and 2006, and that the total budget is eur 163 million. i should like to take this opportunity to thank my shadow rapporteurs, mrs neena gill and mrs elly plooij-van gorsel, for the excellent cooperation i have had with them. it seemed to be a wonderful party, because the council, with whom we were consulting, was also interested in one reading, so that we would not be delayed. we started with 163 million euros. the council, and in particular the irish presidency, pretended that a deal was within reach if we were to go back to 145 million. as good parliamentarians we thought that we would eventually come to an amount of somewhere in the region of 150 million. however, the irish presidency came back into the informal trialogue with the announcement that it could not go above 120 million and added that if we pushed a little, we might be able to get to 138 million. we have indicated that this was not enough for us and that it should be at least 140 million. we have also asked the council presidency to state in writing that it has therefore agreed to 140 million euro. the deal closed at 1:30 this afternoon. it is typical of the council that i then received the following message at five minutes past eleven. i quote, mr president, that is good for history. " however, you ask for a written position from the council in favour of a financial amount of one hundred and forty million euros. on the basis of the coreper discussion this morning, this is not possible as there remains a clear blocking minority at one hundred thirty million euros. however, my own estimate, and that is as the chairman of coreper i and based on the contacts with a number of key delegations at and around the meeting, is that if the european parliament were, through its vote tomorrow, to take a position for an amount higher than one hundred and thirty million, it may very likely prove possible ultimately to gather a sufficient majority in the council for that". mr president, this is the third time we have received the same message from the council, and each time we say to the council: deliver it, the council says he can't. with so much, let me say, uncertainty in the council, we cannot but conclude that we will soon be campaigning for elections. that we will say to the citizens: the council is making great headlines in brussels when it comes to the lisbon process, but when it comes to delivering, also in terms of financial resources to achieve the lisbon process, the council says no way, the council is not at home and leaves us empty-handed. i find it extremely sad that we, who want to have the most competitive and dynamic knowledge-based economy in the world by 2010, are treated in this way by the council as a parliament. i'm glad there's going to be campaigns. it is in any case a motivation for me to join in and to denounce this ambivalence of the council. better days are ahead.
unfortunately, mr president, the outcome of yesterday's hearing was not a surprise. those who have been there in recent years, in recent months, have seen with their own eyes that this process was a sham. there was no interest on the part of the judges, let alone the public prosecutor, to critically examine the old 1994 testimony once again. in this trial, too, the defense was not given any opportunity to call new witnesses who would like to make opposing noises. this was a copy of a bad trial, and the result of a bad trial is a bad verdict. but let me be clear: this says less about the government's reformist spirit than it does about the stubborn resistance of conservatives. these judges, the judges of yesterday, wanted to show who's boss. their answer is clear: we are the ones who are in charge, not the european parliament or the european commission in brussels, not the government in ankara, but we are the ones who decide what happens in this case. i think the only way for the government to limit the damage - which it has undeniably suffered in connection with its request to start negotiations in december - is to show that this is an exception to the rule. that rule must remain: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. two examples. there has already been talk of plans, i emphasize the plans, to abolish the state security courts. i would urge the government to act quickly, to stop delaying and to ensure that these courts are actually abolished by december. the second example, which is of course very symbolic in this case, is the use of the kurdish language, which is the reason for this whole issue. on paper, again on paper, the use of this language is possible, but in practice there are still very many administrative obstacles. it is therefore very important that by the end of this year the use of the kurdish language in education and the media is actually possible. finally, mr president, for those who have concluded that negotiations should not be started on the basis of this case alone, i would like to recall the words of mrs zana herself. in a letter to the european parliament, she said: i would rather be in prison in a country that is negotiating with the european union than be free in a country that has been turned away by europe.
i would like to add my support to the beautiful rhetoric of mr lagendijk. i would prefer that mrs leyla zana should live free in a country that is negotiating with the european union. that's actually the only option i find acceptable. it is a very strange thing, in fact, that someone has been in prison for so long and has been in possession of the sakharov prize for almost as long. that is an extraordinary schizophrenic condition. it is also very strange that the european union has continued for so long with business as usual. we cannot continue to do business as usual in this way. we will have to be more difficult, we will have to exert more pressure so that this pressure can be passed on to the opposing forces in turkey. we are still dealing with a strong influence of the old state philosophy, the old type of kemalism, which was also expressed in this criminal trial. the people responsible for this are themselves the bearers of that old philosophy and naturally come back to the same conclusion. i agree with the colleagues who have spoken on this before that it is a special case that the government, and we must admit this, has done its best and sometimes drastically changed the law. in particular, the possibility of reopening proceedings is provided by the government. the possibility of releasing a person is also provided by the government, but it is not used. that legislation is simply not being used or not being used in an appropriate way. that is something which i think we must also convince our own government leaders of. it's not just some symptoms, like the severe case of leyla zana and the hair. it is a consequence of a deeper issue and we must discuss it with turkey. i also think it is extremely important to try to change the way of thinking in the contacts between judges and prosecutors from the european union and their turkish colleagues, because it is a way of thinking that leads to constantly getting into traffic jams, that leads to nationalistic hatred against minorities who are feared to be a threat to the integrity of the state. mr president, for this reason i think we must insist strongly on using the existing possibilities to release mrs leyla zana and others who are in prison for similar so-called offences, pending a change in the whole judicial process.
madam president, last tuesday mr trichet, president of the european central bank, gave a lecture in rotterdam on the european economy. he said he was extremely concerned about the slow recovery in economic growth. i share your concern, because recovery is going to take too long and it's going to take too long to get there. well, waiting is not the right attitude to promote that recovery. activity is required, but that is what is lacking at the moment. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in the negotiations, but that it should be involved in the negotiations. i would urge the member states to finally put aside their differences and take action. countries like china and india have little chance of outstripping us. activity will require effort and political sacrifice, but waiting any longer is not justified.
mr president, i would like to make a few comments. the procedure followed so far, because it has created the necessary difficulties. i would also like to say a few words about lisbon, about mr kok's report on the quality of work, about china and india and finally about the amendments. as regards the procedure, i think that this is the second or third time that we have found ourselves, especially as regards the adoption of the employment guidelines, in a procedure in which we have little time. this leads to conflicts between the member states, beyond the usual political differences, over how the directives should be interpreted, how they should be amended, what should be changed, etc. i suggest we do it differently next year. that is an opinion to the incoming presidencies of the council and to the commission. it is necessary to ensure that parliament is given the opportunity to do what it is entitled to do under the treaty, namely to comment in a correct way, so that we do not have to vote down a whole bunch of amendments which are not, let us say, in the correct procedural order. i also wonder whether the three-year procedure we have chosen is wise. in view of the rapid developments in the economy and the fact that the commission has had to adjust its optimistic views in a very short time and has had to realise that things are not going as well as they were thought, i think we should have the room to adjust these guidelines, both economic and employment guidelines, on some important points. i would also point out that there is a serious mistake in the treaty. that's one way of putting it. in fact, the employment guidelines are by definition subordinate to the economic guidelines. that is something we do not wish. it leads to all kinds of problems, for example with proposals for liberalization, but it also leads to all kinds of other measures being proposed that have their effects. i agree with what a number of speakers before me, mr jonckheer and older people and women in the workforce. there is progress, but it is far too little and we really need to review the guidelines. we will have to revise the lisbon agreements in the light of, among other things, economic developments. against this background, the statement in the kok report that we must implement and improve the implementation of the whole is quite plausible. that was also the mandate of the council. i wonder, however, whether that will be enough. the commission is proposing to introduce a new system of taxation of capital. but i see in reality that attention is paid to flexibility, not to certainty. the temporary agency workers directive is still blocked by both germany and the uk. the quality of work, which is also one of mr kok's proposals, will not be accepted. in other words, i think that this should be addressed and that we should go back to the proposals of the belgian presidency on quality of work, for example. that was a useful report and it is not just a simple category like job satisfaction that should be looked at. i think we should also look not only at the united states but also at developments in china and india. we are currently facing massive off-shore outsourcing with all its side effects. finally, i would like to thank everyone for their cooperation in the past. i can also tell you that the green group will support the amendments tabled by mrs hermange and probably also the oral amendment tabled by mr bushill-matthews.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) last night i gave mr caudron the opportunity to make his last speech after 15 years as a member of the european parliament, as he is about to retire. he presented the final position of our group on the railway package and made it clear that this liberalisation proposal is appropriate for a very different europe from that which the joint group of the european left wants. in 2000, the commission tried to force member states to privatise all public transport. after the european parliament excluded important parts of urban and regional transport from this experiment on 14 november 2001, the commission has turned its attention to railways. it wants to liberalize not only international freight transport but also domestic passenger transport. in the meantime, we have had some bad experiences in the netherlands. the decision to sell shares in the state-owned railway company nederlandse spoorwegen on the stock exchange allegedly resulted in negligence of the rolling stock and railway infrastructure, delays, labour disputes, etc., and the process has therefore been stopped. it was not done because of a political choice, but because the staff, the passengers and, in time, the politicians learned their lesson. the provision of train services by a competing company, which has since been acquired by the international transport giant connex-vivendi, has also been discontinued. the council has held back similar plans for europe, but they have not yet been removed from the agenda.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) in pakistan, parliamentary democracy has never had a fair chance because of the military's interference. every time voters elect a decent government, it is eventually overthrown by the military. former prime minister bhutto was even killed after he was deposed, and his daughter, who was popular as long as she was considered his political heir, was later also removed from power. this military intervention against progressive governments has created a breeding ground for islamic fundamentalism and for groups that want to turn individual populations against each other. these groups have been able to continue their activities legally under military rule. the current president musharraf was considered until a few years ago to be a dangerous general who had seized power in a coup and who wanted to make his country a nuclear power, gave way to a very conservative interpretation of islam and cooperated with the taliban regime in neighbouring afghanistan. but when he first supported the american military intervention in afghanistan, he was suddenly considered an american ally. this proposal gives him the same status in europe. once again, commercial and military interests take precedence over human rights. it is surprising that parliament first approves an agreement with pakistan and then later in the sitting adopts a resolution deploring the lack of human rights.
the netherlands is a significant net contributor to the eu. unfortunately, the reason for this is rarely discussed. firstly, a large proportion of eu imports arrive at the port of rotterdam. the relevant import duties are collected by the dutch customs authorities and then transferred to brussels. the netherlands does not actually benefit from this. another reason is the agreements concluded by mr kok and mr zalm in berlin in 1999. the result of these agreements is that dutch farmers now receive far less from the brussels cashier than before. dutch farmers account for 7% of european agricultural production. in 1999, the netherlands still received 3.5% of the european agricultural budget, but in 2004 this figure has fallen to 2.1%. moreover, the netherlands receives only 1.2% of the rural development funds because the dutch government at the time gave this issue a low priority in the negotiations. the best way to improve the netherlands' position as a net contributor is to ensure that the netherlands receives a greater share of eu agricultural and rural development funds in the future. it is not the hague, after all, but the rural areas of the netherlands that are paying the european bill. the best way for the netherlands to regain its rightful place in the eu is to correct the errors in the berlin agreement. it is much better than to limit expenditure to only 1% of community gdp.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the majority want more money for the structural funds and the cohesion fund. it will keep payments to the richest member states in check, without depriving the currently poorest countries, while giving priority to the new member states, which will have the lowest standard of living for the time being. the idea behind this is that the eu will become more popular if everyone gets more. a union that is generous to all is expected to be perceived positively by public opinion, the broadest sections of society and local authorities. so wherever a project is co-financed by the eu, we still see signs informing us of it. the question whether the money in question will actually be made available is not raised. and certainly not the question of whether these projects, many of which are undoubtedly beneficial, are best carried out through the mediation of european officials and with european money. eu funds can be better used for development aid to help those who are lagging behind. less money and less bureaucracy can produce better results. all other projects should be returned to the member states.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) in july 2003, the commission suspended the activities of the eurostat management as cases of fraud which had been detected some time before continued. at that point i demanded immediate, transparent action. the commission's investigations are useless because they have to keep their findings secret. the only way to prevent future fraud, subversion and nepotism is to give the public the right information and the opportunity to express their opinion. that could be done by a parliamentary inquiry. at the end of september 2003 the members of the committee on budgetary control received a secret report and the commission continues to act as if it had no responsibility in this matter. the person who was first appointed to deal with the matter, commissioner solbes, is now a minister in the new spanish government. everything is being done to avoid a discussion of a vote of no confidence in the commission, which i and others have signed. the deliberate omission to put this matter on the agenda means that this vote of no confidence cannot be taken this week. after 1 may, the number of signatories will fall to less than 10% as a result of the enlargement of the european parliament. the green party chairman has called on his members to undermine this initiative by withdrawing their signatures. the problems of 1999 continue, but the prodi commission ignores them.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the information and propaganda media are not misused. if all the media are concentrated in the same hands, we will be in the period when the media had a smaller reach and a monopoly was often created, involving the authorities, a powerful party, the church or a regional publisher. people heard only one view of what was good and what was bad and how society should develop. governments and big business are still trying to achieve it. recently, the previous spanish government tried to blame three major bombings on a separatist movement instead of an international movement because it would be better for them in the elections. in italy, the prime minister controls the commercial company mediaset as owner and the state-run company rai by virtue of his position in the government. the christian democrats are not criticizing this situation and have tried to keep this matter off the agenda by ignoring the problems, dragging the negotiations out and putting forward a huge number of amendments. they are abusing democracy to abolish democracy. i support the rapporteur in her efforts to do something about cable monopolies and to push for the introduction of legislative measures aimed at protecting freedom of expression and dissent in the media.
mr president, i am a signatory to this resolution. good news from castro's cuba, is that possible? yes, and i would like to refer you to the page-long article in the neue zürcher zeitung yesterday on the relentless domestic efforts to bring about a peaceful regime change on the island. the headline of the article spoke for itself: "third run-up to the referendum in cuba". the driving force behind this proposed referendum is oswaldo payá, winner of the sakharov prize of this house. and he's not afraid of the hostility and chicanery of castro and his cronies. payá's main concern is the growing popularity of his initiative. more and more cubans are losing their fear of government repression. in may 2002, payá and his supporters were able to hand over 11,020 signatures to the national parliament for a referendum. at the beginning of october 2003, 14,384 new signatures were added. about half of them are from the period after the wave of arrests in march 2003. there was no response from the muzzled parliament. this does not prevent payá from launching a third petition. the european parliament expressly supports the cuban people who are rejecting the achievements of a democratic state based on the rule of law. the house expects the council, the member states of the union and the commission to show the same support. i very much hope that the committee on foreign affairs will deliver on its intention of a full report on the situation in cuba in the next term. we owe it to the freedom-loving citizens of this island, so attractive to european tourists.

rapporteur. – mr president, the request for urgent procedure cannot be accepted by parliament as the council has not forwarded to parliament a text to which the international agreement makes reference, namely the commission's draft decision on adequacy, declaring that passengers' data are adequately protected by the us; nor has it received the so-called undertakings by the us administration. in other words, we still have no guarantee that european passenger data transferred to the us will not be passed on to third countries. this is the first vote of the new enlarged parliament, the only democratically elected institution that represents 450 million citizens. the council is asking us to vote with closed eyes on a question that relates to our fundamental rights. if we accept this request, parliament cannot be taken seriously. in addition, there is a procedural defect in that translation into the new languages is not available. i ask you to withdraw this item and also to respect our referral of the matter to the court of justice. in july 2003 the commission suspended the management of eurostat because the cases of fraud, which had been known for a long time, continued. i demanded the same publicity back then. it is pointless to let institutions which are dependent on the commission carry out an inquiry if they are then to keep their findings secret. only if the public is well informed and can participate in the assessment of the matter can fraud, self-enrichment and nepotism be prevented in the future. a committee of inquiry makes this possible. even after a secret report was presented to the members of the committee on budgetary control at the end of september 2003, the commission can still pretend that it has no responsibility. the main person responsible, mr solbes, is now a minister in the new spanish government. there is no sign that the problems which five years ago forced the previous commission to resign before the elections have been solved. to illustrate the seriousness of the situation and to ensure that the next commission will be required to address the problems, i am voting today in favour of the motion of censure. the fact that the greens, who were previously in opposition, opposed it at the last meeting in order to prove their administrative reliability does not make it any less necessary to continue the fight against fraud.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) our group strongly supported the proposal which has just been rejected. now voters can see at once which members will shed light on the current commission's mismanagement, mismanagement and misrepresentation and which members will cover it up or not care about it. the slow and failed reform, the obsolete accounting system, which is vulnerable to fraud, and the eurostat affair are just three characteristic examples. they show that what the independent expert group found as early as 1999 is also true of this commission, namely that there is hardly anyone within the commission who is willing to take on responsibility. it is a travesty of democracy in europe and a denial of parliament's rights when members of the commission do not intervene when they are asked to do so, but also do not resign. if the commission rejects the european parliament's primary task of control, we have no choice but to dismiss it. otherwise we would not take ourselves seriously, nor would the citizens who elected us. the so-called european constitution is increasingly proving to be nothing more than a showpiece to hide from the citizens what is happening in the european union and how their interests are being harassed and manipulated. the vote of no confidence was a means of putting an end to the hesitation of the prodi commission and marks the beginning of an open culture in which everyone takes his responsibilities seriously.
- mr president, accession to the european union continues to play a part in the public and political imagination of eastern europe, even after, or perhaps precisely because of, the great enlargement round which has just been officially concluded. to illustrate, consider the following story. the president and the minister for economic affairs of one of the so-called new neighbouring countries in the east visit the oracle of delphi together. the minister's pressing questions about the trend of macroeconomic figures are answered surprisingly positively. to his disappointment, it adds: "not just during your tenure". encouraged by these promises, the president wants to know if his country might also become a member of the european union. "certainly", the famous oracle replies, "but not during my tenure". commissioner verheugen recently made a statement which bears a striking resemblance to the last oracle's spell. he stated unequivocally that for a considerable time the western border of the former soviet union, with the exception of the baltic states, would be the eastern border of the european union. clear language, which, incidentally, provoked the comment of the offended ukrainians that a turkish-moroccan camel would seem to be more easily able to pass through the eye of the needle of the european commission than a so-called foreign orthodox civilization such as the ukrainian. she told us. meanwhile, verheugen's choices of position are a testament to his sense of reality. that is to say, for the foreseeable future. speculation about ukraine's long-term eu prospects, for example, depends on several factors. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. can you still have the decency to keep the door closed for kiev? on the other hand, very recent statements by the ukrainian president, leonid kuchma, show that it is not accession to the european union but accession to the wto and nato that are currently kiev's international priorities, perhaps to reassure mr elles. in any case, the european union is faced with a new neighbourhood policy after this enlargement, independent of the question of membership of the european union. the new eastern border is a source of considerable concern, as the internal situation in belarus, ukraine and moldova is a cause for great concern. how can we promote the establishment of democratic states of law in these countries? concrete neighborly help is the most obvious. the european union must also be able to take account of the need to ensure that the community's trade policy is not distorted. the new member states will also play a major role in shaping the union's relations with its eastern neighbours. they are the most affected. we are counting on them today, just as the reformist forces in the societies of belarus, ukraine and moldova must be able to count on the european union in the near future.
- mr president, i would like to return to a point which has been emphasized in an equally powerful and charming way by my group's chairman, mrs frassoni, namely the possibility for turkish cypriots to take part in the european elections. i would like to make a few points and would like to hear the council's comments on this, if it is listening. if the turkish cypriots cannot vote, do you not think that it should be considered to leave two of the six cypriot seats specifically reserved for the defence of the interests of the turkish cypriots unoccupied? secondly, in the debate on the current enlargement, the question is often asked: where does it end? what is europe stopping at? this is a point for commissioner nielson. the european commission has so far taken a very pragmatic approach to this. in addition to romania, bulgaria and turkey, the balkans have been given a 'yes' in principle, without a time-frame being set. ukraine and belarus have received neither a yes nor a no. however, i read in the newspaper that the president of the european commission, romano prodi, said in dublin last weekend that the books are closed for ukraine and belarus. they can never become members of the european union. is this a change of attitude on the part of the european commission? has the european commission defined the ultimate frontiers of the european union between the companies this weekend, or is there a misunderstanding here? the third point, mr president, concerns an old debate, namely whether it is possible to expand and yet deepen. in other words: what are the implications of enlargement for the prospects for a common foreign policy? some, including ourselves, believe that the chances of success after enlargement are less, because there are now 25 countries, so the chances of unanimity have become smaller. others are very happy about that, because they don't want any foreign policy at all. i think the lesson from iraq is that we never want to see a division like this in europe again. i think that the chances of a european foreign policy have increased and i do not expect the new member states to be a trojan horse, as many think. i think it is possible to expand and deepen it. this means that a common foreign policy can also be established after enlargement.
- mr president, cooperation between the europe of the twenty-five and its neighbours to the east, such as ukraine, and to the south, such as north africa, should not be primarily about membership of the european union, but about stability and security pacts, with joint control of borders and border regions as the main priority. at the same time, the europe of the twenty-five must itself put things in order. in this europe too much is neoliberalism and there is no social dialogue, no social security and no work. in this europe too many decisions are taken in back rooms and there is no public-political debate. in this europe, too much bureaucracy and too little selectivity. a more social, democratic and selective europe means a strong europe with a constitution. the european union should then be able to support the stability and security pacts with the new neighbours with the confidence of the citizens. the european parliament has already expressed its views on the commission's proposal for a regulation on the establishment of a european agency for the evaluation of medicinal products. only if the citizens of the twenty-five feel stronger and safer will we, as the europe of the twenty-five, be able to be good neighbours. stability and security pacts mean fair trade areas, human rights programmes and knowledge exchange programmes, but also cooperation agreements to keep a close watch on our external borders and regions against terrorism, trafficking in women and crime. however, these stability and security pacts should not be at the expense of europe's global contribution to the fight against poverty and the ending of conflicts. on the contrary, our contribution, including that of the new members, must be increased. this is also part of the social model for europe - social inward and social outward. i believe, mr president, that europe should withdraw from iraq until the un has really taken over the mandate.
- mr president, it has been a privilege for me to have been involved in the accession process as coordinator of epp policy during the last three periods, from the liberation of eastern europe to the accession of the new countries, and also as rapporteur on strategy in the beginning. it was about making the union what the once so oppressed peoples hoped it would be: the european union is a federation of democratic states governed by the rule of law, which is internationally recognised as a factor for peace. the acceding states expected to come home to a community of values that also corresponded to their deepest desires. we are not a market in the first place: "it's not the economy, stupid!" the council may be less inclined to this, but we, as representatives of the european i would like to welcome the dutch presidency, mr president, on behalf of the dutch and european social democrats in the european parliament. your minister for european affairs has asked us to focus on the political debate. we as european socialists are happy to accept this challenge. you talk about modernisation, competition with the usa and asia, and the implementation of the lisbon objectives. we socialists fear that with these seductive words you are actually trying to do something quite different: the european parliament has a duty to support the european union. if a few years ago there was a huge effort to combat unemployment by reducing working hours and division of labour, now it is called extended working hours and you talk about shorter holidays. what's so modern about that? we were talking about quality of work, health and safety at work, prevention of illness and accidents at work with european-wide regulation, now you are talking about deregulation. has this removed the objective of lower absenteeism and higher labour force participation? we were talking about greater flexibility in the labour market with greater legal protection, and now you are talking about deregulation of this protection. your aim of abolishing administrative burdens seems more like an excuse for abolishing certain social and administrative acquisitions. at the same time, you do not address the european procurement rules which are suffocating small and mediumsized enterprises. we were talking then about investing 3% of gdp per member state in research and training, which the netherlands itself has so far fallen behind, and now you are talking only about the stability pact, cuts, and the hard play of the 3% deficit, without making money and space available to invest 3% in research and training. i would have liked to have seen a fight in the court of justice about that. that would be the only way to take the cook report - work, work, work - seriously. in 2000, we were talking about high-quality public services, and not just in infrastructure, but now you are talking about liberalising and privatising the services sector. read the following: electricity, health care, public transport. are you prepared to put a stop to mr bolkestein's disastrous proposals for the liberalization of services? the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. president, for the social democrats this seems to be an abuse of the lisbon agenda to implement a hard conservative economic agenda under the name of modernisation. we social democrats say: it is a way of turning back the clock, of refusing to work with brains, of putting quality, research, education and high-quality public services first. we fear that this will increase unemployment in europe, the weak will pay the price of austerity and you, with your candidate barroso, will take the wrong path to win the battle with asia and the usa. we challenge you to the debate, you understand your presidency and you are welcome, but your political choices still present us with the necessary problems and we look forward to the debate with your presidency. president, the 35th session of icao is about to be a memorable one. twenty-one delegations have put forward proposals which will not improve the position of aviation in terms of emission levels. the commission is also proposing to abolish the individual initiative of countries or regions to take emission control measures and to replace it with a more legislative initiative from icao. it is a contradiction in terms, because we never agree on such matters in icao. the community is committed to sustainable development in both ecological and economic terms. i've been a big supporter of that for years. in that light, we must dare to face reality. aviation and the environment are not friends. we are trying to change this on all fronts in this house, and we must continue to do so, in principle with our partners in other parts of the world, but if that is not possible, then without them. at the moment, aviation has certain unintended advantages over other modes of transport, and we have to get rid of them. the maintenance of tax advantages and the failure to cover certain social costs do not contribute to this. we must be careful not to throw our own glasses in. our measures must not lead to european companies being forced to stop fighting. measures are fine, but they have to be applied to all players. but that's not a free pass. i therefore also believe that the proposed amendments to icao resolution a33-7 should be withdrawn. a sustainable policy requires courage and foresight, and these proposals do not address these issues. the european community must show that transport and the environment are less contrasting than in the amendments.
president. - as the climate spokesman for the press group, i would naturally like to join all the colleagues who have expressed their concern about the icao resolution before us. this would be a step backwards, and we should be moving forward. our group is committed to a very decisive climate policy and we do not shy away from difficult choices. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not affected by the effects of the gatt agreements. so it is aviation that we must tackle. we believe that international emissions trading can provide an additional incentive for airlines to develop new and efficient aircraft, and thus sustainable aviation. i call on all members, and particularly on the epp colleagues, to support the resolution before this house and thus send a clear signal to the united states. i also urge the commission and the dutch presidency, if they come again, to support an american motion for a resolution on this subject at the icao meeting without reservation. instead, the european union should focus, together with its partners, on a constructive contribution by icao to the next conference of the parties to the kyoto protocol in buenos aires.
mr president, it goes without saying that the ulla garbage ship in south-west turkey has gone really wrong. it is also clear that this disaster could have been prevented. now over 300 million litres of water have been polluted, and a lot of investment will have to be made to clean up this pollution. but it is much more important to look for ways of preventing such situations in the future. this concerns in particular the implementation and enforcement of the international basel convention and the european regulation on shipments of waste. as rapporteur, i have been working intensively on the revision of the latter regulation for over a year. the current regulation clearly states that member states must take back their cargo if it is refused on good grounds. in november last year the european parliament proposed to extend the grounds for refusal. i hereby call on the european commission to adopt these proposals of the european parliament. in this case of the ulla ship, which was loaded with hazardous waste, it is particularly strange that the ship left without the permission of a receiving country. so apparently spain has acted against the rules. i would therefore ask the european commission to take additional enforcement measures and, if necessary, legal action.

president. - since 1 may this year belarus has been a member of the european union. it is well known that this geographical proximity is not necessarily parallel to political affinity. exactly one week ago, the belarusian head of state, lukashenko, highlighted this gap. he issued a decree to legalize and consolidate his authoritarian presidency by ballot after 2006. in just over a month, on october 17, this constitutional amendment could be a fait accompli. this predictable development does not in any way simplify relations between brussels and minsk. as belarus is a major transit route for energy and other goods supplies from russia to eu member states, the repressive regime of lukashenko is strong. and incidentally, over the years it has proved immune to direct international attempts at gradual democratization and reform. the undeniable desire of lukashenko to retain power will only strengthen this tendency in the coming period. what can the european institutions do in belarus? the political isolation of the current rulers towards the west seems complete, doesn't it? nevertheless, we must not abandon belarusian society to the unbridled lust for power of lukashenko. this is a path that is certainly difficult, as a german study this summer points out. the eu has also been active in the fight against corruption and organised crime. there is indeed a glimmer of light here, for despite the difficult conditions of recent years some 2500 registered civil society organisations have managed to survive to date. long-term contacts with foreign partners certainly contributed to this. the federal republic of germany is leading the way with hundreds of initiatives. how does the commission view this way out of the political impasse between the european union and belarus? as mrs guy-quint has just said, there is always something predictable about debates such as the one we are having today. the commission draws up a preliminary draft budget, the council draws up a draft budget and without knowing the figures, it can be predicted that the council will cut back on agricultural expenditure, as it will on almost all other budgetary categories. a large part of agricultural expenditure is regulated by law. i have always felt that the commission is better equipped than the council to determine the exact expenditure, and therefore i think it is a good tradition for parliament to take the commission more seriously than the council in this area. in any case, we shall have to wait and see what the note of amendments will say at the end of october. as a liberal group we hope, and this is already partly reflected in parliament's position on the 2005 budget, that two pilot projects will be continued. the first concerns the possibility of future insurance against infectious animal diseases, which will certainly occur again at some point and will weigh heavily on the agricultural budget. the second pilot project will lead to a system of quality assessment of european agricultural products. it is difficult to justify that farmers in europe have to compete more and more with farmers elsewhere in the world, while farmers elsewhere in the world do not have to comply with the same standards. there is much to be said about the many budget categories. as far as the structural funds are concerned, i think it is essential that the impact of the n+2 rule be analysed precisely and that it be checked how it relates to the estimates. if it is found that there are still large surpluses, it can be checked whether it is possible to cut back. i would also like to mention - president, when president prodi said two years ago that the stability pact was too inflexible, he was right. you, minister zalm, were at the forefront of the criticism, because anyone who wanted to change the stability pact was a softie. you took, as you often did, the obvious pleasure of throwing all the critics on a pile, because they were all pruts. now , may i ask you something ? is it not true that what you can live with now, what you have just read out, is very similar to what those same critics of the stability pact said two years ago? two examples: the commission has always been in favour of paying more attention to the public debt and less to the annual budget deficit. we have always said that there are situations where a member state is not penalized for exceeding 3%, for example if there is low growth for a long period. now there seems to be a willingness in the council and the commission to interpret the stability pact more flexibly, and i welcome that. but minister zalm, be honest and flexible, don't say: "we clarify and we improve and we clarify the statute". no, why don't you just say, "we are finally taking the constructive criticism of our green and left colleagues seriously and that is why we are modernising the pact". minister zalm, everyone has to admit they're wrong sometimes, this is your moment.
i would like to congratulate mr almunia on his achievements. he has made a courageous attempt to revive the stalled growth and stability pact. i would also like to congratulate and thank mr gerrit zalm as president of the ecofin council for the fact that he is not stuck in the old cows and i would not like to take them out of the ditch, as my colleague mr lagendijk did. what i do hope is that he does not do so merely on opportunistic and tactical grounds, but that he is now also convinced in substance that it is also important from an economic point of view to emphasize more the growth element of the pact. i would also like to say that my group very much appreciates the fact that you have indicated your willingness to continue the debate with our economic committee. i think it is important that we in the european parliament should be involved in this. as regards the content: my group is positive about the direction of the commission document, that will be clear. we have always advocated a better balance between the objectives of growth and stability. we want to get rid of the friction between the ambitious lisbon objectives on the one hand and the budgetary standards that are too tight for a prolonged period of recession on the other hand, in order to be able to invest in renewed economic growth. it is absurd that in portugal, for example, research and development and education expenditure is being cut rather than increased, and that greece is being punished - another example of budgetary overruns - for the wonderful olympic games we have all enjoyed so much. i also see, for example, in my own country the discussion with the lower levels of government on how they can finance investments from the reserves. the following also apply: they have the money in those reserves to make investments. that could be good for growth, but because of not exceeding the 3% budget deficit they would be forced to stop investing, which is absurd in this period. my group therefore supports a more precisely formulated growth pact. i would like to put forward two elements. first of all, i'm curious to hear your thoughts on the golden rule. as you know, there is a very strong call in the united kingdom, which is not part of the euro, for a distinction to be made between current budgetary expenditure and investment expenditure. your objection has always been that those boundaries cannot be drawn clearly. my plea would be not to leave this to the ingenuity of individual national finance ministers, but rather to see whether we cannot draw common standards from the lisbon objectives and the broad economic guidelines and hopefully also from the advice of wim kok's high level group in the future. i would suggest that we then translate the many fine commitments of lisbon and the subsequent summits into real investment and growth plans. the second element is that i propose to do so in multiannual plans, which coincide with that deadline of 2010 or with subsequent years. what do you think of the idea of writing off that growth and growth investments over several years so that they don't count on an annual basis for that 3% norm? i would like to hear from you whether you are prepared to continue such discussions in the ecofin council and i hope that we can thereby really put the pact back on its feet again, on the two legs it needs, namely stability and growth.
president, it is almost two years since president prodi called the stability and growth pact stupid. during these two years, the debate continued and led to the commission's proposals which were discussed at the last ecofin council in scheveningen. the european commission's proposals, in my view, provide a basis for further shaping the debate on the stability and growth pact, and in this respect the implementation of the pact continues to benefit from clear and unambiguous decision-making. sanctions in a clear manner remain required. the debate now taking place must not be detrimental to the implementation of the pact and to confidence in the euro. the debate at that time suggested that greater account should be taken of cyclical fluctuations. thus, in times of need, the surplus saved from good years can be used. i would like to hear from mr zalm whether this suggestion has been accepted and whether it may be the key to an acceptable new pact. i would also like to ask the council to clarify the duties and responsibilities of the next president of ecofin, mr juncker.
the court's ruling of 13 july was a positive development in the fight for the stability and growth pact. there was a clear signal from an independent body that agreed with the european commission on its approach to member states structurally exceeding the 3% standard. but now the same commission, albeit with a new commissioner, is presenting a proposal for the reform of the stability and growth pact which, because of its vagueness, will be particularly welcome in the accounting chambers of the adulterous members of the council. the commission is considering a more intelligent application of the pact, but i wonder what definition of intelligence the commission is using in this context, because since when is rewarding violations intelligent? let's not make this any more complicated than it already is. in the simplicity of the pact lies its strength. two large member states are now in difficult times and want to find a solution to their economic situation at the expense of the pact, and are only too eager to get ahead of the future. but how can we be confident that in a new system there will be any guarantee that those member states, france and germany, will continue to adhere to the agreements and, above all, as my colleague mr klinz has already said, that they will carry out the necessary economic reforms? a review of the pact could of course be useful, and i wholeheartedly agree with the president of ecofin. but whereas in the past i was definitely in favour of a much stronger role for the european commission in maintaining the pact, the new commissioner has yet to convince me that the pact is in good hands with him. and i am glad that the council has at least shown itself to be responsible under the dutch presidency.
the commission proposal, mr president, is a bit of a shock to us: i am not saying that the commission should be unable to do so. i believe that the core of the pact must be maintained, i.e. a maximum of 3% of gdp for the budget deficit and 60% of gdp for the public debt. if flexibility is to be introduced, it must be based on objective and generally accepted criteria. the proposal fails to meet this requirement. the european commission must have more powerful means of binding the member states to the commitments of the pact. at the moment the council is too much in the way; this leads to the politicization of the stability pact. the sanction mechanism in the current stability pact has not proved to be as harsh and neutral as it should be: the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. the current pact does not sufficiently establish the need to work towards building up a surplus in times of economic prosperity. however, this is essential for the success of a stability pact. the creation of such a buffer is essential, for example, in view of ageing. the commission's emphasis on the importance of this in the revised pact is therefore to be welcomed. at the same time, the 3% ceiling pact provides member states with sufficient room in principle to stimulate their economies in times of economic downturn.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that is what is happening here in the debate in this house, of course, but also in the media the debate on women's reproductive rights, which are not granted in all eu countries, has been revived. and that discussion, that's exactly what the organization women on waves is trying to do. any politician who is for or against that organisation and wants to engage in the fundamental debate on abortion is therefore doing justice to the aims of this women's organisation. i can be brief about that myself. i fully support the position of this european parliament as set out in mrs van lancker's report. it said that abortion should not be taken lightly, but that women should have the right to choose a safe, legal abortion. that is what the majority of the european parliament has said and that is what women on waves has said. but portugal refused to let the ship into port, and that's bizarre. women on waves does not carry out illegal activities in portuguese waters, but the organisation does provide information on a subject that is not of interest to the government. if we start with that, of course, we'll never know the end. the next time greenpeace says something the government does not like, they will not be allowed to enter the ports, and we cannot accept that. the portuguese government has argued that the ship is a danger to public health in the borndief. does the european commission agree? mr president, this is the third time the boat has sailed. first, as you know, the boat is already sailing to ireland and poland. the boat is a dutch boat, according to the schedule. that is not correct, although he is sailing under the dutch flag. the boat is an initiative of a few dutch women and i find it surprising in that sense that it is on the agenda here. we're not going to talk about greenpeace boats and other organizations. i think it is important and i welcome a debate on ethical issues, mr president, such as abortion and women's rights, but here we are dealing specifically with this boat and the action on this boat. i find that action unfortunate. in portugal, they want to change the law, but in a democratic culture this is not the way to encourage another country to do so. a similar foreign intervention in the netherlands, for example, in the area of drugs or euthanasia, could also be met with a similar wave of protests in the netherlands. we need to debate each other in a different way, not like this. i also think this action is irresponsible, mr president. the boat was sailing to ireland without a licence, so even the dutch legislation, which is already quite extensive in this area, was not respected. there was a great deal of discussion about the use of the unapproved abortion pill when we left for poland, and now there is discussion about the aftercare that cannot be provided. women on waves has now published online how you can promote abortion yourself with over-the-counter medicines, and has immediately pointed out the huge risks that this entails. a little later, the boat sails away. i don't think this is a good way to promote what you want to promote. countries have different ideas about things like abortion. we must discuss this, mr president, and i welcome that. we are a community of values, too, but not in this way.
i would like to ask the commission to give its opinion on the right of european citizens to information, particularly the right of women to information. this right is also a key element of the lisbon strategy, which is to increase and share knowledge in the interest of women's sexual health and rights. the women on waves boat did not intend to export the achievements of the netherlands in the field of abortion to portugal, but wanted to inform portuguese women and men about their sexual rights in an objective way. what they do with it is up to them. two years ago, through the report by anne van lancker, this parliament adopted a resolution calling on governments to ensure that objective, scientific and clear information and education on sexual and reproductive health is provided. this included the prevention of unwanted pregnancies and the risks of unsafe abortions performed under inadequate conditions. the portuguese government has failed to take this house's resolution seriously by preventing the activities of women on waves which are aimed at providing information. meanwhile, the boat has left portugal. women on waves has announced that it will be back in portugal for the next elections. this could be a good opportunity for the portuguese government to respond to the resolution of this parliament and to show that it does not want to deprive its citizens of the right to information. mrs wallström, can the commission make a strong effort to put forward and implement best practices in the field of information on women's reproductive and sexual rights, for example by organising a conference on this subject in lisbon? i am in full agreement with mr graça moura. i also do not understand why we are debating here in the european parliament a matter between portugal and a dutch-flagged action group. there is a court ruling and the matter is not within the competence of this parliament. that seems to be the end of the matter, but the question is not only legal but also moral. since when does the free movement of persons, goods and services include an attack on the legislation of another member state? because that's what's going on with women on waves here. in the nrc handelsblad last saturday, this organization calls on people to support the fight for free abortion in portugal. that is different from service, and it shows this organization what it is really about. that is something entirely different from giving information on board the borndief. i note that the dutch health inspectorate is conducting an investigation to assess whether the activities of women on waves are legally permitted. progressive activism seeks the limits of the law. abortus provocatus is not a matter of course in the netherlands either. at the time, only a slim majority supported a legal provision to allow abortion provocatus. a large minority still has great difficulty with the abortion legislation from the point of view of the protection of the dignity of life. the netherlands need not be a guiding country in this. i should like to answer the questions put to the commission by my colleagues. they argue that the european commission should take action on the basis of community law. article 28 of community law provides for the free movement of goods and services. article 30 of that community law provides that freedom of movement may not be applied for the purposes of protecting public morality, public policy, public security or public health. portugal therefore has every right to refuse entry to an action ship, even if that ship is there at the invitation of a portuguese organisation. what a privilege it is then that the member states of the european union still have the right to set their own policies! i therefore find it disrespectful that the european legislation should be used to try to justify the progressive. the portuguese government is to be commended for its concern to protect unborn life.
imagine that fanatical, aggressive anti-abortion activists come to the netherlands to protest abortion there with the kind of improper arguments that i have heard from that side of the house and they are stopped at the dutch border by the military police. then this house would be on its back foot. yes, abortion legislation is a national matter, but freedom of expression is a fundamental right of all european citizens throughout europe. every six minutes a woman dies somewhere in the world as a result of an illegal abortion, and in portugal alone there have been a hundred victims in the last two decades. the portuguese government's blocking of information and debate is therefore incomprehensible in this light and, in my view, a disgrace to democracy. our fellow europeans in portugal have the same right to information and debate as everyone else. i therefore count on the european commission to defend the fundamental rights of europe.
in writing. aviation has long been a privileged industry. the tax levied on land transport is not allowed as a result of old international agreements for aviation. this was a means of giving a newly emerging but economically weak form of international transport a chance of survival. now that's all changed. air traffic is growing rapidly, the old airlines are being supplanted by price-fighters, the inhabitants of the ever-growing airports are being sickened by noise pollution, global warming continues and international rail traffic is being destroyed by competition. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's air transport sector is not left behind. in order to limit environmental damage, to pay for the costs of nuisance caused by aviation and to protect other modes of transport, air traffic should be subject to normal taxation. any new decision at world level which leads to the maintenance of the tax ban or which is an obstacle to reducing the burden is therefore unacceptable. it is astonishing that the largest group in the transport committee, both on 1 september and now, is opposing a resolution which seeks to put an end to the american drive to perpetuate privileged aviation.
in writing. in early 2003, a majority of this parliament was against the plan for a pre-emptive war in iraq. the argument for that war, the possession of weapons of mass destruction by the regime of saddam hussein, has now been proven to be false. thanks to the intervention of america and its allies in europe, iraq has been turned into a breeding ground for fundamentalist islamic resistance. the fear of the consequences of the withdrawal of the occupying forces now dominates european politicians. with or without immediate withdrawal, it is becoming urgent that europe detach itself from the american positions in iraq. america is looking for oil, contracts for american companies and replacing the euro with dollars for international contracts. it fails to repair power plants, water treatment plants, schools and hospitals. europe can take up this civil task and contribute to free elections by giving a fair chance to others besides the conservative religious forces and the satellites of america, such as the communists and the kurdish parties in the north. as long as there are european armies, it must be clear that they will do no more than that and will leave as soon as the iraqi people ask for it. i object to a resolution which does not call for this, but i naturally vote for the release of the hostages, although the chances of this succeeding are slim if we do not offer the iraqi people an improvement.
the summit in hanoi will take place on 8 and 9 october, and it is of course good to see it taking place, because the dialogue between asia and europe is of great importance. however, some footnotes should be made at the top. burma has already been mentioned, and i shall come back to that in a moment. first of all, i should like to say that the location of the summit in vietnam is certainly a catalyst for the reforms achieved in that country, but it should be noted that there is still a gap between the dream of a well-functioning and fair trading climate and reality. i have made a point of speaking in this house on many occasions - i did so in october 2001 and again a year ago - for example on the case of mr bin vinh trinh in vietnam. he had invested in his homeland after a long stay abroad, which was taken away from him under false pretenses. his possessions were confiscated and he was imprisoned. this was a dramatic event for him and his family, but above all a negative signal for potential investors in vietnam. i think that the vietnamese government should make a gesture at this point. secondly, human rights are and will continue to be a subject in other countries in the region. thailand can also serve as an example. the netherlands and the thai government have just signed an extradition treaty for the exchange of dutch prisoners who have been detained for long periods in thailand. it is good that this has happened, but in the case of a prisoner, machiel kuijt, with whom i am particularly concerned, it was agreed that he would appear before the court of cassation before 1 july. that has still not happened, and i hope that the thai government will keep its promise. the commission has also dealt with this matter in a positive way. it would be good if the council did the same. finally, burma has been mentioned, no other asian country has such a problem with human rights and democracy. they're not yesterday's problems either. of course, the liberals are proposing a dialogue on principle, but a dialogue in which both parties are taking part seriously, otherwise there is no point in sitting down together. i also believe that the other asian countries, especially the asean countries, which are so keen to see burma take part in these talks, also have a responsibility to ensure that burma finally takes this kind of dialogue seriously.
president, after the series of violent conflicts in the states of the former yugoslavia between 1992 and 2001, everyone hoped for a peaceful coexistence of the survivors. unfortunately, in vojvodina, where civilians have been bombed but no ethnic war has been fought, non-serbs are being harassed again by their neighbours. today we will again express our horror at this and call for peace and reconciliation. but merely putting condemnations and moral indignation on paper is not going to solve this problem. if we limit ourselves to that, i fear we will be able to repeat this kind of just resolution many times over, because it is not understood by the people concerned. we see violence and intimidation by ordinary citizens everywhere where there is a feeling that many of our own people are being excluded by state borders and that many others who were once seen as occupiers have been left behind in their own countries. the opinion of the serbian public is that the serb population of northern and eastern bosnia and northern kosovo are administratively unjustly cut off from the motherland and that the areas that are recognised as serb territory should therefore be made fully serb. anyone who opposes it is seen as an enemy and associated with former turkish and german occupations. political parties which share this feeling of many citizens can count on many votes, because they dare to do what many people want. on the 1856 map hanging on the second floor of this building, anyone can see how ethnically fragmented vojvodina was then between hungarians, croats, germans, slovaks, romanians and serbs. since its incorporation into serbia in 1918, it has steadily become more serbian and other groups have moved away or been expelled. since 1989 the autonomy of the large hungarian-speaking minority has also been severely curtailed. the recent riots fit into that long-term development. without clear and broad agreements on language rights, autonomy and administrative boundaries, future outbreaks of ethnic hatred in and around serbia can be difficult to prevent. so we have to do more than just adopt this resolution, but it is a step in the right direction. mr. speaker, you, as a member of this house, will one day find a large kitchen knife under your front door with the accompanying message: "we will slaughter you". could it be more shocking, more terrifying? this death threat, written in serbian, was sent to béla csorba, vice-president of the hungarian democratic party of vojvodina, on 9 april this year. this outrage against a member of the hungarian minority in the serbian province is by no means an isolated incident. it is not without reason that the motion for a resolution before us lists the concrete manifestations of physical and psychological violence to which serbian citizens of hungarian origin have been exposed for some time. how much hostility is there that the outside world doesn't know about? the atmosphere of fear and intimidation in vojvodina is marked by the fact that several victims of ethnic violence wish to remain anonymous. the extensive documentation available on the present situation of the hungarian minority in vojvodina paints a disturbing picture. if you speak hungarian as a teenager on the street or in a public place, you risk collective beatings by serbian peers. chances are the police will look the other way or quickly dismiss the incident as "not ethnically motivated". equally offensive are the texts of anti-hungarian graffiti. for example, at dawn on july 15, 2004, in the city of novisad, a wall was covered in cow lettering that read: "ok, ok hungarians, a deep mass grave awaits you". shortly before, in early may, numerous buildings in this vojvodina capital were smeared with graffiti arguing that "serbia belongs to serbs, hungarians: disappear!' mr president, in view of the undeniable radicalization of the political climate in vojvodina, i would like to emphasize paragraph 5 of the motion for a resolution. the paragraph calls for a thorough on-the-spot investigation by the ep delegation for south east europe. in advance, i'm going to give this fact-finding mission a few urgent questions. i would also like to ask the council and the commission these questions. what about language education and other cultural facilities for the hungarian minority in vojvodina - think of newspapers, magazines, other media? how much money does the serbian government spend in total on public services for this specific ethnic group? in short, what exactly are the minority rights of the hungarians in vojvodina? the personal commitment this week by the dutch presidency that the council will examine the position of minorities in vojvodina was encouraging. then neither this house nor the commission can be left out. the attitude of the competent serbian authorities is, however, decisive. first and foremost, we call on them to work for human tolerance in vojvodina. mr president, the generalised system of preferences offers trade advantages - as has already been said - to those countries which need them most. we in the european union have set and committed ourselves to the millennium development goals. these are primarily aimed at combating poverty. economic development is an important condition for this and we believe that this is the main objective of the review. it is a pity that we have to do this under time pressure now that india's complaint to the wto has been declared well founded. mr president, as regards the revision, our group believes that first of all - fortunately the commissioner has already said this - the poorest countries should not be left behind. where preferences have been eroded by reductions in the most favoured nation tariff, we therefore ask the commission to consider what more could be done in such cases. secondly, we believe that the graduation system should meet its objective of excluding only those countries or sectors which are sufficiently competitive on the market and no longer require gsp. we also believe that good governance should be included in the new sap+. finally, the revision should also lead to greater use by the countries most affected. what should be the most important instrument is now only used by 52%, the commissioner said 55%. the increase is good, but the result is not good enough. president. - i have two questions to the commission. the purpose of the revision of the gsp is good and clear. the aim is to make the system simpler, more transparent and more objective. we are of course in full agreement with the commission on this point. less clear is how exactly that can be achieved. for example, how does the commission intend to prevent the planned sap+ scheme, which will become a single scheme, from becoming so complex in terms of implementation, compliance and control that little of the proposed simplification and transparency remains in practice? the european union is a community of values, and we want to reflect this in the various regions of the world. we have basic services in the west, such as water, education and health care, and we want them to be available elsewhere. international trade is an important factor. trade receipts can help to increase investment in basic services. it is only in the third world countries, particularly on the african continent, that world trade plays too small a role. the african continent has only a 2% share. we in the european union can and must change that. it is of the utmost importance that we, as the world's largest trading bloc, open our markets to products from the least developed countries and, through positive sanctions, give the poorest countries a chance to develop. the present system of trade preferences has, as has been said, been limited in its practical effects, particularly in the social field. only sri lanka and moldova have scored on that point and even sri lanka - as mr sturdy has just said - has actually made too little use of the potential because of the complexity of the rules and the limited capacity. it is therefore good that we are moving towards a sap+ system which is much simpler and involves many fewer countries, but which also brings substantial advantages in terms of graduation and new products, which also creates real incentives. if we really want to see the basic labour standards in those countries - to name one large sector - respected in practice and given a chance, then we must concentrate really hard. we must prevent lobbying from leading us to a huge patchwork of targets and countries which actually benefit a great many people, but not the poorest countries. commissioner lamy deserves praise for his excellent work on the epa scheme and we must continue along that line here too. if the caribbean and the south pacific have special problems - and they do - let us try to solve them with them in a specific and generous way through the epas. let us play a little harder and make more demands on the basic labour standards of the new emerging powers, china, brazil and india. finally, we must not only have fair trade but also fair competition to protect our own workers. as regards the rules of origin, mr president, i ask the commission to promote benefits for the least developed countries and less stringent rules of origin, and to allow regional and development cumulation. finally, if we want to stimulate the regional economy, we must also be generous on this point. such a provocation, mr president, which is substantial and concentrated, could really help the african continent to take a step forward.

moreover, the council itself can hardly decide on the location of these agencies. sometimes they come to the strangest parts of europe and are temporarily housed elsewhere. that too must be paid for. then the move must be paid for again and everything must be brought forward by the programmes which parliament finds attractive. i think that is a wrong attitude and i think it is therefore a good thing that we should raise this matter properly. it is not possible for one branch of the budgetary authority to take decisions unilaterally which have financial consequences without indicating how they are to be financed in the future. for category 3, i am still a big supporter of one aspect, namely the financing of smes through investment aid, through the european investment fund in luxembourg. i think we should give as many opportunities as possible to small and mediumsized enterprises, to private initiative and especially in the new member states, because in some of them there is still a lack of a good and healthy banking sector. it is therefore good that this year too an amendment has been adopted in the budget at first reading to support this kind of thing. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the products. i think it is inevitable, as was the case last year, to call again on the flexibility reserve.
