buttiglione. as frank and free as you are in your talk about reducing the burden and cutting costs, the dutch presidency is so absent from the statements of the commissioner-designate who is a threat to european standards and values. those standards and values that you yourself so passionately advocate. the dutch presidency is absent, and yesterday morning it was absent in person, just when the citizens seem to be seized by political passion. the dutch presidency, however skilled in its technical skills, is politically divided and thus becomes a blank slate. europe deserves better, more social, stronger and, above all, more democratic. we challenge you, the dutch presidency, to show more profile and political face. the european citizen has a right to that, starting with a clear statement by the council which now asks barroso to meet the demands of the european parliament. or are your words about a strong european parliament really a sham? it's your choice. on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, after this debate parliament can show that it is not a puppet of the council and the commission. if the new european commission is voted out under the dutch presidency, it will be remembered for a long time as the moment when european democracy took a step forward. the commission that will be set up will not have a different political composition, but it is in everyone's interest to remove the most controversial candidates. this applies both to candidates who have a tradition of prioritizing their own business interests and to candidates who want to maintain the disadvantaged position of women and gays. the important news is that the media are reporting that mr prodi and mr kok are finally acknowledging that the main objective of the lisbon agenda of 2000 cannot be achieved. the measures adopted to give greater scope and freedom to large international companies will not lead to the most competitive economy. the failure of lisbon is opening up new opportunities for europe. it 's time for a new objective: europe as the most social economy in the world. that was the original bet in 2000. the mistake was then made of confusing the long-standing desire for a social europe with the search for as many jobs as possible in the commercial sector. that mistake has now led to the proposal for a services directive which puts national legislation and collective agreements in competition with each other, to further privatisations which lead to lower quality at higher costs, and again to a port directive which would further destroy the work of port workers. we're one of the richest places in the world. our problems are the disintegration of society and environmental pollution. these problems will not be solved by ever-increasing economic growth at any cost. not growth, but a better distribution of what we already have needs much more attention. instead of maintaining a crippling stability pact that seriously undermines the role of democratically elected government as a problem solver, we can strive for better public services, livelihood for all and the fair sharing of available labor. we have the means to actively combat poverty, indifference, crime, the destruction of nature and the degradation of the environment. that is the new challenge after the failure of lisbon. (applause from the left)
president, thank you to minister nicolaï for his presentation. i think he has made three very important points on behalf of the council. first of all, the lisbon agenda. i agree with all those who say that after all the nice words, it is time for real action. it is time we really took the lead as a european economy. i think it is good that there has been a certain change of paradigm. not with those big plans that some have for a 'supercommissioner' or something like that, not just from above, but more focused on the countries themselves: where are the good examples, how can we support them? a bottom-up approach, with special attention to the regional dimension, to better link a european policy with an efficient national policy, for example by encouraging top-level technical regions. these success factors must be supported, but this also means that we must better monitor whether national parliaments are actually doing their part of the deal. perhaps his excellency could elaborate on that. secondly, the issue of terrorism. there have been many fine words spoken about that. but how is it possible, mr president, that so soon after that dramatic attack in your own country, spain, when it was said here that the security services really need to work together better - we said that on 11 september 2001 - that momentum seems to be slipping away again, and it is again considered less important? how can we really achieve cooperation in the fight against terrorism before the weather turns bad? the third point, tampere. this is a crucial point if we are to maintain the credibility of a social union. we must act decisively. it is no longer acceptable that we, through various policies, allow human smugglers to smuggle hundreds of thousands of people a year across the internal borders of our union like cattle. i therefore fully agree with the council, mr president, that qualified majority voting and co-decision should be used. we must be determined to continue along this path, because it is time for europe to really solve the problems of the people. i would therefore say to those who may be hoping for a victory against the commission this afternoon: i am aware that many people on the streets will be confused because they do not understand that the union is shooting itself in the foot when there is so much to do.
president, the dutch ambassador for human rights has stated this week, on the eve of the eu-india summit in the hague at the beginning of november, that the violation of the rights of 260 million casteless dalits is an international human rights problem. despite democracy and the rule of law in the world's largest democracy, the practice is different in a number of respects. caste discrimination is illegal in india, but it is practiced. i am in favour of explicitly addressing this issue in our bilateral and multilateral trade talks and in the rightly advocated broad eu-india partnership presented here. i believe that positive support can help to give the partnership a commitment to combat social isolation, child labour, exploitation and unfair trade in a joint way. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the establishment of a european centre for the development of vocational training (ecvet) in the field of vocational training. it has to be done there, but it is precisely this kind of international trade problem, a truly international legal problem of 260 million people, that deserves this joint approach, not only in india but in other countries as well. i hope we have the courage to take up this broader partnership in this way.

fair and to refrain from holding a referendum on the constitutional amendments. this constitutional amendment would in theory allow the current president, mr lukashenko, to remain in power for life. i on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, ukraine is at the heart of the european union's neighbourhood policy because of its location - between the european union and russia - its size and its historical connection with our part of europe. the democratization of ukraine is one of the main challenges for the neighbourhood policy, which aims to strengthen relations with the countries surrounding the european union. the developments in ukraine are of course also an important indicator of the success of this neighbourhood policy. the presidential elections this weekend and the second round a few weeks later are central to these developments. the choice is, in simplified terms, between the present course of a semi-democratic regime on the one hand and the european course of the democratic opposition on the other. the government, president kuchma, with the oligarchs who support him in the background, and russian president putin who began a multi-day visit to ukraine yesterday, are backing their own candidate, prime minister yanukovych. despite some formal reforms, it has a poor record in terms of press freedom, freedom of expression and guaranteeing an open and fair democratic process. in the run-up to the election, numerous incidents have been reported, including most notably, the mysterious illness of the main opposition candidate yushchenko. we are very concerned about that. we are, however, pleased that, as commissioner verheugen has already said, a real electoral campaign is under way in the country, and we hope that these campaigns will continue. nevertheless, it is questionable whether the elections will receive the full approval of the international observers, our own and those of the osce. i support the call by commissioner verheugen that these observers should take their responsibility, which is very, very great, very seriously. the declarations made after such an election often have a major impact on relations between the european union and the country concerned. we therefore call on the ukrainian authorities to ensure equal access to the media for all candidates in the final days before the first round of the presidential elections, to repair what can be repaired and to continue to work for a fair campaign in the second round, because that will of course be the most important, so that the people of ukraine can form their own opinion. ukraine has expressed its desire to cooperate even more closely with the european institutions and to strengthen relations with the european union. the strengthening of ties with the european union can only continue if ukraine shows that it takes its own democracy seriously, because a disruption of the democratic process in these elections could put a heavy strain on relations between the european union and ukraine.
president. - there are many reasons for being pessimistic about the fair and honest conduct of the presidential elections in ukraine. the vote will take place in two rounds. it is therefore of great importance that the so-called interim period from 31 october to 21 november 2004 be closely monitored by the european parliament in this ukrainian election campaign. i therefore expressly call on the european institutions to do so. the european union should also not be concerned with who will emerge victorious from the battlefield, but how. if this has been done unlawfully, i expect the council and the commission to send a clear signal to kiev. the international isolation of ukraine may be detrimental to the interests of the member states of the union, but business as usual should not be allowed to continue. finally, a very pressing question: how does the council view the apparent interference of the russian president in the ukrainian presidential elections? the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval, and the council will be consulted on it.
council. mr president, it is very clear from remarks made by members that the european parliament is following closely developments in ukraine. let me talk specifically about the election process and eu activities concerning the election, because a lot of members made remarks about that. as i said, we follow the elections very closely. the basis for our opinion on the process will be the report of the osce election observation mission. the eu provides about half of the observers for this mission. on a national basis, several member states provide funding to ngos that want to contribute to a free and fair election process. in any case, the eu will not remain quiet once the first round of the elections has been completed. we will issue a declaration clearly expressing our opinion about the election process. some members - mr tannock and mr wiersma - spoke of the possible poisoning of the opposition candidate, viktor yushchenko. we are worried by these reports, but i do not want to speculate about what happened. i am just happy that he is alive and able to continue campaigning, thus maintaining the broad field of candidates from which the ukrainian people can choose.
the commission. when i was asked what would actually change in the european union in the context of the accession of eight central and eastern european countries on 1 may this year, i said: a very important change will be that we will have to take more care of our neighbours ukraine, belarus and russia. i am glad that this afternoon has proved that this prediction has come true, even though i do not agree with everything that has been said here, especially by the polish colleagues. i think it is right, however, that the representatives of our new member states should take this opportunity to draw attention to the particular problems of their region. i only spoke once more because i wanted to counter the impression that we are, so to speak, neutral observers in relation to ukraine. we are not, we are very active in this country because it is so important to us. mrs schroedter, our activities are precisely what you have asked for, namely the promotion of civil society, and there have been some successes. it's not like everything we have to draw here is black. i felt that in this debate we were, on the whole, in complete agreement as to what we wanted and how we were going to get it. the link between political reform in ukraine and european perspective - that's the real question here! does a clear european perspective help the democratic forces in ukraine to gain a better foothold? my answer is a resounding yes. i am firmly convinced that a truly convincing european perspective will help this country. and that is precisely why we have offered ukraine with the action plan a policy which starts with this action plan, because it is a policy of different, successive steps, and the steps will bring us ever closer together. i have always been very clear in my talks with president kuchma about where this is going to lead, and ukraine itself says that it understands that it will one day lead to the european union. i think that the country should not be denied such an ambition. my final comment: i do not entirely agree with the remarks made this afternoon, for example that we should have more understanding of the democratic deficits in ukraine, the country is having a hard time, it is a young democracy that has only been independent for 12 years. with your permission. estonia, latvia and lithuania have also only been independent for 12 years. and we have asked these three countries to meet our political and democratic standards in full, which they have been able to do. although they come from a similarly difficult past as ukraine. i am in favour of our being demanding towards ukraine too, of telling the democratic forces in that country what we expect from them. the comparison with a small child drawn here this afternoon is not accurate in my eyes. a small child is taken by the hand or occasionally slapped when necessary. both are not possible in relation to ukraine, and in both cases the picture is not correct. ukraine is not a child, it is a very confident, important and mature country, and i think we can already ask this country to behave like an adult.

in european policy we have for many years considered the precautionary principle to be an important starting point and we will soon be dealing with a substantial piece of legislation on chemicals in this parliament which is based on it. in environmental policy too we try to prevent new technical developments from harming flora and fauna. as for the new sonars now under discussion, i can be brief. we see cases of marine mammal stranding where hearing loss is found in those animals. these strandings are taking place in areas where ships are sailing equipped with a new type of super-loud sonar. the combination of these data tells us that these sonars appear to be harming marine mammals. i believe that we should apply the precautionary principle here too and therefore temporarily ban the use of these types of sonar in european waters until it is shown that there is another cause or until a definitive ban is imposed if the damage is indeed caused by these sonars. the amendment tabled by the ppe group. i understand that restrictions may be placed on the protected areas, but in order to send the strongest possible signal to the council and the commission, it seems to me wise to maintain the current text of the resolution.
in writing. the dutch members of the ind/dem group voted against the european budget for 2005, as amended by the european parliament. despite our positive appreciation of certain european policies, such as environmental, agricultural and internal market policies, we believe that the budget as amended by parliament is spending far too much money on matters which - according to the principle of subsidiarity - should not be included in the european union's work. the commission's proposals are aimed at improving the quality of life of the citizens of the union, and at improving the quality of life of the citizens of the union. the european parliament has been asked to give its opinion on the draft constitution, and the commission has been asked to give its opinion on the draft constitution. finally, we do not agree with the approach of increasing appropriations as much as possible to the ceilings, instead of pursuing an economical, controlled and responsible use of taxpayers' money.
in writing. india is a large federally organized parliamentary democracy with diverse languages and cultures. as a result, india is seen by some here as a kind of eu for south asia and, moreover, as a buffer against the mistrusted islamic world. some in this parliament therefore want to emphasize alliance and close economic cooperation, aimed at mutual benefit, without paying any further attention to the major unresolved problems of human rights and the threat of war. despite the current laws, indian society is still divided into castes, and some 150 million people are untouchables, considered untouchable by others. in the countryside, in particular, they are condemned to the dirtiest and worst-paid jobs. previous governments, based on hindu nationalism, have no longer made any effort to lift their discrimination. they have also failed to find a solution for kashmir, where the majority of the population is muslim and has felt occupied for more than half a century. the problems of other minorities have not been solved either. i reject the current predominant emphasis from the eu on commercial interests at the expense of peaceful coexistence and human rights. international solidarity with the disadvantaged and their organisations in india will continue to be of great importance in the years to come.
in writing. in belarus, two visions are very sharply opposed. a third of the population sees themselves as a western frontier region of russia, sharing the same language, culture and religion. they consider their territory to be more important than other parts of russia, because it is located on the routes connecting them with the eu countries. they regard the current president lukashenko as a guarantor of stability and security of life in the midst of the turmoil in their neighbouring countries. another third of the population sees belarus as a state with its own language and culture, a buffer between poland and russia, which can join the eu. the supporters of this view are being severely opposed by the current regime, which is obstructing in every possible way organizations, newspapers, demonstrations, minority religious services, education in one's own language and opposition election campaigns. that's why the victims experience their country as a dictatorship. between these two visions there is an intermediate group with no clear opinion that does not oppose. lukashenko wants to remain in power and play an important role alongside putin in a great russia. since it can only do so by suppressing others, it is right that the plans for externally organised radio broadcasting and education in the belarusian language developed from poland and lithuania should be supported by the eu as a whole.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the proliferation of cluster munitions. i would personally prefer to advocate a ban on cluster munitions, but the cluster munitions coalition, which includes a fairly broad group of ngos and which probably realistically assesses the situation on the international scene with regard to negotiations on a protocol, has advocated a temporary moratorium, because that seems to be the most likely outcome. after all, that's what politics is all about, as we've seen these days, the threat of real power and majority. of course, if we succeed - and we have the broad support of this parliament - this will require the commitment of the council, the commission and the member states. we need a new protocol to deal with all the humanitarian problems associated with cluster munitions. these were again raised in a hearing of the committee on development and cooperation. we have seen how much of this ammunition ends up in the poorest countries in the world, from afghanistan to the falklands, and even closer to us in serbia and montenegro. 78 countries still have some 400 million antipersonnel and antivehicle mines in stock, and this shows that there is also a huge stockpile in our 15 member states, of which 10 are still producing. of course, this can only be addressed by international agreements. we need a protocol. this requires tough and intensive negotiations, and it is europe that can speak with one voice. we are all in favour of this. the red cross and the un are on our side. i would actually like to ask my colleagues in the epp to come to us on this point. i can't imagine we don't have some common ground here. maybe it's not easy with buttiglione, but with the cluster bombs, it's got to be.
mr president, today this parliament would again express its indignation at the continued discrimination against women, at torture, censorship, the imprisonment of people who are not punishable by european standards, the death penalty and, in particular, the killing of children in iran. although the aggressive and ultra-conservative iranian government enjoys the support of only a minority of the population, it has little reason to worry about europe's solidarity with the iranian people. the justified condemnations from europe are being overtaken by a very different practice. last week, the french news agency afp reported that the three largest member states of the european union had reached a mutually beneficial agreement with iran. in exchange for a limitation of iranian developments in the field of atomic energy and possibly nuclear weapons, the iranian democratic opposition in exile would be kept on the list of terrorist organizations. we in europe have no problem whatsoever with these exiles. the only one who suffers is the iranian regime. the designation of organisations as terrorist organisations should not be a bargaining chip. why, when the neighbouring countries of iraq and afghanistan have been dealt with extremely harshly, is this regime in iran being treated as if it had eternal life?

i would like to thank you, mr president, in particular for the words of support and solidarity which you have just addressed to the netherlands on behalf of the european parliament. as is well known, on 2 november last year the dutch filmmaker and columnist theo van gogh was brutally murdered by an islamic extremist. in response to this, several attacks have already been carried out in the netherlands on mosques and islamic schools. the european union has not yet strongly and loudly condemned these acts of terror as a matter of concern to all of europe. the reasons for this are the subject of much speculation. the council would not dare to debate islamic extremism at this time if the events were to be considered an exclusive dutch matter. i do not know whether these speculations are correct, but a condemnation of the whole of the eu would be very welcome. we must not avoid a debate on islam in europe, if only to unite all the moderate forces - religious or not - in order to isolate and combat extremism and racism. the events in the netherlands can happen anywhere in europe, and the netherlands and its citizens could use the solidarity and support of the eu.
president, arms supplies from eu member states to third countries must be subject to a common and clear framework, which is binding and verifiable. that is, of course, a prerequisite if we are to have a truly responsible european policy. it is simply a pity that some of the countries, as mr nicolaï has said, are still obstructing such a binding framework. three important criteria for evaluation are the classical human rights policy and, of course, the practice resulting from it in a country, regional tensions in a particular area and also the relationship between what a country spends on poverty reduction on the one hand, and the size of the defence budget on the other. if you look at the request from china and now also from a number of countries within europe to lift that arms embargo, you really have to weigh that against those criteria. the human rights situation is a constant and justifiable concern in the eu. on the one hand, china has signed up to essential conventions, but on the other hand, there is a practice that deviates from them. the treatment of dissidents, ethnic and religious minorities and the widespread use of the death penalty are simply very worrying. the severe restrictions on freedom of expression and on labour rights such as trade union and strike rights also remain a major concern. the other point, regional development, is also obvious. these tensions are present on a geopolitical level. finally, the economic point. china is, of course, a society of enormous change, with enormous economic dynamism, which is also characterized by a hard-core capitalism that is gigantic in nature, and by great economic growth. but there are also big losers. the inequality is that some 200 million people in this country are job-seekers, living below the poverty line, with all the social unrest that goes with it. in this situation, economic change must be accompanied by thorough political reform and social democracy. i therefore wholeheartedly support the line which my group in this parliament has always followed, namely that the arms embargo must not be lifted now. i would also like to stress the need for dialogue with china, a major player both economically and politically. we must try to promote the process of democratization and help solve social problems. we must help china to play its role as a global player effectively and fairly, in the interests of the international legal order.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party president, i would like to take this opportunity to congratulate commissioner patten, who is actually here in extra time, on the role he has played in the balkans in recent years and in particular in the former yugoslavia. despite his gloom over the stabilisation and association agreement with bosnia and herzegovina, i think it is a positive thing that after all these years the european union has achieved its ambition to launch a major operation, and that in the former yugoslavia. after the dayton accords of 1995, nato took responsibility for the stabilization force in bosnia and herzegovina. as the european union's common security and defence policy is taking shape, it is a logical step for the european union to take responsibility for stability in the heart of its own continent. it is therefore a good step that the european union is to dismantle the nato stabilisation force in bosnia and herzegovina at the end of this year. althea is an important test of the european union's defence ambitions. the operation, with an initial force of 7,000 troops, is considerably larger than previous european union operations such as concordia in macedonia and artemis in congo. from an operational point of view, the mission is building on sfor. althea has the same robust mandate which, in our view, also ensures the safety of our military personnel to the best of our ability. the experience gained by nato in bosnia and herzegovina can be used, but the political responsibility will be on the european union, so that the european union can demonstrate that it can play a joint external role on a larger scale than before. my experience is that the general attitude in bosnia is positive towards this mission. everyone i spoke to during a recent visit to bosnia and herzegovina welcomes the european union's increased involvement. at the same time, as has already been mentioned, there are still tensions between the various parts of the country. while the bosnian croat federation is clearly seeking closer ties with europe on the way to eu membership, there is still a reluctance in the republika srpska to cooperate with the hague tribunal. the operation shall contribute to the further stabilisation of the situation in bosnia and herzegovina. althea should contribute to the best possible extent to the building of the state of bosnia-herzegovina, the rule of law, the police and the army and stable democratic structures, so that the country can integrate into european structures. finally, and this should be the ambition of the european union in my view, this country as a whole, the state of bosnia-herzegovina, must be able to stand on its own feet. there is no endpoint for the eu mission at the moment, but it cannot be the intention that a military force simply remains until bosnia is ready to join the european union. there is no realistic timetable for this yet, but accession to the eu is a civilian operation, not a military one, so to speak. only a functioning state that can guarantee its own security can also apply for membership of the european union in a credible manner. the aim is therefore normalisation, and it is therefore necessary that the european council, which has decided on this mission, should also develop as soon as possible a sort of exit strategy which might be linked to the stabilisation and association process. such an exit strategy will also require the eu to further intensify its engagement with developments in bosnia and herzegovina. only if it is worked on from the outset can althea make a substantial contribution to the ultimate objective of sub-stabilising bosnia and herzegovina in the european integration process.
president. - in the light of recent history, it is difficult to say that european involvement in the western balkans has been a resounding success. to stabilize the troubled balkans, as the german publication aptly titled it, assistance from across the atlantic was necessary. after all, we don't have nato for nothing. it is precisely this continuing involvement of nato, including the european mission althea in bosnia-herzegovina, that gives me some comfort. in fact, this whole so-called european operation is part of transatlantic cooperation based on the 'berlin plus' arrangement between the european union and nato. i draw this conclusion, mr president, from the letter from the dutch presidency to the dutch parliament of 9 november. mr president, the althea mission is already calling for european modesty. i am convinced that this is a very serious question about the usefulness and the need for a european security and defence policy. the european union is not a military force, but a political force, and it is not a military force, but a political force.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. the european agency for reconstruction has had a significant positive impact on the image of the european union in the balkans. until 2000, the european union was essentially a bureaucratic monster that either failed to deliver on all its promises or delivered them far too late. after 2000, the european union, through the agency, was indeed able to provide rapid and efficient assistance in often difficult circumstances. never before has it been shown that such a large proportion of the money allocated to relief has actually been spent. again, i give you credit for that. that said, the question remains, and we have discussed this with the commissioner on several occasions as a parliament, which is exactly the idea behind the commission why a particular task is carried out by the bureau or by the various offices of the bureau or by the decentralised delegations, the embassies of the european union. the commission may not say it out loud, but it does occasionally suggest that it is a bit of a nuisance on the part of parliament that we keep insisting on it. the reason for this is that we want to avoid any further ambiguity. i'll give you an example. there is a strong possibility that the european union will be given more tasks in kosovo in the course of next year and in 2006: less un, less unmic and more european union. does this mean a bigger role for the bureau or does it mean a bigger delegation and a bigger embassy? it is important for parliament to know this, because we as a parliament have to make difficult and difficult political and financial decisions. i will not hide from you, mr president, that i too feel a certain melancholy. this debate is the last of many debates we have had with the commissioner. i think that one of the reasons for the commissioner's popularity in this parliament is that he has given the european union's balkan policy a face, a voice, and he has done it in a splendid way. i want to thank him for that too. i wish the balkans a good successor and i wish the commissioner, above all, much pleasure in the governance of oxford university and in writing fine books.
commission. mr president, there were one or two points made during the debate which i should address very briefly. i hope that when these debates are held in future without my attendance they will, if i may say so, have as broad a base as possible in the points of view we hear. i should like to say to mr samuelsen that the commission would be perfectly content to hold a debate on the western balkans in december. mr duff spoke about the trade regulation being done within three months. again – speaking for myself – the commission would be perfectly happy to accommodate that reasonable request. a couple of other members suggested that we should concentrate our support on one place: famugusta. that would not be sensible. we have to spread our assistance more generally than that. i wish to make three other points. first, on the agency based in thessaloniki, i would like without reservation to thank the greek government for its hospitality. it provided the facilities we have used in thessaloniki. it was an imaginative proposal by the last government, which has been continued by the present one. it has given us a splendid headquarters. everybody who has worked in the agency is grateful for that and certainly, on behalf of the commission, i am also very grateful. second, mr lagendijk made the point – although he was too kind to put it this way – that there is a degree of intellectual incoherence, which i accept, between the policy which i have pursued vigorously around the world of deconcentrating our management of assistance and what we have been doing in the western balkans, which is to establish an agency rather than simply deconcentrate to our delegations. i have to say to justify that decision that my main concern – particularly given the position in the western balkans and given the position in kosovo, serbia, montenegro and the former yugoslav republic of macedonia – the position was so difficult it seemed to me that the most important thing was delivery of assistance. since the agency has been so effective it was not right to dismantle it and i thought it sensible to continue to use it as our main instrument for managing assistance. so, i plead guilty to a degree of intellectual incoherence, but the record of the agency has justified it. finally, i do not wish to make my last remarks in this parliament into the beginning of a hot controversy. however, i would just say to the honourable member who said that he wished mr verheugen, my distinguished colleague, had been able to state the advantages of the annan plan more vigorously, that mr verheugen would have loved to do so, but when he tried in cyprus, he was stopped. that is a matter of record and was not the happiest episode in this tale. it would have been better all round if during the course of the referendum campaign he had been able to say in cyprus what he believed the advantages of the annan plan were. it might have been that he would have given a more balanced view than some of the letters sent out by the government. i very much hope that we can have this debate in as calm a way as possible. i hope that all sides of the argument will be heard reasonably. the one thing i would like to say is that i recognise, as mr kasoulides said, that there is a genuine commitment on the part of many honourable members to the betterment of everyone who lives on the island. i have no doubt at all about the honourable member's commitment to that and i hope that we see everybody living a more prosperous, stable and peaceful life on that glorious island in the future.
council. mr president, concerning the issue of climate change there are two main areas in which the eu is playing a key role. the first is the implementation and entry into force of the kyoto protocol. member states and the commission are currently preparing the european emissions trading system, which is due to be launched on 1 january 2005. the second focus of attention is climate policy in the longer term. in december this year, the tenth session of the conference of the parties to the united nations framework convention on climate change is due to take place in buenos aires. one of its tasks will be to assess the results of the convention over the last ten years and future trends. a very important result so far has been the kyoto protocol. but the protocol is only a first step and co2 emissions will have to be reduced even more after 2012. i should like to convey the council's thanks for the efforts parliament has made to persuade russia to commit itself to the kyoto protocol. the european union welcomed the good news that on 5 november president putin had signed a bill confirming ratification of the kyoto protocol by the russian federation. that signature clears the way for the kyoto protocol to come into force early next year. once in force, it will legally oblige countries with binding targets to reduce greenhouse gas emissions to reach these targets by 2012. the kyoto protocol is the main instrument in the global fight against climate change and a good example of the effectiveness of global cooperation. leading by example, the european union has passed legislation. it has already made these provisions legally binding in the european union. the entry into force of the kyoto protocol shows that the eu was right in its early implementation of the obligations then expected. in spring 2005, the european council will adopt a position on the eu's future climate policy. the heads of government will consider mid- to long-term emission reduction strategies and their objectives. the basic aim of long-term eu climate policy is to limit the rise in temperature to a maximum of 2°c above pre-industrial levels. in preparation for that meeting, the european commission is currently producing a cost-benefit analysis taking account of both environmental factors and competitiveness. action on climate change demands the broadest possible worldwide coalition. i believe that will only be achieved through a dialogue between the european union and other countries. the european union presidency, in the framework of the troika, is currently implementing action with key countries all over the world regarding the forthcoming conference of parties to the united nations framework convention on climate change and early discussions on options for a regime for the period beyond 2012. members of the european parliament can also play a significant part in this process by discussing long-term climate policy with parliamentarians from countries outside the european union, for example at the forthcoming tenth conference of parties to the united nations framework convention on climate change.
commission. mr president, by now 189 countries are party to the un framework convention on climate change and i hope they will all turn up for the tenth conference of the parties in buenos aires. this event will also mark the tenth anniversary of the entry into force of the united nations framework convention on climate change. the russian decision to ratify the kyoto protocol means that it will now enter into force, and this will give new impetus to the discussions during the conference of the parties in buenos aires. the commission has four main objectives for cop 10, as it is called. firstly, to take stock of the work done so far. secondly, to explain what the european union is doing to address climate change. thirdly, to advance the discussions on what will happen after 2012. fourthly, to contribute towards finding solutions for the technical issues on the agenda. the first thing is to take stock of what many parties have already done to address climate change. this is also an opportunity, especially for developing countries, to be able to explain that many of them have already taken action and to illustrate the way in which they are the first ones to be affected by climate change. this is important for the political debate in general. the second objective is to explain what the european union is doing through our european climate change programme as well as the emissions trading scheme that will start from january 2005. the system will also be officially presented to all ministers in an event that we are organising in close cooperation with the dutch presidency and the argentine government. the third objective will be to begin looking to the future, at the global regime that we will have after 2012, and to have an exchange of views on the post-2012 framework. again, this is extremely important in order to involve the developing countries and to show that there is a balance in the kind of actions that we are discussing post-2012. the fourth and final objective is to resolve some of the very important technical issues that are still on the cop agenda. in particular, these issues concern topics related to developing countries as well as the balance between adaptation and mitigation. i understand that several round-table discussions will be organised during cop 10. the commission looks forward to working closely with the members of the european parliament who will be part of the delegation in buenos aires. we will ensure full briefing and also rapid transmission of information to the members of the european parliament and we hope that you can engage in a lot of outreaching activities during cop 10. having a strong eu delegation – composed of representatives of all the european institutions at the cop – gives the union an important opportunity to encourage the rest of the world to take more action now in response to the threat of climate change. with five years' experience of working with members of the european parliament, this has been one of our definite strengths, and the fact that we have held joint press conferences, etc. is a sign of strength and good cooperation. i hope that we can continue in a good atmosphere and in close cooperation both at cop 10 and in the future.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, madam commissioner, it would not have been much of a stretch if we had not been able to have this debate with you, but with your successor. i am really glad, to be honest, that we are able to speak to you today about climate policy and the eu's commitment to the climate conference in buenos aires in december. it is a fine on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, it is almost seven years since the famous kyoto protocol was drawn up, and very soon it will finally come into force with the ratification of russia. that is good news, not only for those who have fought for this protocol, like you, mrs wallström, but also for those who experience the effects of climate change on a daily basis. it is certainly worthy of a heartfelt congratulation. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 2052/88 on the common organization of the market in milk and milk products (com (97) 0243 - c4-0179/97). i would therefore call on the council and the commission to show in buenos aires, together with the european parliament, that the eu wants not only a strong economy but also a strong environmental policy. there is still much room for improvement in this protocol, for example adding aircraft emissions. the support base can also be further expanded. your new portfolio can help to achieve this, mrs wallström. the eu can help. more countries joining the protocol means better control of global emissions. 55% of annex 1 emissions are now covered, let's work hard on the remaining 45%.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. commissioner wallström, this is your last debate as environment commissioner. i would like to congratulate you on your excellent work over the past five years, on your great personal commitment and thank you for the good cooperation you have established with our parliament. you have been a fine commissioner for the environment. tonight we are discussing the leftovers of the revision of the packaging directive. the ten new countries need clear objectives. recycling and reuse cannot be proclaimed all at once from the capital or from brussels. it takes time to convince people to set up systems. i therefore think it is best to stick to the dates proposed by the new member states themselves. i have of course also looked at the views of colleagues from the new countries and no other views have emerged, hence my proposal for amendment no 3. i agree with the commission that we must complete this dossier as quickly as possible and that we must try to create political clarity for the new member states as soon as possible. president, commissioner, the packaging directive is in need of a fundamental revision. one of the key points is the continuing tension between maintaining the internal market and protecting the environment. this leads in some cases to uncertainty and lengthy procedures. clearness must be brought about, both for producers and for consumers. the european commission is calling into question a number of reuse schemes in europe under the packaging directive, which is also intended for the environment. commissioner, i am very disappointed and have decided to take up this amendment. in amendment no 4 i propose, on behalf of my group, that the directive should include the possibility for member states to encourage the reuse of packaging material. member states may introduce new packaging reuse schemes or maintain existing ones if they provide an environmental benefit compared to recycling or recovery of packaging material. this amendment, which is a recital, does not say that member states should encourage reuse. it states that member states may promote reuse, provided that - and this is important - this is done in accordance with the treaty and does not disturb the internal market. commissioner, you said that you could accept this amendment and i understand that it does not raise much resistance in the council either. i cannot understand why even the epp group and the european packaging industry are opposed to this. the attitude of the epp and industry is quite astonishing if we take the state of the environment and nature seriously. recently, the follow-up to the club of rome report was published. this new report concludes that the limits of sustainable development are becoming clear. if we do not conserve and carefully manage natural resources such as water and energy, then two worlds collide, the world of nature and the world of human civilization. we have not been sufficiently successful in integrating sustainable development into our production and consumption behaviour. it's obvious we need to make a change. recycling materials and the economical and careful management of natural resources are vital. that is also the background to amendment no 4. a packaging industry which resists the possibility of encouraging reuse within the confines of the internal market is turning its back on the future. european industry must be the most competitive economy in the world by 2010. this requires companies that can assess the social reality. this requires companies that can make sustainability a brand, companies that understand that citizens and consumers care about the future of the planet and are willing to contribute. of course, the industry is right when it says that reuse should not be a cover for protectionism. reuse should not be an excuse to annoy foreign suppliers. reuse is also not always better for the environment and there is no reason to make reuse compulsory. however, recycling has an important function, namely to impress upon people the need to be conservative with nature and with materials. a new step towards the throwaway society is the last thing we are waiting for. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community's energy policy is not being implemented in a way that is compatible with the common market. tomorrow this parliament will also be discussing the kok report on the implementation of the lisbon strategy. wim kok notes that this laudable quest for a sustainable society in which people, the environment and the market are in balance is not being derailed by a lack of political will. however, he does not conclude that we should just throw the lisbon strategy in the trash, and i agree with him. we must make it work and show political will. in all its modesty, this package leaflet will give you an opportunity tomorrow to show your political will. not only can we express our opinion in the resolution on what needs to be done, but we can also express our opinion on a concrete, sustainable addition to the packaging directive. i call on all colleagues to come forward tomorrow. you have a choice: a voice for a europe that strives for a sustainable society and acts on it or a voice for a europe that speaks beautiful words about sustainable development and the lisbon strategy but lacks the strength or the courage to back it up with action. commissioner wallström, communication is your new main task in the european commission. you have said that you intend to continue to spread and promote the message of sustainable development. i wholeheartedly support you in this and wish you every success. i hope that after the vote on the packaging directive tomorrow you will be able to point out to the press that europe is not only talking about sustainability, but is also acting on it.

i would like to begin by talking about the lisbon process. i think that mr kok's report is very welcome and also puts the finger on the sore spot. the remedy for the problems is well known, but action is lacking. and i am beginning to feel a little desperate, seeing that we have said this so many times and that we have still not found the means to get the member states moving. i would like to hear what the eu presidency intends to do about this. secondly, i have heard that a false contrast is being created between competitiveness and solidarity. these are two complementary issues, there is no contradiction. liberalisation - we all agreed on this in lisbon - is necessary and liberalisation does not undermine our european socioeconomic model, but rather strengthens it. now, if we look at the facts, and i say this with emphasis to the left of this house, liberalization, free markets, has brought more products and more services within the reach of more people. that is the reality and i think we should not resort to the old means of protectionism, state aid, which is a bad way of spending taxpayers' money, and monopolies. secondly, mr president, i should like to refer to the programme of the hague conference. that is very welcome and cannot of course be seen in isolation from the events in the netherlands. it is not a dutch problem, it is a european problem. in fact, freedom and a peaceful society are the raison d'être of the european union and we must therefore fight very hard to preserve them. on the one hand, this means that we will be working very hard on security, and in this light i would like to stress once again that i find the reluctance of the member states in recent years to cooperate more under the banner of national sovereignty incomprehensible. human lives, our democracy and social cohesion are at stake. finally, mr president, we must look at the other side of this one-sided approach to repression and terrorism. a one-sided approach is not effective, it is like wiping the faucet open, so we must also fight against hatred and against racism. i would like to end by pointing out the tone of the debate which also contributes greatly to the solution of this problem.

in writing. after 40 years of de facto separation and 30 years of turkish military presence in the north, the opportunity to reunite cyprus as a federal state finally opened up earlier this year. the core of this solution was to guarantee self-government for greek-speakers and turkish-speakers in their own constituencies, following the successful model but with which earlier in belgium the tension between flemish and walloon was resolved. this peaceful solution, proposed by the un secretary-general kofi annan, was unfortunately rejected by the greek-speaking majority in a referendum. a solution which is very different from this will never be able to win the approval of both groups of the population separately. i understand the feelings of the greek cypriots and the greek cypriots who would prefer the turks to be in a minority position in a predominantly greek-speaking country, as they were between 1960 and 1964, but that remains impossible. so for the time being, the divorce remains in place. i welcome the fact that the european commission has sought ways of not punishing the turkish-speaking minority, who did want peace and reconciliation and voted in favour of the peace plan in the referendum, for the fact that their territory could not yet be incorporated into the eu on 1 may. as long as the present situation continues, we shall have to vote on the following short explanation of vote in accordance with rule 163:
in writing. bosnia and herzegovina is treated as an eu protectorate, which dictates how the government is to be organised, how the police and border guards are to function and even what the tax system is to look like. the opportunity to critically assess what nine years of foreign military presence have produced has unfortunately not been used. the fact that the eu will take over responsibility for foreign military forces from nato in december is now being advertised on posters all over the country. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union is able to meet the challenges of the 21st century. this gives the impression that bosnia-herzegovina is being incorporated into the eu by means of occupation. this will undoubtedly provoke negative reactions among the serbian population, who may see this as a means of achieving a final separation from serbia. i am in favour of an eu policy which takes account of the different aspirations of the three peoples of this country and which is limited to contributing to the peaceful settlement of their mutual problems and to living together as good neighbours. today, mr president, we are holding the second debate on the appointment of the new european commission under the chairmanship of mr barroso. the first debate, three weeks ago, was mainly about problems in the appointment of a number of new commissioners and about the lack of cooperation between the european parliament and the incoming president in the composition of the new commission. the eyes of all the european media were then on the european parliament. the question was whether a majority of the european parliament would be prepared to draw the logical conclusion from the work done in the preceding weeks in the hearings. the question was whether a majority of this parliament would have the discipline and courage to weigh the merits of the individual commissioners and the new european commission as a whole in a substantive, fact-based way in its final decision. the final verdict was then: the commission's proposal to the council, which was adopted unanimously, was a clear in writing. as dutch members of the ind/dem group, we were faced with a dilemma in this vote. at the october plenary in strasbourg, the left and liberal groups showed great vehemence against buttiglione's biblical views on marriage and sexuality. views that we identify with. we are critical of the european parliament's attitude. here is a spiritual climate in which there is no room for differing views of principle. we therefore have difficulty in agreeing to a commission without mr buttiglione. too much has happened to be able to assess the capacity of the proposed commission. this vote cannot be separated from the injustice done to mr buttiglione. and that while he was loyal to the treaty. the pain and indignation over the action of the european parliament and an assessment of the capacities of the proposed commission are competing for priority. there is only one vote, and it is the one in which all our feelings and considerations must be expressed. in view of the dilemma outlined, we abstained from the vote. in this way we make it clear that we value freedom of religion and expression as much as we value the administrative capacity of europe.

the worrying situation in the field of religious freedom is an important part of the political dialogue that the european union is conducting with the eritrean authorities on the ground. that was the answer given by the dutch government on may 6 of this year to widely discussed parliamentary questions about religious freedom in eritrea. the motion for a resolution does not do justice to this important communication from the current presidency of the council, and i very much regret that. let's get the main facts straight. one, the eritrean constitution provides for complete freedom of religion. second, however, a government decree of may 2002 provides for the registration of all religious groups. three, but that registration does not apply to four of them, namely the orthodox christians, muslims, catholics and members of the evangelical church of eritrea. four, many small protestant churches have been denied registration for years, while their houses of worship have been officially closed. fifth, according to a recent u.s. government report, there are likely more than 200 members of unregistered religious groups currently in detention. six, eritrean police force members of unregistered churches to sign statements denying their beliefs. mr president, commissioner, there is absolutely no freedom of religion in eritrea. i therefore hope that the european union will continue to make every effort to do so, preferably in cooperation with the american government, which will show equal concern in the event of serious violations of religious freedom in eritrea. this is the opportunity to give shape to transatlantic cooperation in defence of western values, which is very much needed in the european union of 2004.
