mr president, 40 million people are suffering from aids. of these, 70% live in africa. the millennium development goals are a central effort to focus on basic health care in the field. europe spends too little of its development cooperation budget on basic health care, which must be linked to good nutrition, access to clean drinking water and basic education. commissioner michel has said that he will be presenting a new communication setting out europe's priorities in development cooperation. mr commissioner, please ensure that the words you have just spoken are actually reflected in that new communication! the un estimates that 45 million people are at risk of infection. of these, 27 million could be prevented with a targeted campaign. we have a lot of experience with this kind of campaign around the world. but it requires a huge effort and that is where we must concentrate our action. we in europe, as has been said before, can help our member states to say to the pharmaceutical industry: you can make cheap products. we can say to the developing countries: we're going to provide a waiver, so that the trips agreements don't prevent you from producing on the cheap market. finally, mr president, the vatican recently spoke through javier cardinal lozano barragán, president of the pontifical council for health care pastoral care, about immunity, moral deficiency and pathology of the mind as causes of aids which must be combated by correct sexual practices. i think that is a discriminatory and stigmatising remark and commissioner kyprianou was right when he said that we should not stigmatise aids patients in this way. i am glad that the commissioner has thus given the vatican a clear answer.
mr president, on 25 october the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs voted on the report by mr coelho. it was already clear that biometric data would be included in passports in the near future and mr coelho is trying to impose a number of conditions on this, so that the privacy of citizens can be guaranteed in any case. well, i'll give you that. however, even before the commission could vote on it, the council came up with its own proposal, which went much further. the council did not even have the decency to wait for parliament's opinion. the council has been very active in this area since the beginning of the year and has been very active in the field of the internal market. that is a very undemocratic attitude, especially when one realizes that the passport directly concerns every eu citizen. i have no illusions, however. i am sure that the governments represented in the same council will point to europe in the national discussions on this issue once the criticism is in hand. it must be from europe. i would like to make it clear, however, that this is not a european decision. it is a decision that national governments enforce at european level. and they do so by making the best use, or abuse, if you will, of the democratic gap that still exists. i was curious to hear the response of the responsible minister, but there is none. let us hope that his reaction would have been less cynical than the reaction of mr donner, the dutch minister of justice, to critical questions about this course of events in the dutch house of representatives. he said that this was not dishonorable, as he had been told, but simply political.
i would like to add my voice to those of the previous speakers who warmly welcomed commissioner frattini and paid tribute to the rapporteur, mr coelho. the previous speakers have also indicated that the democratic process in this case is rather rattling. the decision to add a second mandatory feature was taken behind closed doors, leaving the ep and the national parliaments out of the equation. the european parliament is then more or less blackmailed by the council into adopting something as quickly as possible, when it is a very fundamental matter. i should also like to say that i am very sorry that no one from the council is here today to answer this. commissioner frattini said that this was a security measure and that we wanted better passports. that is all very well, but let us not forget to mention that it is also a request from the united states. they want us to record one biometric feature. they do not ask for two, and i cannot see why the second feature should suddenly be added, especially if the united states itself is not at all prepared to do the same. i therefore ask you to support our amendment tomorrow to keep the second feature optional. finally, looking at the proposals, i wonder whether there is really an improvement in security or whether there is a false security, because terrorists, as we know, travel simply on their own passports.
the rapporteur. - mr president, children must be able to use the internet and other new media safely without being confronted with illegal and unwanted content. that is the ambitious goal of the safer internet plus programme. the european commission's proposal clearly favours self-regulation. a great deal of responsibility is placed on parents, educators and other end-users. that is an approach which i wholeheartedly support. the programme makes a clear distinction between illegal content and undesirable content. that is a crucial distinction, i think. the emphasis is on the information of parents and educators. i wholeheartedly support that too. the four lines of action of the programme relate primarily to the fight against illegal content, and in particular child pornography. the network of child pornography hotspots in the european union is being extended. the initiative to set up such points of contact must come from the member states themselves, but the european commission can and must encourage and accelerate this process. it is of great importance that each eu member state has its own hotline as soon as possible, so that we can fight against child pornography on the internet throughout the eu. the programme also provides internet users with the means to protect themselves against unwanted content. an example of such a tool is content filtering. in close cooperation with the shadow rapporteurs, i have chosen to focus on research and evaluation of this technology. there are all kinds of content filters on the market for parents who want to use them, but there is often a great lack of information about the performance of this technology. the european union can make a good contribution in this respect. the safer internet plus programme will allow research into the performance of the technology and the criteria by which these filters work. it can also be examined whether the filters are a good means of helping users to protect themselves. because there are a lot of examples of filters that filter out way too much. for example, there are filters that work on the basis of keywords and that make unintended information, such as sex education or breast cancer, inaccessible. of course, that can never be the intention. other filters do not hold up well enough, making them basically worthless. and let's not forget that kids can often easily, without their parents knowing, disable those pesky filters so they can surf freely again. this brings us to the third line of action, which is information. i think that is the most important line of action. many internet users, especially parents, are completely unaware of the risks their children face online. many parents therefore have no idea what their children are doing or experiencing online. to promote a truly safe environment for children, therefore, a major investment in parental education is needed. just as parents try to make their children resilient in the offline world by telling them not to go with strangers, parents could do a better job of guiding their children online. it is necessary for these parents to be aware of the fact that there are risks. i believe that technical security is also a crucial aspect of information. a lot of the unwanted content that internet users face is the result of a lack of security on their own computers. for example, spyware, which redirects your internet browser to a certain pornographic or gambling site, or annoying pop-ups that you can't get rid of. the lack of technical protection of the computer can also unknowingly lead an internet user to disseminate illegal or unwanted content, with all the consequences that this entails. research shows that most european broadband users still do not have sufficient protection for their computers. this is why information campaigns must take this into account. the fourth line of action is the establishment of the safer internet forum. a unique forum for discussion among businesses, internet providers and software developers, but also user organisations such as parents, child protection organisations, consumer clubs, civil rights movements and so on. this forum can be used to work on self-regulation. codes of conduct should be developed to examine how the sector itself can contribute to a safer internet, for example, by means of information for the users themselves, but also by means of good agreements made through self-regulation on transparent and careful notice and take down procedures, something which is very much needed. it is important to realize that the use of the internet will always be associated with certain risks. it will never be possible to completely clean up the internet of unwanted content, and that is not at all desirable. it is the responsibility of parents to guide their children. just as every parent or child learns not to go with strangers when playing outside, so there are rules of conduct for the internet and that is why the education, which is addressed in this programme, is also extremely important. since the programme is due to come into force in january next year, a rapid procedure was necessary. i have been working with the shadow rapporteurs in a very constructive dialogue with both the council and the commission in recent times and this report is the result of our work. i would therefore like to thank the shadow rapporteurs and the interlocutors in the commission and the council. we've taken most of our points by far. the european commission and the council fully support this text. the report was unanimously adopted by the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs. i hope that the same support will be given in the plenary so that the safer internet plus programme can be launched with a vigour on 1 january 2005.
i would like to begin by thanking mr fabra vallés for the way in which he has always been willing to enter into an open dialogue with parliament over the last three years. i would also like to wish mr kallas every success in his new role and i hope that the european court of auditors will issue a positive statement of assurance at the end of this period. most of us have fresh memories of the elections and one of the main criticisms we have always heard - mr kallas pointed this out in his introduction - was that after ten years the european commission still cannot get a positive statement of assurance from the court of auditors. that is the wheat on the mill of the euro-sceptics. we must work hard to get that explanation. the court of auditors should, however, be more clear about what the commission is required to do. what are the criteria? i read year after year that it's getting better, but i never know how much better it's gonna get. what concrete measures should be taken? the european parliament has already made a number of suggestions in the past. for example, the increase in financial corrections. has the court of auditors ever analysed that? we stated in the resolution last year that if it should be found that money is being misused in a particular sector, the commission should suspend the payment of advances. in the commission's reply to the court of auditors' observations, the commission says that this also happens regularly. what exactly has been the effect of that? did it help? and what is the relationship between the european court of auditors and the national audit bodies? is it true that the national audit bodies apply the same criteria for the statement of assurance as the european court of auditors? and is it not possible for a member state to sign a declaration at the end of each financial year that all expenditure is reliable? if that is not the case, the european commission can go to that country and say: you're gonna get a ticket for putting something on our sleeve. these are all concrete measures that could be taken. could the court of auditors analyse this further? i think it is important that we work towards that. yesterday we had a debate on the new financial perspective. most people say, we need to have more money than the 1% that some member states want. but how can that be defended when year after year it turns out that the money is not being spent well? the arguments for more money are less strong. i therefore believe that there is a lot of work to be done for the european court of auditors. i was also surprised to see point 0.6 of the general introduction to the 2003 annual report. it states that the increase in outstanding liabilities has never been so high. at the end of 2003, commitments were equivalent to five years of payments. that's incredibly high. so we could actually go on for five years without making any new commitments. those five years just go on, or am i wrong? so i think there's a tremendous amount of work to be done. it is not possible that in two or three years' time we will still not have a positive certificate of reliability. the commission must set clear criteria, those criteria must be checked by the european court of auditors and the european court of auditors must indicate the extent to which the objectives we have set ourselves have been achieved.
the court of auditors' annual report is a good tool for parliament. that is also a compliment to the outgoing president. however, before i go into the report, i have two political comments. the liberal and socialist groups have made comments on agricultural policy. i will not deny that there are times when something goes wrong with the payment, but it can never be that when the european commission or a national member state does not apply the rules properly, the sector has to bleed for it. implementation can never be a reason to launch a new attack on the european agricultural policy with its high standards of food safety, the environment and animal welfare. i would like to turn to one specific point in the report, namely the animal disease policy in the european union. the court of auditors rightly points out that the abolition of the non-vaccination policy in the early 1990s was based on assumptions. those assumptions have changed a lot. the number of slaughterhouses has decreased dramatically and the transport of livestock has increased explosively. the fao warned the european commission in 1998 of the consequences and the possible spread of animal diseases. i note that the commission has done almost nothing with these warnings. under pressure from the temporary committee on ped, legislation on ped has been partially amended, but still not in accordance with the advice of the fao or the international office for infectious animal diseases (oie). the commission's much more forceful approach would do justice to the court's report on this point. the commission should tackle three issues. first of all, it must ensure that non-vaccination policies are put on a back foot, that vaccination becomes the norm and that it is also established worldwide in accordance with the requirements of the oie. secondly, the implementation of the directive must be coordinated in the member states. thirdly, the whole business of european regulations, including the payment of slaughter premium, must never again be allowed to stand in the way of the implementation of this policy.
rapporteur. - mr president, when i was given the responsibility of rapporteur on the eu-turkey relationship, i intended to write my report in a substantive, fair, balanced and factual manner. in addition to the european commission's study and other reports, i have also drawn on my many visits to turkey. i've been there for the past few months, since the beginning of october, for over two weeks. let me say that i am impressed by the reforms that turkey has undergone. more has happened in the last few years than in the four decades before. the support of parliament for this, but also the continued support of the people for these changes, i say in my report with conviction is impressive and worthy of commendation. at the same time, i have also become convinced that, as the report has indicated, more reforms must be introduced and implemented. the current reforms must also be better implemented to ensure that turkey fully meets the political criteria. i would like to take this opportunity to thank the rapporteur, mr van miert, for his excellent report. human rights first. in the area of legislation, the government has made considerable progress in this area. i am also of the opinion, apart from the european commission and the council of europe and all the human rights organisations, that there is no systematic torture in turkey. the debate about whether or not it is systematic, however, ignores the real question before us. the real issue is that the approximately 100 cases of torture reported by amnesty international, human rights watch and other organizations in police cells are far too many. a hundred too many for a country that really wants to grow into europe. that is why this report pays so much attention to this. there should be an independent inspection service working with ngos, which should be able to enter police stations. it is also necessary to establish very strongly the practice that any reported case of human rights violation in police cells is immediately dealt with in the judicial system. then religious freedom. there are still many problems in this area too, and in particular there has not been much progress on this point. the alevis, more than 30% of the population, are not fully recognised. the smaller religions have problems with property rights, with training their priests, their monks and their clergy, but also with simply recognizing that they too are part of that turkish identity. i think we really need a button on this. or as mr. ali birand, a well-known journalist from cnn turk, put it last week: "let turkey be proud of these smaller religions too, instead of limiting them". the report makes very clear statements about the necessary progress in religious freedom. a third point concerns women's rights. as with human rights, there have been many positive changes in the law on women's rights. only the practice is much less rosy in some respects, with forced marriages and honour killings. turkey needs to invest heavily in combating illiteracy among women and in improving their education. ngos that provide shelter for female victims of such cases should also be strongly supported. the report is also very clear on this point. three other points, mr president. turkey has already made many constitutional changes, but its constitution still dates from the military period. i think it is therefore important that this report says that if turkey really wants to grow much further, we recommend that it adopt a new constitution. then the sensitive point armenia. the report proposes that a bilateral committee of armenian and turkish experts be set up to establish the historical facts. the european union must also be able to take action to help the people of armenia, and to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia, to help the people of the former yugoslavia. last but not least cyprus. i think the report is very clear on the recognition of cyprus, on the withdrawal of troops and on the push for new negotiations to bring about a lasting peace and reunification of the island. the report before you, mr president, is constructive, but not naive. it's realistic, but critical where necessary. the negotiations can be opened without undue delay, according to the report, under three conditions. firstly, that a number of laws, which the commission is also talking about, have actually been changed and introduced. i know turkey is working on it. secondly, that the monitoring by the commission is fully under way. thirdly, and this is very important for us and for myself as rapporteur, it is emphatically stated that when negotiations begin, the first phase of those negotiations will be focused on further refining the political criteria in law, but also in practice. i have done my utmost to achieve cooperation in parliament in which this balance is maintained in a constructive but critical manner. i ask all the parties involved to remain true to these compromises, including in this final debate in plenary. as rapporteur, i say, and this is also stated in the report, that it will now be up to turkey to prove whether it can do what it has to do and whether it can really achieve the level required for membership of the european union by further changes in the field of human rights, religious freedom, women's rights. it's up to turkey now, the ball is in their court. we must, however, ensure that turkey's cooperation with the european union will be very strong in the future. the report is very clear on this point. i think we should all now send a strong signal to turkey to seize this opportunity. the country will have that real opportunity, but we in this house must also send a strong signal about our own relevance as a parliament by looking in the years ahead to see whether turkey is really moving forward and whether it is moving sufficiently forward. the european parliament must encourage turkey to do so. i think that is very important and that is why, mr president, i hope that by the end of this week it will be clear that not only the committee on foreign affairs has been able to support this report in a broad sense, but also this plenary of the european parliament.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, my group also supports the report in its main lines, as amended by the committee on foreign affairs. as the rapporteur has already said, it is a critical but constructive report. that does not mean that we agree with every paragraph - that is why our group has amendments - but we support the report in general terms. i would like to respond to two positions in the debate in european public opinion, but also in the debate in this parliament. firstly, the position of those who are simply opposed to turkish membership. mr poettering has said this and i do not have time to go into all these arguments. what i would like to warn against is the attempt to try to get that position in mr eurlings' report by means of amendments, by anticipating the possible failure of the negotiations in a sometimes weakened form and by referring again to other forms of relations between turkey and the european union. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, let me be very clear. the european parliament is not here to kick in open doors or to reward christian-democratic symbolism. the report is very clear at the moment. the negotiations should be opened as soon as possible and without undue delay. nothing in life is certain, and neither are these negotiations, but the objective must be clear and obvious, namely full membership. that's how it is, and that's how it should stay. colleagues, vote all attempts to get that out away. a second position taken in the debate is that of the critics, who feel that too little has been done and that much more should be done, particularly in the field of human rights. let me be clear about this, too. i was in turkey last week with the president, mr borrell, and mr eurlings. we have spoken to many people in istanbul, ankara and also in dyabakir. we've spoken to human rights activists, lawyers and religious minorities. and they all say that there is indeed still a lot of evil in turkey and according to some we are only halfway there. however, at the end of all the talks they all asked to please start the negotiations, because that is the best guarantee that the reforms that are taking place will continue. it is also the best support for all those who have dared to work for reforms over the last 15 years. the european commission's report, like that of mr eurlings, contains sufficient guarantees to simply stop the negotiations should the human rights situation deteriorate in the future. i am in favour of this, and so is my group. if you are in favour of human rights, if you really believe that torture should be further reduced, if you believe that the kurds should also be able to exercise their rights in practice, then vote in favour of opening negotiations. that doesn't mean it's going to happen tomorrow, that doesn't mean it's going to be easy. i realize that, and so do the turks. but the opportunity is there, and it's not the time to waste it. i hope that the european parliament will be able to vote on wednesday and the european council two days later to take a strategic decision with far-reaching consequences for europe. turkey can show - and mr schulz has already referred to this - that democracy and islam do go together. that is the best antidote to the sceptics who now have the upper hand in europe and say that this will never be possible. turkey can show that it can and europe can show that it can contribute to preventing a clash of civilizations between the west and the islamic world, a clash that many fundamentalists in east and west hope for. europe can show that there is room for multiple cultures on the basis of shared values and that there is room for multiple religions in the europe of the 21st century.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is precisely this laudable attitude that our rapporteur, mr eurlings, is proud of. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's citizens are not left behind. the turkish prime minister and the president of the turkish parliament give the strong impression that their country already meets the political criteria for accession. in short, brussels would question ankara on this crucial point. but nothing could be further from the truth. this is a promising development for the council and the commission if it is decided this week to start negotiations with the complainant mr erdogan. the rapporteur's findings suggest that paragraph 4 of his report could be deleted. it is a statement of appreciation for the continued strong motivation and political will of the turkish government and parliament to move towards the european union. i have also been very frank with the rapporteur, mr president, about the fundamental lack of religious freedom in turkey. i am really at a loss for words about the downright petty attitude of the turkish authorities towards the various tiny christian minorities in their territory. for example, see the frankfurter allgemeine of the past few weeks, but also the neue zürcher zeitung of friday. i therefore very much appreciate the critical attitude of mr eurlings in this basic violation of human rights. still, i think he's overdoing it by fighting the symptoms. i therefore urge him once again to support my amendment calling on the council and the commission to demand from the turkish authorities that, in accordance with the political criteria of copenhagen, the christian churches be granted legal personality immediately and that the presidency for religious affairs be abolished without delay. the latter, moreover, does not dismantle a persistent political myth, namely that of the secular turkish state with all its backwardness of non-sunni. mr president, the category of non-sunni includes the substantial minority of alevis. they and the large kurdish community do not wish to consider themselves as minorities but as an integral part of the republic of turkey. more like co-founders. the european institutions must really avoid such explosive turkish political problems, or else they will implode.
i would like to begin by congratulating my compatriot, mr eurlings. he has had to act as rapporteur on this very complicated issue and i think he has done a very good job so far. we in our group have of course also followed closely the debate on turkey and whether or not to open negotiations. we have recently visited turkey as a group to learn about the discussions taking place there. it has reaffirmed our position. we believe that turkey should be able to become a member of the european union in principle, and that is, of course, the first question you have to answer. the integration of turkey into europe would be a crucial and symbolic step. in this respect, the eu does not in principle present itself as a closed association, but opens itself to a country that is oriented towards european standards. we expect a positive impact of turkish membership for the future, not least for developments in what we might call the greater middle east. the progress made in turkey is of course also of great importance to us. the democratic changes will benefit the turkish people in particular, but europe also represents the values they want to share. turkey is changing rapidly, and in recent years it has shown that turkey has a huge capacity for reform in many areas, and we would like to continue to support this process. our group has recently emphasized four points in particular. firstly, the negotiations are about membership of the european union. we reject a formula which points to something else. secondly - and this is something we all have to realise - the negotiations will take a long time. thirdly, because it will take so long and there is so much to be done, the opening of negotiations does not, of course, guarantee a successful outcome in advance. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the accession of turkey to the european union is fully respected. we are positive about the current dynamics in human rights. of course, this one has to go on. if there is a relapse, there is an explicit possibility of suspending the negotiations. this has also happened in previous cases, notably in the case of slovakia. when we were not satisfied with the political situation in that country, the negotiations were suspended for a number of years. there is no discrimination in this respect. all these points are also reflected in mr eurlings' report. so he can count on our support during the course of this week.
president. - thank you to the rapporteur, mr eurlings, for his efforts. much has been said about the political criteria of copenhagen. let me be clear: i am not opposed to turkish membership on principle. i cannot and will not support the view that the opening of negotiations with turkey is necessary to ensure that the protection of human rights and minorities, for example, is given a smooth start. it's the wrong order as far as i'm concerned. the enlargement process in general is characterized by the setting of conditions and criteria which are forgotten at the moment of supreme need. i do not want to make human rights as such an instrument, as one of my colleagues has said. i would like to stick to the agreements we made. the question is whether turkey meets the political criteria of copenhagen, and the answer is no. i see the opening of negotiations as the starting point for a process such as we are now seeing with romania, namely that of a train that continues and ends in accession, in which negotiating chapters are artificially closed and the criteria set no longer matter. this would sooner or later weaken the union. it would be a good thing for me if this house also respected the agreements made, if this house also finally put an end to the great arbitrariness that characterizes the enlargement process, the double standards.
i too believe that we must start negotiations immediately, with a clear objective, namely full membership. yes, there are still a lot of problems, which have been discussed at length here, but i would urge that the debate be kept pure and not be based on fear, prejudice and hypocrisy. we have spoken a lot about european values here and many people doubt that turkey will ever be able to embrace these values. i would point out to those people that all 25 of the present member states have trampled on those european values at some point in the course of the 20th century. there have been criminal regimes and dictatorships. human rights were trampled on in the most brutal way in the second world war, and yet today we are all members of the european union and full democracies. it is arrogance to think that some peoples are destined to live in oppression, misery and backwardness. the european union was created to guarantee peace, freedom and prosperity after all the horrors of the second world war. if a modern, democratic and free turkey joins, the mission of the european union will have been successful.
i am glad that after so many amendments, mr eurlings' report seems to have come through. i am also glad that prime minister balkenende today confirmed with chancellor schröder that full membership will be negotiated with turkey and nothing else. i am therefore not so pleased that amendments have been tabled in this house suggesting something other than full membership. mr president, these are exciting days for turkey and for europe, and there will be many exciting years ahead. the european union is also committed to the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms, and to the promotion of the rule of law and respect for human rights. turkey must also learn to deal well with ngos such as the red crescent and to see them as useful to the european integration process rather than as a burden. the same ngos are calling on the eu to open negotiations with turkey. many of them believe that this will help the human rights situation in turkey. mr president, many people in europe will soon be celebrating christmas, but many will also be celebrating the feast of the sacrifice. also in turkey. i hope that the council's decision on 17 december will be a cause for celebration for everyone, because both turkey and the european union can benefit from a fair and open process of negotiations. the eurlings report is a first step in the right direction, towards full turkish membership of the european union.
the european parliament is debating today, mr president, the strategic and political objectives of the european commission, bearing in mind the recent confrontation between these two european institutions. in many ways parliament is now reminding president barroso that he has made sometimes far-reaching commitments under pressure from the european parliament. i would like to draw particular attention to one of mr barroso's commitments, namely that he will actively promote and protect fundamental rights, equal opportunities and the fight against discrimination. we believe that the european commission should shape this policy in such a way that the european union does not overstep its ethical boundaries. the fight against discrimination must not lead to a reduction in the scope for biblically based views on marriage and the sanctity of life. secondly, i would ask the new european commission to exercise restraint in the ratification process of the european constitution. this ratification is and remains a matter for the national states. the commission must refrain from a unilateral campaign in favour of the european constitution. of course, the european commission's efforts to increase turnout in national referendums must also benefit those who oppose them. they too must be taken seriously in a genuine democracy.

i would like to add my support to the many warm words already spoken by a number of colleagues and i would also like to ask you to support this report. it is an excellent report. we have a very serious problem and we urgently need a solution, but a real, lasting solution and not a stop-gap. what is really needed in the long term is, of course, legal immigration into the european union. it is a missed opportunity in that respect that the council has explicitly excluded legal immigration from qualified majority voting. it seems that member states can more easily agree on how to keep people out of the eu than on how to get them into the eu in a good way. the eu and parliament too have spoken a lot in recent weeks and months about european values and human rights. this is particularly emphasized in the debate on the commission, but also in connection with turkey. our level of civilization is measured by the way we treat our fellow men and the way we put our values into practice. asylum seekers are not criminals, they are fellow human beings and should therefore be welcomed under humane conditions. they deserve our support. i therefore support this report.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, if i could reduce drug use by voting against this report, i would do so immediately. unfortunately, that is not possible. i therefore call on this house to support this report. it is a very good and courageous report. what's the purpose of it? i think we all share that goal. i have also listened to mrs brepoels and we have supported a large part of her amendments. the aim is harm reduction, reducing the harm to the user and the environment. we must look at how best to achieve that. we have to look at what kind of policies are producing results, because that is what it is all about. it's not about ideology, it's about results. we must make policies based on facts, on research and the exchange of knowledge and experience, not on ideology. it is therefore regrettable that the evaluation of the previous strategy was not available in time. it has just been said that the council will decide in the coming days to adopt this strategy. i would be very sorry if the council did that without the european parliament having a say in it. the report takes a pragmatic, realistic approach and, frankly, i read it very differently from mrs brepoels. i do not think that it says anything about the absolute prohibition of repression. it is of course true - and this also applies to dutch policy, which is the subject of a great deal of misunderstanding - that this repression must be targeted and effective. if we look at the great american war on drugs, i see that it has only paid into the pockets of the drug lords, but has not led to less drug use. prevention and a pragmatic approach must be the key. let me give you a few examples. these are also controversial in the netherlands. everyone has a hard time with it, and it's not a decision to be taken lightly. for example, you're supposed to give needles to drug users who live outside on the street, right? does that mean you approve of their drug use? no, of course not, but if you're giving out needles, at least you know for sure that there are no dirty needles lying around and that no dirty needles are being used. like, at big house parties where young people are taking all kinds of pills, ecstasy and stuff, do you have to test the quality of those pills? do you have to provide information? for example, should you make sure that these young people do not drink alcohol when taking these pills? is that your way of saying that drug use is okay? that's a risk. on the other hand, i see that if you don't, young people die at those parties. as long as they're still using drugs, and they are, i think prevention and information are important. we have tabled two amendments, and i will end with these, on the exchange of information and the study of alternative methods.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, today we are discussing the controversial report by mr catania on the european strategy against drugs. the fight against drugs, not the promotion of drugs, is the main issue. it is obvious that we are, if i read the title of the report, in agreement on that. drugs have no place in a healthy society. if we are to take the fight against drug trafficking seriously in the european union, we will need to apply sanctions. we can no longer tolerate people who make money off addicts and destroy lives. but it is a waste of time if we do not, on the other hand, do everything we can to combat drug abuse. in addition to preventive measures, the possibility of taking punitive action against drug use should be maintained. i have therefore tabled two amendments on behalf of my group to allow the continued application of sanctions for drug offences alongside preventive measures. i urge you to support these amendments. that does not make it a good report. i must say that the report is full of ideology, despite the fact that it is claimed to be the opposite. president, i'm going to circle back. thank you very much for the opportunity to speak.
mr president, drugs which were once used only in the private sphere by the rich were also discovered in the 1960s by rebellious and critical young people. they saw the use of soft drugs as part of their liberation struggle. since then, policy in a number of states has been to isolate, criminalize, punish and eliminate drug users. this repressive approach has not prevented the spread of drug use. the alternative to this approach is not to become indifferent and therefore resign ourselves to the damage drugs, like alcohol and nicotine, do to the health of their users and the harm they cause to others. the conflict with the traders, who make a lot of money off the problems of others, we must not avoid. my group colleague giusto catania, in his report, points out the right way to reduce the problems as much as possible. it is in line with the practice of tolerance, prevention and assistance to users and of distinguishing between the addictive risks of different types of drugs, which has developed in the netherlands, belgium, switzerland and parts of germany. i know that this approach raises emotional objections among those who had the illusion of being able to eradicate drugs by punishing the users, but it has only produced resistance to unfreedom. that is why we need broad support for the alternative now.
we are at an important moment in this chamber and the main topic of discussion these days - not only in politics but also in public opinion - is of course the possible start of negotiations with turkey. as rapporteur, i should like to say - and i have said this on many occasions - that my visits to turkey have given me a very mixed feeling. on the one hand, there is very great progress, impressive progress, but on the other hand, there is still a considerable backlog if we were to put turkey on the same footing as a country which really wants to be a member of the european union. the reforms in the areas of human rights, women's rights and religious freedom must therefore continue. i cannot stress enough that the pressure on that point simply has to be high. the turkish media and turkish journalists themselves say: "stick and carrot, keep the pressure up". the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. in the field of religious minorities, there must be a change of mentality so that people are proud of minority religions instead of trying to suppress them. in the area of, for example, the law on foundations, further legislative changes are needed to make room for minority religions and minority cultures. i would therefore like to stress once again - apart from the whole great debate which has been going on for months, including in this house - that it would be good if an important part of the report now before the final vote were also to be included in the council's conclusions. the point is this. i'll read you a few sentences in english, because i have the english text in front of me.
the dutch presidency is coming to an end. first of all, i would like to make an assessment of the efforts to reduce regulation at european level. the aim was to put an end to the brussels rule-making and to reduce the number of rules in brussels by at least 25%. of course, we are not at that 25% yet, but the tone has been set and that alone means a lot of profit as far as i am concerned. less regulation means more room for entrepreneurship, because it is entrepreneurs who have to pull the european economy out of the rubble. however, i still think it is desirable to have a european commissioner who works full time to abolish unnecessary and outdated rules. a second topic during the dutch presidency was the so-called hague decisions on terrorism and the fight against crime. the agreements reached in the hague show that the european union and, finally, the council, are making the fight against terrorism and crime one of their priorities. at the same time, a balance has been struck between effective approaches and additional legal possibilities on the one hand and the protection of personal data and fundamental freedoms on the other. it is the weak link that we are still following on this point and i think the council has done a fairly good job of it. a subject that has received less attention but for which good work has been done is human rights. i am referring in particular to the protection of human rights defenders. the practical implementation of the eu guidelines on human rights defenders has come a step closer under the dutch presidency. a handbook will be prepared for eu delegations around the world providing for a rapid, flexible and robust eu response when human rights defenders are in danger of death or abuse. i congratulate mr atzo nicolaï for putting the need for good communication on european integration on the agenda. the citizens still see europe as a far-from-my-bed-show and turn their backs on it far too quickly. it is only through frequent communication and constant dialogue with the citizen, not only by the members of this house but also by the members of the commission and the council, that this gap can be bridged. finally, a comment on the debate on standards and values which has opened this presidency. as a liberal i am reluctant to have such a debate in general and to the purely christian character which the christian democrats tend to give in particular. i am also disgusted by the way the left-wing church has relativised the notion of standards and values. it is important that this debate has begun. it is a debate about what europe is, where it should go, where our borders lie and what we do and do not do. in short, the question of where our foundations lie. we have the union not just for the money. i thought the article by prime minister balkenende in the volkskrant of 7 december was excellent. one of its conclusions was that the union is about dynamic values. i believe these dynamic values include: respect, responsibility and trust. the debate has begun, and although the end is still far from being in sight, it is a compliment to the presidency. one last note. the presidency has certainly brought the european union closer to the dutch citizens and even to the dutch parliamentarians. i know this is a crusade of atzo nicolaï and it has proved very successful. i am also pleased about that as a dutch member of the european parliament.
i would like to begin by talking about the financial perspective. i hope that you will take the time at the summit to discuss in depth what kind of europe you are actually looking at before you leave this subject to the ministers. some member states want a budget that is more economical than the current one, which is just over 1%. the council cannot accept a reduction in the agricultural budget and there seems to be no possibility of agreement on cuts in the structural funds. what do you think will be reduced to? the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. a budget born of euroscepticism will only sow more euroscepticism. on terrorism, the draft conclusions of the forthcoming summit state that in our efforts to combat terrorism we must respect human rights and fundamental freedoms. i agree with those terms, but i fear this is nothing more than a charm. see the discussion on storing the traffic data of all telephone and internet users or the biometric data in passports. the motto is: tough, tougher, tougher than ever. it is almost inappropriate to raise critical questions about the effectiveness or legitimacy of counter-terrorism measures. like generals in the military, european counter-terrorists always want the best stuff. at least in the case of defence procurement, politicians still have the discretion to weigh the costs against the benefits. when it comes to counterterrorism measures, hardly anyone dares to make that critical assessment. but i would invite you to do so at this summit, because if we continue in this way, we will be destroying the rule of law and the terrorists will have their way. i would like to conclude by making a small comment on transparency. the dutch presidency has promised to try to bring forward the implementation of the clause in the constitution on the basis of which the member states now meet in public - after all, they all agreed on this when the constitution was signed - or at least to hold more meetings in public. i would like to ask you, mr nicolaï, what you have achieved in this respect. i hope that during the luxembourg presidency the doors of the council of ministers will really be opened. we will then be able to see what is happening there in full transparency and the politicians will be held accountable for the decisions they take, just as we would like to be held accountable today for the decision we take on turkey.

the dutch presidency is almost over. it was not, of course, the easiest period to be able to give europe leadership. it started a little later, after the holidays, then there was the handicap of a parliament that had just been elected and started to organise itself, and then the european commission also came into being with its hoards and bumps. the netherlands has tried to get on with the job under this not-so-lucky weather. that is why i think the netherlands should count its blessings, because despite that short period a great deal has been done. for example, the hague programme on internal security, the security of our citizens. on this point, minister donner, who is so often under attack in the netherlands, has done a very good job with his colleagues. camiel eurlings has already said a number of things about turkey. i won't repeat that. what i do have difficulty with, and which i think needs to be mentioned, is accepting the inclusion of romania and bulgaria as a fact. i think we should let the council, the summit, know that the matter, especially with regard to romania, is not yet settled, because there are still violations of human rights, children's rights and women's rights, and corruption is still rampant. i expect that if this continues, parliament's 'yes' will not be so certain. i would also like to make a similar remark about croatia. it seems that there is much more restraint on that point and i would ask the dutch presidency whether this now leads to something being done on that point too. finally, the lisbon agenda. the ambitions of the lisbon agenda must of course be compared with the resources devoted to it. i think that the 1% club at the top should at least ensure that there is a better look at what is needed for lisbon and how social cohesion with the 10 new member states can best be achieved. ambitions and means must be in harmony.
in writing. - since atatürk's revolution in 1923, turkey has been a typical european country, but in an old-fashioned authoritarian way that we no longer want in europe today. the power of the military is great, there are political prisoners, parties and newspapers are often banned and a 10% electoral threshold even keeps most legal parties out of parliament. the kurds, armenians and assyrians are given the choice between leaving or adapting to the language, culture and religion of the turkish majority. turkey needs to change a lot to be welcomed, not just in legislation but also in daily practice. after more than 40 years of waiting, it is now proposed to open negotiations on full eu membership soon. this is also the wish of the millions of europeans of turkish origin. the question of turkey's membership will not be decided before but during the negotiations. the risk is that the turkish government may feel that after three or five years of negotiations, its country has earned eu membership, while the problems of democracy, human rights and ethnic minorities are still unresolved. this can lead to conflict and eventually to a breakup. the copenhagen criteria should not be replaced by the ankara criteria.
in writing. i believe that negotiations with turkey should begin as soon as turkey meets the political copenhagen criteria, for example, in terms of protecting human rights and women's rights, fighting corruption, punishing torture and preventing child labour. turkey has made impressive progress in these areas, but according to reports from non-governmental organisations, it is not there yet. negotiations are being opened with a view to membership - only if it is concluded that membership would not be feasible will other forms of cooperation be considered.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party president, bulgaria will become a member state with many unresolved problems. long-standing environmental problems, caused by waste from mining and nuclear power, have not been solved. the construction of new roads through protected natural areas and the expansion of the largest airport are causing a lot of damage. poor maintenance, decay and poverty are visible everywhere in bulgaria today. the roma population is still backward and disadvantaged. the european union funds for projects for this group are allocated and spent in a controversial way. in the minds of many bulgarians, the nearby greek and romanian coasts actually belong to bulgaria, as do most of the republic of macedonia and some border areas with serbia. the fact that bulgarian voters have not yet made stable choices is evident from the rapid changes within the two major rival political parties, and especially from the fact that a newcomer almost won the majority in the last general election. shortly after the surprise election of the ex-king as prime minister, the candidate of the communist heirs was elected president. however, one positive consequence of this instability is that the turkish minority, which has been discriminated against in the past, should be better involved in political decision-making. unlike romania, the problems in bulgaria are not greater than those in some of the current eu member states. it is therefore right to allow bulgaria to become a full member of the european union as from 2007. there is a strong historical and linguistic link between bulgaria and the slavic-speaking part of macedonia. in 1878, last-minute intervention from western europe prevented these two from becoming one country. it would be good if the negotiations on the accession of macedonia - for which the committee on foreign affairs has appointed me as rapporteur - were as close as possible to those on croatia, which also emerged from the former yugoslavia, so that we can welcome bulgarians and macedonians together into the european union.
president. - this debate is really a congratulatory message to bulgaria. of course there are still many problems in this country, of course there are still many issues to be resolved, of course there is still much to be done in the field of human rights and human trafficking, but they are doing their best, and we were not always used to that in the past. i therefore believe that bulgaria should be able to join the european union in 2007. they're part of it. i would also like to see a debate on the accession of bulgaria given more attention than a debate on the accession of turkey. bulgaria is a genuine european country, it is also partly on the frontier of our new europe and it deserves all the attention. i see some problems with the accession. of course there are problems in bulgaria itself, but perhaps the biggest problem is in the european union. the financing of this accession has not yet been completed. after 2007, we are talking about the financial perspective until 2013, and i am surprised at the discussion taking place in the council, among other places, which states that in europe everything could be worth 1% of gross national product. if you look at all the prospects, there is no funding for that accession. i would also like to criticise the commission, which has rightly said that it needs a little more money than 1%. however, if we look at the proposals, for example for agriculture, we see that they do not include a budget for agriculture in bulgaria, whereas the development of the countryside in bulgaria will be essential for the development of the country itself. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. i am therefore curious to see how the council intends to finance this accession and how we can ensure in a neat and tidy way that bulgaria can become a genuine european country in 2007. i would like to conclude on one other point. i was struck by the reactions of the united left - on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, ladies and gentlemen, let us be clear: this is not a debate between those who are for and those who are against romania's accession including our political allies.

mr president, let me be clear. i am very much in favour of romania's accession, but everything will be done in due course and as agreed. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure house were to speak out clearly against the premature and artificial termination of the negotiations with romania, if this house were to stop the great arbitrariness in the enlargement process and stick to the agreements reached. the way in which we are now proceeding with romania will sooner or later lead to the erosion of our legal community and to a weakened union.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, i would like to thank commissioner kyprianou very warmly for the open way in which he has raised this subject again. you have just started your task and i think that your attitude offers at least prospects for very good future cooperation between parliament and the commission, also in this area. i'm not saying this for nothing. you have struck the nail right on the head when you say that the biggest problem with animal transport in europe is that it is hardly a priority for national governments. that's where we're stuck, and if we can improve that, that's a great step forward. let me illustrate this with an example. i note that at the moment the austrian government in particular is doing its job well, that it is doing its control well. this means that a large proportion of international transport of slaughter animals is no longer going through austria but via other routes to italy. such situations arise when a number of other member states fail to fulfil their obligations. in this respect, the austrian model should become the european model, with good legislation and good enforcement. it's a way to get rid of all the miserable arguments. the council has reached agreement and has made an adjustment without notifying parliament in advance, which i find very painful and which i also blame on the council. on the other hand, i must add that we would have been further from home without this initiative, because we cannot deny that this agreement is also an improvement on existing legislation. you know how this parliament thinks. i myself was rapporteur for a maximum transport time for slaughter animals. this, it now appears, was not feasible in the council. at the same time, i note that the introduction of the satellite tracking system is at least a fact and that there will be better enforcement of existing rules. i would like to hear from the commission on this point, how it intends to enforce this on the member states. does this require other instruments, including those of this parliament? i would also like to know how the commission intends to support the improvement of training. finally, i would like to point out that farmer-to-farmer transport has been extended. we would have liked to have seen 100 kilometers. it seems that this was not possible in the council, but we are still a step ahead. a couple of other points. first of all, there is still a lot of criticism about the way in which the transport of breeding animals is regulated and the rest periods mentioned in connection with it. the transport of animals must be carried out as quickly as possible and it would be a step forward if we also reduced the number of rest periods on the road for the transport of breeding animals, which is generally carried out under very good conditions. this means that animals can be transported over medium distances. the same applies to the roll-on/roll-off system on ferries. it would be helpful if we also said to the member states bluntly that this is just a rest period. this would be a further step forward in improving the quality of animal transport. as a former rapporteur, i am somewhat disappointed that there is no agreed maximum transport time for slaughter animals. i can see that this whole package is a real step forward. i am very curious to hear what proposals the commissioner will make before 2009 to ensure good cooperation with parliament, but also to force member states to comply with european rules.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, i thank the commissioner for his words and share his disappointment at the council's compromise. there is no improvement in the driving times for transporting animals, no improvement in rest periods and no improvement in the maximum load levels, and this may continue for years to come. i wish the council every success in its attempt to explain this to european public opinion. it is extremely bad, also for the image of the whole sector, and it is unacceptable for the welfare of animals. i therefore support the proposal by my colleagues to limit the maximum driving time to eight hours. only such a decision would show that europe is really serious about sustainable agriculture, in which animal welfare must be one of the priorities. europe is built in small steps, as we all know, and that is also very good, indeed often the best. while the eu is taking too few steps to improve animal welfare, animals in europe are being dragged back and forth for thousands of kilometres in miserable conditions. it will be a pleasant and reassuring realization to them that they are being watched by satellites.
mr president, this is a crossborder problem which we cannot solve by national legislation alone. that is why the european commission and the european parliament have a role to play here. for years parliament has been flooded with petitions from people rightly calling for attention to animal transport abuses. this is especially true of horses that have been discarded in the east and destined for slaughter and consumption in the south. many animals are on long journeys in trucks, with little water and food, polluting, panicking and injuring themselves. the only way to prevent this is to reduce considerably the distance and time over which live cattle can be transported. at the beginning of this year the battle in this parliament was between 8 or 9 hours of continuous transport and between limited cheap protective measures or more far-reaching more expensive measures. the european parliament then made a very moderate choice, but even that was taken too far by the governments of a number of member states. the result is that there is still room for these abuses to continue. the key question is whether we accept this unnecessary suffering of animals because it is expected to reduce the cost of livestock and road transport companies, or whether we regard this suffering as completely contrary to our civilization and responsibility for the welfare of our fellow creatures. i choose civility and responsibility. what the commissioner is now saying is the failure of decision-making in the european union. we don't seem to have the resources to do what's urgently needed. national economic interests override our common civilization and responsibility. i do not doubt the good intentions of commissioner kyprianou when i hear him speak. i support his announcement of new initiatives by the european commission. i also agree with him that we had better start quickly with a result that is far too small than with nothing at all. satellite surveillance is of little comfort to the animal. let us not just rely on new techniques, but build the political will to really help this scourge out of the world. that's the whole point. i hope that we can achieve real results before the end of the term of this parliament and this commission, because what is happening is actually shameful.
in writing. i voted against the papadimoulis report today. if you look at my voting record in the past, it's consistent, too. the community action programme is something which, in my view, cannot stand the test of subsidiarity. each member state has its own programmes to protect citizens and its own coordination, training and assistance programmes. some european cooperation is appropriate, but a separate european centre with its own powers and 24-hour surveillance is not necessary. the financial basis of the proposal is also not in order, which is a second reason for me not to support it.
in writing. the dutch delegation of the socialist group in the european parliament agrees with the moscovici report, emphasizing the strict agreements made on monitoring the progress of the reforms and their implementation, particularly as regards the political criteria: the european union is committed to the protection of human rights, corruption and the independence of the judiciary. the dutch delegation of the socialist group in the european parliament will not hesitate to ask the commission to use the guarantees set out in the accession strategy if romania's progress so warrants. the dutch delegation of the socialist group in the european parliament reserves the right to take a different position in the spring when the final decision on romania's accession is taken, for example if it appears that the new romanian government is not fulfilling the commitments it has made. this could mean that the link between the accession of romania and bulgaria is being loosened.
president, the mugabe regime is continuing its strategy of political equalisation. for example, in the last 18 months, the zimbabwean authorities have forced the closure of two critical newspapers. in addition, mugabe's supporters in the opposition movement for democratic change have made life as hard as possible. the law on non-governmental organisations, adopted in harare just a week ago, shows that the mugabe regime is undoubtedly increasing pressure on independent organisations in view of the upcoming parliamentary elections in march 2005. they are no longer allowed to receive foreign financial support. any zimbabwean civil or human rights organisation with even one foreign employee is now considered foreign. foreign ngos cannot be registered under the new law and will therefore be banned in the near future. the purpose of this new legislation is clear. many zimbabwean civil and human rights organisations rely on foreign donors. the zimbabwean government is clearly trying to silence them. the ngos concerned are reportedly unwilling to accept this attempt at state muzzling. that's an extraordinarily courageous attitude. the council and the commission have a fine task to perform in assisting them in this by their advice and action.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, the zimbabweans are a strong people and zimbabwe is a strong country. after independence, it was also a country with economic opportunities. it is sad to note that at present some 60% of the people there are in economic crisis and often live below the poverty line. of course, that tells you something about how that country has been run and what has gone wrong gradually. the sad thing is that the zanu party and mugabe, especially the immediate circle around mugabe, are the ones who are doing the talking. there are many in the zanu party who are of course very much aware of the fact that we need others, that we need a different economic development to survive. we know that there are many informal contacts and attempts to unravel the case from neighbouring countries. i think that is precisely where the opportunities for us as a european union lie. we must increase the pressure with the support of the african union, nepad, mbeki and all those other contacts, because it is only through the african route and our cohesion with it as the european union that we have a chance of getting something done. the elections in march have undoubtedly already been largely mismanaged and structured. it's going to be very difficult to make them fair and open. there's little point in observing. yet it is in that democratic process and in civil society that the power lies. that force is still strong in zimbabwe, still non-violent and still focused on peace and democratic solutions. i hope that with the smart sanctions, with the path we are taking as the european union, in conjunction with those other countries, we will do our utmost to take that democratic step there. the other way, bloodshed, is a hopeless one. i really hope that we will be able to use all our diplomatic means to increase the pressure so that there are people around mugabe who will drop the dominoes. it must be done there, then the democratic route has a chance of success. i hope that we here on the european side will also make the maximum use of all the resources at our disposal.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, as a legacy of the colony of southern rhodesia, the agricultural land in zimbabwe is largely owned by a small group of farmers of european origin. my group supports the wish of the black majority of the population to give back a large part of the land to that black majority. that desire is sadly being seriously abused by robert mugabe, the man once popular as the leader of the successful liberation struggle against the colonial occupiers and the racist minority government of ian smith. it was only when voters lost confidence in him that the long-promised land reform became a priority for him. the expropriation of large farms is now primarily a means for mugabe to finally reward his old crony of freedom fighters after decades of delay. moreover, it is part of a campaign to eliminate political opponents through suspicion, intimidation and coercion. if europe is isolating this regime, it must not be because of old colonial interests and old colonial arrogance, but because we want to help every country in the world to promote human rights and democracy. that's the whole point.
mr president, since colonial times the third world countries have supplied the rich countries with cheap products from agriculture, mining and small craft industries. they depend on the rich countries of the north for the import of expensive products based on new industrial technology. this makes these countries extremely dependent on imports and exports and results in a trade balance which is very unfavourable to them. this division of the third world, as in the days when european countries exercised their power by military force, still leads to permanent poverty and backwardness. it is therefore quite understandable that the governments in those countries thought that any new industry was to their advantage, particularly large-scale industry in the field of metals and chemicals. in the past, these countries were not attractive for this type of industry, not only because the workers were not yet sufficiently skilled, but mainly because the customers were mainly in richer parts of the world and the transport of a finished product was expensive. the only industry that emerged was either from state-owned enterprises or served only the local market and tourists. the need for stronger industry in those countries was and is being exploited by industrial firms from the rich north, because these firms do want expansion, but as a market the southern countries are still not very important as long as the purchasing power is low. however, investment in the south is attractive to them if wages are low and especially if environmental and safety requirements are below par or very poorly controlled. this makes possible disasters in which many people become terminally ill or lose their lives. this danger threatens not only the workers in these companies but also the people living in the surrounding area. when disasters happen, companies don't want to bear the cost. the gas explosion in bhopal 20 years ago and the way it was dealt with are all connected with this. union carbide, dow chemical and the indian government are not willing to pay enough for the more than 20,000 deaths, the more than 100,000 lifelong injuries and the cleanup of contaminated soil and groundwater. that must be resolved, and such a disaster must never happen again. let us in europe take our responsibility, if necessary pay our share and above all ensure that european companies do not repeat this kind of mistake.
